Bitcoin fell as low as $62k late Saturday, its sixth straight weekly decline. Meanwhile, tokenized stocks crossed $4 billion in onchain value for the first time on Nov 11. Tokenized stocks are now roughly five times their value on January 1.

Tokenized stocks track equity prices, not crypto, so a falling BTC doesn’t directly pull their prices down. What stands out, however, is that crypto users appear to keep buying them through the selloff. Demand for 24/7 trading and onchain access to US equities appears to have found strong product-market fit as they are holding up even as the rest of the crypto market weakens.

In September, rising share prices drove most of the growth in value of tokenized stocks. Since mid-October, new minting has driven the growth in value even as share prices dropped. Over the eleven weeks to Nov 13, minting added $1.12 billion while price moves took away $339 million. Binance’s bStocks accounted for about four fifths of that minting, led by NVIDIA, SpaceX and Circle.

Market value has stalled over the past two weeks. We expect minting to pick back up again with the launch of OKXICE on Nov 23 opening large-cap tokenized stocks to US retail.

Securitize reported $11.5 million in tokenization revenue in Q3, its highest on record and 28% more than Q3 2025. Securitize's average tokenized assets reached a record $5 billion as of Q3 as BlackRock, Ark Invest and Neuberger brought new products onchain.

Tokenization revenue is more variable because it rises and falls with issuance, while asset servicing has settled around $7 million a quarter. Securitize's acquisition of River One could help smooth out those revenue swings and diversify its top line. Lending against tokenized assets adds recurring income on top of issuance. We expect credit to show up as Securitize's third revenue line within a year of the deal closing.

Most of the roughly $4 billion of tokenized stock sits in wrappers that US investors can't buy. Only about 10% is open to American investors, and almost all of that is crypto companies including Securitize, Figure, Exodus and Forward Industries. NVIDIA, SpaceX and the other large names are effectively offshore only.

That could change on Nov 23, when OKXICE opens to US customers who have onboarded through its CeDeFi Wallet. It’s the first venue built for American retail at scale, so the onshore share should grow post-launch. We expect issuer-backed listings to drive it, with 67 companies having opted out.

#4963. Anthropic Tops SpaceX's Initial IPO With $81 Billion Deal: Where the Money Went

Anthropic Tops SpaceX's Initial IPO With $81 Billion Deal: Where the Money Went

Anthropic has priced the largest initial IPO share sale in history at $81.34 billion, topping the $75 billion SpaceX first sold in June. But SpaceX still has the larger completed IPO after an additional share sale.

The Claude maker sold 980 million shares at $83 on Wednesday and is set to begin trading Thursday on Nasdaq under the ticker ANT.

The price beat the original $70-to-$78 range, while the company increased the offering from 825 million shares. Demand was about six times the number available before pricing.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perps on Decrypt.

At $83, Anthropic is valued at roughly $1.5 trillion including stock options and other potential shares, though its value based on outstanding shares is closer to $1.1 trillion.

The headline $81.34 billion isn't all money for Anthropic. About 956.35 million new shares bring $79.38 billion into the company before fees. Another 23.65 million shares, worth $1.96 billion, are being sold by its seven co-founders—roughly $280 million each. Anthropic receives none of that founders' cash, according to its final prospectus.

On top of the public sale, seven technology companies are buying $26 billion of nonvoting stock in a separate private placement. Google and Nvidia are putting in $10 billion each, AMD $2.5 billion, Broadcom, Micron and Microsoft $1 billion each, and TSMC $500 million.

The public offering and private placement total $107.34 billion in gross share sales, including the $1.96 billion going to founders rather than Anthropic.

The $104.8 billion net figure is for both transactions, not for the IPO alone. Anthropic gets $79.38 billion from its newly issued public shares, plus $26 billion from the private placement: $105.38 billion before costs. Subtract roughly $580 million in company-paid underwriting fees and estimated offering expenses, and the prospectus estimates $104.8 billion net.

That said, it could rise to about $117 billion if underwriters buy all the extra shares allowed. Anthropic plans to spend the money on operations, hiring and, above all, computing infrastructure.

Goldman Sachs and Morgan Stanley led a 22-bank underwriting group, with roughly $550 million in underwriting commissions. The company also offered access through brokers including Fidelity, Schwab, Robinhood, and SoFi, but retail investors received less than 20% of the offering.

Amazon remains the largest disclosed shareholder after the transaction with about 14.6% of the company, followed by Google at roughly 13.4%.

Early financial backers also hold enormous paper fortunes. Bloomberg estimates put Lightspeed's stake near $28 billion, Iconiq's above $26 billion and Menlo Ventures' around $22 billion. Ashton Kutcher's Sound Ventures reportedly holds shares worth almost $11 billion. Those figures are estimates of stock value, not cash they received at the IPO.

Investors, however, aren't buying much control. A special share class gives the founders' holding company 50.1% of shareholder voting power, while the Long-Term Benefit Trust can appoint a majority of the board.

So, Anthropic can raise historic sums while keeping key decisions largely away from ordinary shareholders.

Anthropic generated $33.24 billion in revenue during the first nine months of 2026, including $16.96 billion in the third quarter alone—the latest reported quarter. Its $81 billion annualized revenue run rate at the end of October projects recent customer spending over a year; it isn't money already earned.

Roughly 250 organizations now spend at least $10 million annually with Anthropic, and more than 1,900 spend at least $1 million, using trailing 12-month figures. Its net revenue retention reached about 900%.

In plain English, the same eligible customer group spent nine times as much in the latest three-month period as it did a year earlier. That measure excludes fixed-price Claude subscriptions.

But the business still depends on a few major accounts and distribution partners. Its five biggest customers generated about 25% of revenue over the first nine months of 2026, while cloud marketplaces accounted for 44%. In other words, companies like Amazon, Google and Microsoft can invest in Anthropic, provide the machines it runs on and help sell access to Claude.

Even the product changes at extraordinary speed: Claude models released during the six months through September made up more than 80% of third-quarter revenue. At the end of September 2026, Anthropic had about 5,300 employees and was generating more than $6.6 million in trailing annual revenue per employee.

Rapid growth doesn't mean easy profits. Anthropic posted a $567 million operating loss in the third quarter of 2026, after a $974 million operating profit in the second quarter. Over the first nine months, its operating loss totaled $1.26 billion. Those numbers tell a very different story from its much bigger headline net loss.

In the first nine months of 2026, Anthropic posted a $104.04 billion net loss, including $94.3 billion in noncash revaluations of convertible financing and related instruments. Yet it generated $6.57 billion in cash from operating activities during the same period. That is not free cash flow, which also accounts for capital spending, but the distinction matters: a $104 billion accounting loss does not mean $104 billion of cash burned.

The latest balance sheet, dated September 30, 2026, shows $95.6 billion in cash and equivalents plus $36.3 billion in short-term investments—$131.85 billion combined. Convertible notes payable stood at $130.50 billion at their accounting value, with some converting into shares around the IPO.

The final prospectus estimates $232.35 billion in cash and short-term investments on a pro forma, post-transaction basis. That last figure is a modeled balance after the offering and related adjustments, not cash the company held on September 30.

The more consequential number may be what it has promised to spend next. Anthropic disclosed about $900.9 billion in commitments, overwhelmingly for computing and infrastructure, stretching as far as 2045.

It separately estimates another $150 billion to $175 billion in payments under arrangements without fixed dollar amounts. Together, that points to potentially more than $1 trillion—but it is not a bill due today, and some contracts are conditional or can be terminated.

Some $70.7 billion of the stated commitments fall due in the 12 months after September 2026. That explains why even an IPO this large is not necessarily excessive capital for a frontier AI company. Every breakthrough attracts customers, but serving them requires more chips, power, and data centers.

There is a technical catch to the record. SpaceX's June IPO initially sold $75 billion in shares, but the final deal reached $86.25 billion gross after underwriters bought more shares—about $85.675 billion after fees, according to SpaceX's SEC filing.

Anthropic's $81.34 billion beats SpaceX's initial deal, not its final gross total. The extra $26 billion from Anthropic's private placement cannot be counted as public IPO proceeds. Its own option covers another 147 million shares worth about $12.2 billion; if exercised in full, its public IPO would reach $93.54 billion, comfortably above SpaceX's final figure.

Now attention turns to OpenAI. The ChatGPT maker recently raised $11 billion at a reported $1.25 trillion valuation and has indicated it will not list before 2027. Anthropic's debut offers a public-market test of what investors are willing to pay for AI revenue, while exposing the cost of generating it.

The interesting part isn't just that a five-year-old AI lab topped SpaceX's initial IPO. It's that it assembled more than $100 billion in combined public and private financing, started generating cash from operations—and still faces a potential trillion-dollar computing bill.

Should your workout top be loose or tight? While it does come down to personal preference, there are pros and cons to each. #4960. CFTC Chair Selig outlines how Hyperliquid and other onchain venues could enter the US

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Onchain venues looking to offer derivatives to U.S. retail traders would need to register as an exchange or partner with a registered one, Selig said.

Commodity Futures Trading Commission Chairman Michael Selig said crypto onchain platforms looking to offer derivatives to U.S. retail traders can register as an exchange or partner with one that is already registered.

Selig was asked how a decentralized platform like Hyperliquid could enter the U.S. during an interview Thursday at The Hill and The Block's Capitol Crypto Forum. He pointed to Backpack, which earlier this week received three licenses for its U.S. entry, as an example of the first route. Other platforms are pursuing partnerships with registered firms, Selig said.

"You're going to have to come in and partner with an exchange or obtain your own exchange, which doesn't change the ability and the great features of crypto and blockchain," Selig said.

Backpack's three licenses include Futures Commission Merchant (FCM), Designated Contract Market (DCM), and Derivatives Clearing Organization (DCO). "Together, the three licenses provide the full regulatory stack for Backpack's U.S. entry, the first by a crypto-native exchange offering leveraged perpetual futures with global unified liquidity," Backpack said earlier this week.

Selig said platforms can continue using onchain technology, but they would still have to follow the same rules as other exchanges.

Selig also said the CFTC would work with the Justice Department when states sue federally registered platforms in an effort to challenge the agency's jurisdiction. The CFTC and the U.S. government had already sued Utah together in October, and the agency filed separate lawsuits against Arkansas and Louisiana on Nov. 13.

"If it's a legal and lawful derivative, it should be regulated by the market regulator. It's not something that goes to the state," Selig said.

Selig distinguished derivatives exchanges from casinos and state-licensed gambling venues, saying the CFTC has no business regulating the latter. On event contracts, Selig said the CFTC checks whether they are derivatives and meet its rules, including whether they can be easily manipulated. He said the agency's proposed Rule 40.11 lists the public policy factors it would consider when reviewing these contracts.

Selig said just because a single-stock perpetual contract has no expiry date does not make it a security-based swap. He said the structure of these contracts matters and compared their daily funding payments to variation margin payments on other futures contracts.

"It doesn't have an expiration date. And so there's what's called a funding rate, where payments are exchanged on a daily basis," Selig said. "This is very similar to variation margin with other types of futures contracts. But they're structured as futures contracts; they're not structured as a swap or as a security-based swap."

#4957. Ripple’s Alderoty doubts Elizabeth Warren will ‘set crypto policy in this country,’ backs durable agency rules

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Alderoty downplayed Warren’s influence, backing durable agency rules, and urging crypto groups to unify their message to Congress.

Ripple Chief Legal Officer Stuart Alderoty is looking to federal regulators to carry crypto market rules over the finish line, arguing that Elizabeth Warren's expected rise to lead the Senate Banking Committee won't put her in charge of the industry's future.

"I don’t think Elizabeth Warren is going to set crypto policy in this country," Alderoty said at The Hill and The Block’s Capitol Crypto Forum on Thursday. "She'll probably focus on oversight and hearings, which I think will be a short-term distraction."

Alderoty, who also serves as president of the National Cryptocurrency Association, said he expects other lawmakers to take the wheel on crypto policy over Warren, who he described as "overtly anti-crypto."

Despite this, he does not expect a version of the Clarity Act to be reintroduced in the coming Congress, pointing to a packed legislative calendar and the 2028 presidential election as priorities.

Pieces of the proposal could be folded into tax legislation or other bills, he said, but a broader bill would likely struggle to garner the committee support needed to reach the floor.

"I think the emphasis and the focus is going to move to the SEC and the CFTC," Alderoty said, also mentioning collaborations with the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency.

Asked whether action from those agencies would be enough for the industry over the next two years without market structure legislation, Alderoty said: "I think it's enough, and I think it's really important."

He argues that rules created through public comment and data analysis would be difficult for a future administration to reverse because they would need to carry out their own policymaking and justify disrupting the market's reliance on the existing rules, he said.

Alderoty contrasted that with the approach taken by former SEC Chair Gary Gensler, arguing that reliance on enforcement left little to no durable policy when he left.

"The Gensler SEC did not do the hard work of rulemaking," he said. "What they decided to do was fall back to regulation by enforcement."

"This administration is doing the hard work of rulemaking, and that is durable," he added.

Alderoty also pushed crypto companies to improve how they present their priorities to Congress.

He cited a handful of possible reasons for the breakdown in the Clarity Act's passage, including the Senate's decision not to advance the House-passed bill, the schism between stablecoin and market structure legislation and disputes over rewards. Ethics concerns surrounding the Trump family’s crypto ties ultimately "overwhelmed the conversation," he said.

The industry's competing messages also made things murky, he added, with different groups and companies all going after lawmakers with "esoteric issues" on DeFi, stablecoin rewards, and token classifications.

When you consider that lawmakers' understanding of crypto can vary widely, it makes the industry's more technical pitches hard to follow. Alderoty likened the situation to a policymaker telling him about fishermen coming in to discuss fishing laws, tackle and tides that the lawmaker didn't understand.

"We need to do the hard work of seeing what do we agree on, making that clear," Alderoty said.

Despite the setbacks, he urged the industry to continue working across party lines.

"Crypto is a policy issue," Alderoty said. "It's not a political issue."

#4956. What Are Perpetual Futures? How Crypto's Most Popular Derivative Works

What Are Perpetual Futures? How Crypto's Most Popular Derivative Works

Perpetual futures are the engine room of crypto trading. Centralized exchanges processed $85.3 trillion in perps trading volume in 2025 alone, according to CoinGecko's State of Crypto Perpetuals Report 2026, and the U.S. Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) describes perps as the dominant form of crypto derivatives trading.

They aren't only for crypto, either. Exchanges now offer perps on stocks, commodities and currencies, and Decrypt has become the first news publisher to offer perps trading, powered by Solana exchange Phoenix.

This guide explains what futures are, what makes perpetual futures different, how funding rates and liquidations work, and what to know before trading them.

To understand perpetual futures, it helps to start with ordinary futures.

A futures contract is an agreement to buy or sell an asset at a set price on a set date in the future. Futures have been used for centuries: farmers used them to lock in prices for crops before harvest, and today they are traded on everything from oil and wheat to stock indexes and Bitcoin, on regulated exchanges such as CME.

Traders use futures for two main reasons. Hedgers use them to protect against price swings; an airline might buy oil futures to lock in fuel costs, for example. Speculators use them to bet on price movements without owning the underlying asset.

Traditional futures have an expiry date. When a contract expires, it settles, either by delivering the asset or, more often, by paying out the difference in cash. Traders who want to keep their position open must "roll" it into a new contract, which can add costs and complexity.

Perpetual futures, often shortened to "perps" or "perpetual swaps," work like futures, but with one key difference: they never expire. Traders can hold a position for as long as they like, provided they have enough collateral to keep it open.

The idea was proposed by Yale economist Robert Shiller in a 1993 paper as a way to create markets for assets that are hard to trade directly. Perps took off in crypto. Crypto exchange BitMEX, widely credited with bringing perps to crypto, launched a perpetual contract on Bitcoin in 2016. BitMEX itself closed in September 2026, but the product it pioneered has become the most traded instrument in crypto.

A perpetual contract never actually involves owning the asset. Traders deposit collateral, or "margin," and their profit or loss depends entirely on how the price moves.

When opening a perp position, a trader chooses a direction. Going long means betting the price will rise; going short means betting it will fall. Being able to short as easily as going long is one reason perps are popular: it lets traders profit from falling prices, or hedge assets they already own.

Perps are usually traded with leverage, which lets traders control a larger position than their collateral alone would allow. With 10x leverage, for example, $100 of margin controls a $1,000 position. If the asset's price rises 5%, the trader makes $50, a 50% return on their margin. But if it falls 5%, they lose $50, half of their margin.

Exchanges track two prices to manage this. The index price is the asset's price on spot markets, usually averaged across several exchanges. The mark price, based on the index and the perp market itself, is used to calculate profits and losses and to decide when positions must be closed. Using an averaged price makes it harder for a sudden spike on one exchange to trigger liquidations.

Because perps never expire, nothing forces their price to converge with the underlying asset, as traditional futures do at expiry. Instead, perps use a funding rate.

At regular intervals—most commonly every eight hours, though some platforms use shorter or longer schedules—traders on one side of the market pay traders on the other:

Decentralized exchange Hyperliquid, for example, pays funding every hour, at one-eighth of the rate calculated for an eight-hour period, to stay consistent with centralized exchanges.

Funding payments go directly between traders, not to the exchange. For traders holding positions over long periods, these payments can add up, and they can either boost or erode returns.

Funding rates also offer a window into market sentiment. Strongly positive funding suggests traders are crowded into bullish bets, while negative funding shows that bears are paying to stay short.

Rates for the same asset can also differ between exchanges. On November 19, 2026, Bitcoin's funding rate was 0.01% per eight hours on Hyperliquid, compared with 0.0037% on Binance, according to Hyperliquid's funding comparison tool.

Some traders aim to earn funding payments rather than bet on prices. In a "cash and carry" or basis trade, they buy an asset on the spot market and short the same amount through perps, collecting funding while their positions offset each other's price moves.

Leverage is a double-edged sword. Every perp position has a maintenance margin, the minimum collateral needed to keep it open. If losses push a trader's margin below that level, the exchange automatically closes the position, a process called liquidation. The trader typically loses most or all of the margin they put up.

The higher the leverage, the smaller the price move needed to trigger a liquidation. At 50x leverage, a 2% move against a position can wipe it out.

Liquidations can also feed on each other. When prices fall sharply, liquidated longs are forced to sell, pushing prices lower and triggering further liquidations, a so-called liquidation cascade. On October 10, 2025, the crypto market saw its largest liquidation event on record, with more than $19 billion in leveraged positions wiped out in a single day.

To cover losses when positions can't be closed in time, exchanges typically keep an insurance fund. In extreme cases, some use auto-deleveraging, closing profitable positions on the other side of the market to keep the system solvent.

Crypto perps trade on two main types of venue.

Centralized exchanges, such as Binance, Bybit and OKX, run their own order books and hold users' funds. They account for the vast majority of perps trading: Binance alone held about a third of centralized perps volume in early 2026, according to CoinGecko. Many are unavailable to U.S. customers.

Decentralized exchanges let traders use perps through smart contracts, while keeping control of their funds. Some, such as Hyperliquid and Solana-based Phoenix, run order books onchain, matching buyers and sellers much like a traditional exchange. Others, such as GMX and Jupiter, let traders trade against a shared pool of liquidity provided by other users.

Decentralized perps are growing fast. Their trading volume jumped from $1.5 trillion in 2024 to $6.38 trillion in 2025, led by Hyperliquid, CoinGecko found. Decentralized exchanges' share of perps trading volume peaked at 13% in late 2025 before slipping to 10% in April 2026, while their share of open interest, the total value of open positions, reached 13.5%.

In the U.S., perps developed largely on offshore exchanges, outside the reach of the CFTC, but that has started to change. In July 2025, Coinbase introduced CFTC-regulated "perpetual-style" futures for U.S. customers, with five-year expiries and up to 10x leverage. Two months later, CFTC-regulated exchange Bitnomial launched what it called the first U.S. perpetual futures, with 25-year terms and eight-hour funding. In May 2026, the CFTC approved a Bitcoin perpetual futures contract from prediction market Kalshi and set out a framework for listing more, and in June, Kraken rolled out perps to eligible U.S. customers through Bitnomial, which its parent company had acquired.

Some of these contracts, including Kalshi's and Bitnomial's original design, have long but fixed terms of 25 years, so are not strictly perpetual. The regulator says perps can be offered on CFTC-registered exchanges under federal oversight, and has advised U.S. traders to avoid offshore venues.

The shift has been contested. CME Group, the largest U.S. futures exchange, has sued the CFTC, arguing that perps are swaps rather than futures under U.S. law. The CFTC has nonetheless continued to approve more perps platforms, starting with Backpack.

Perps are now spreading beyond crypto. Exchanges have begun listing perps tracking stocks, stock indexes, commodities such as gold and oil, and currencies, giving traders around-the-clock exposure to markets that normally close in the evening and on weekends.

Those products carry the same mechanics, and the same risks, as crypto perps. They track an asset's price without giving traders ownership of the asset itself, so a perp on a company's stock doesn't come with voting rights or dividends.

Perps can be useful tools for hedging and speculation, but they are among the riskiest products in crypto:

Decrypt users can now trade perps directly alongside the news, through the Decrypt Money Account. The service is powered by Phoenix, a decentralized exchange on Solana, and lets users go long or short on crypto, stocks and commodities.

Availability varies by region, and as with any leveraged product, traders should only risk money they can afford to lose.

#4953. Protesters Arrested Outside Nasdaq as Activists Oppose Anthropic IPO Over AI Bubble Risks

Protesters Arrested Outside Nasdaq as Activists Oppose Anthropic IPO Over AI Bubble Risks

At least four protesters have been arrested outside Nasdaq’s Times Square headquarters Thursday as Anthropic celebrated its $81.34 billion initial public offering, according to a press release shared with Decrypt by organizers of the #StopTechOligarchs demonstration.

The Stop Funding Billionaires campaign accused Anthropic and its early investors of benefiting from an AI boom that could leave ordinary retirement savers exposed if share prices tumble. Organizers called on pension funds and other large investors to stay away from the company's stock.

“Teachers, firefighters, civil servants will pay for that loss,” said Jose Gonzalez, an activist with Stop Funding Billionaires and New York Communities for Change, in a statement. He argued that workers have little control over how large institutions invest the savings they depend on for retirement.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

The concern centers on faster rules for adding new companies to major stock indexes. Under Nasdaq’s fast-entry policy, an eligible IPO large enough to rank among the Nasdaq-100’s top 40 companies can enter the index after 15 trading days, allowing index-tracking funds to buy the shares sooner than under older rules.

That does not mean all pension funds or 401(k) plans must purchase Anthropic shares, let alone buy them during the IPO. Funds tracking a benchmark that admits Anthropic would normally adjust their holdings, while other managers can make different choices.

Protesters also raised concerns about AI-driven job displacement, the water and energy used by data centers, and AI applications in surveillance and military targeting. Their claims about specific military and law-enforcement harms were allegations made by the campaign, not findings established by the release.

“I’m proud to join activists and community members out here today to stand against Anthropic and the tech billionaires who put profit over people,” said New York state senator-elect Aber Kawas in the organizers’ statement. Kawas also criticized AI’s military uses and the prospect of companies replacing workers with automated systems.

One protester identified by organizers as among those arrested called for a freeze on what they described as reckless AI development. “Don’t bail out Big Tech when their bubble inevitably pops,” the protester said.

Anthropic priced 980 million shares at $83 apiece, raising $81.34 billion in the initial offering. That exceeded SpaceX’s original $75 billion IPO sale, although SpaceX’s final offering was larger after underwriters bought additional shares.

The IPO also followed a sharp change in market expectations. Earlier this month, an AI-stock sell-off had led some investors to question whether Anthropic could command a valuation above $1 trillion, before demand pushed its IPO price higher.

The protesters had called for Anthropic to halt its IPO, even as the offering went ahead. The campaign is also urging pension funds, university endowments and other institutional investors not to buy shares in companies it says undermine workers’ rights.

#4951. A Brooklyn Townhouse Celebrates a Forgotten Design Pioneer

In the early 1920s, the Pittsburgh-based manufacturer Armstrong Cork Company (now Armstrong World Industries) made the prescient move of tapping a young arts educator named Hazel Dell Brown to act as its in-house decorator. Hers would be an under-recognized yet wildly influential career. Over the course of more than three decades, she designed countless rooms for advertisements, booklets, catalogs, and more that showcased the brand’s products, chief among them linoleum flooring. With their bold colors, fearless mix of patterns, and daring pastiche, her interiors shaped an entire generation of homes in the Rust Belt and beyond.

For two contemporary artists setting out to renovate a Brooklyn town house for their young family, Brown’s little-known work recently served not only as inspiration but also as a litmus test. When interviewing architects to take on the project, the couple first presented examples of vintage Armstrong schemes. “A lot of people would take one look and then show me something different,” reflects the wife, who learned about Brown while working on a project in Pittsburgh. Not so in the case of Tal Schori and Rustam Mehta of GRT Architects. The AD100 duo instantly responded to Brown’s surface-heavy approach and inward-facing embrace of small spaces, or as Schori puts it, “rooms within rooms.” As for their interior designer, Penelope August, a friend of the wife’s since preschool—she implicitly passed with flying colors.

A circa 1980 Gio Ponti chandelier crowns the living room, which is furnished with a vintage Vladimir Kagan sectional sofa and a Jens Risom chair upholstered in a custom fabric based on a Macgregor Harp painting; artwork by Rose Marcus and wallpaper by Antoinette Poisson.

The kitchen features art-glass cabinet fronts by Paul Wissmach Glass Company and a custom terrazzo floor inspired by ancient roman mosaics of unswept debris.

Hembus wallpaper in a custom blue distinguishes the primary bedroom; painting (above bed) by Michael Wang.

The project evolved into a dynamic collaboration as the couple and their team gutted the town house, set in Carroll Gardens and previously renovated in the 1990s. Together they took care to preserve historic details like the original doors and staircase, with its original mahogany newel posts and handrails. Wherever appropriate, they embraced imperfections, filling in the gaps between old floorboards with contrasting tinted epoxy and leaving levels ever so askew. “This house shows signs of age and that’s not a bad thing,” says Schori. Adds Mehta, “As artists, they understood what is and isn’t precious so that the home does not feel overly restored.”

The backyard was updated by Brook Landscape, with a shed and swing set by GRT Architects.

In the son’s room, shaker-style cabinetry reveals a reading nook.

A refurbished tub from The Demolition Depot anchors the primary bath, which is lined in tiles by Balineum (on walls) and Artistic Tile (on floor).

Brown was nothing if not a gleeful problem solver, presenting flexible solutions to the needs of everyday life, often through inventive secondary spaces. (“Your rumpus room can be anything you like” proclaimed one Armstong campaign.) True to that strategy, GRT devised a floor plan full of versatile surprises. Adjoining the dining room is a cave-like lounge for watching television or just relaxing, with a pass-through to the kitchen for the easy arrival of snacks. Opposite the cookspace—more on that showstopper later—is a cozy breakfast area, with a load-bearing skylight that offers glimpses of the roof terrace (and sometimes curious children) above. And on the second level, a secret passage leads from the primary suite into the library, one column of shelving concealing a door. “All these appendages to the main spaces allow multiple things to happen at once,” says Schori.

In the daughter’s room, Shaker-style trim is painted in Farrow & Ball’s Blue Ground.

The kids’ bath mixes terrazzo walls with custom floor tiles by Haand.

Oak millwork incorporates vintage Piero Fornasetti panels in the dining room, where Guillerme et Chambron chairs surround an antique farm table; painting by Jo Nigoghossian, and custom fireplace fabricated by Daniel Armitano.

Surfaces and furnishings tend toward the eclectic and one-of-a-kind, with a mix of wallpapers and fabrics, some deadstock, setting the stage for vintage treasures. In the living room, a Vladimir Kagan sectional sofa found at auction doubles as a jungle gym of sorts. The primary bath is anchored by a salvaged earthenware tub, since glazed a light blue to complement the retro checkerboard tiles and scalloped vanity. And the dining room’s custom millwork incorporates a series of midcentury Fornasetti panels. Doorknobs, lighting, motifs, art by their circle of creative friends—it’s all a mishmash. “We liked things independently of how they worked together,” says the wife. Adds August: “It was so fun to stitch all these elements into an old house—I love a space where you come upon something unexpected.”

A detail of the kitchen’s terrazzo floor shows stone and metal shapes (some abstract, some legible) inspired by detritus.

A custom sofa frames the lounge off the dining room, which has a pass-through to the kitchen; carpet by Stark.

In the living room, a Gretta Johnson painting hangs above the fireplace, which is flanked by artworks by Cletus Johnson (left) and Ted Mineo (near right) and Miles Huston (Far right).

The husband’s office/music room is cocooned in cork; painting (at left, bottom) by MacGregor Harp.

The stairwell’s painted checkerboard floor hints at classic Americana.

Floor to floor, the mood shifts. The son’s and daughter’s rooms each riff on classic Shaker woodwork, with rails and built-in cabinetry that respond to the exposed ceiling joists. Enlivened by a splatter-painted floor and celestial configuration of ceiling lights, the plywood-lined attic operates as a kids’ club, craft room, and family theater in one. (How’s that for a rumpus room, Hazel?) Thanks to some clever excavation and engineering on the part of GRT, the combined garden level and cellar is now a double-height studio for the wife, complete with a gantry crane for hoisting heavy sculptures. Outside, meanwhile, Brook Landscape transformed the backyard into a romantic swath of greenery.

Vintage globe lights from a one-time optometry office add a surreal touch to the powder room.

Loose arrangements of greenery frame the steps to the backyard.

GRT Architects combined the garden level and cellar into a double-height art studio with a register of exposed brick.

A vintage Vilhelm Lauritzen ceiling light punctuates the primary bedroom; painting by Margaux Ogden.

As for that kitchen, here the team took inspiration not only from Brown but from two additional touchstones. Cabinet fronts nod to art glass at the Upper East Side’s late great Glaser’s Bake Shop, the salvaged interiors of which August and the wife chanced upon at The Demolition Depot. The floor, meanwhile, riffs on ancient Roman mosaics of unswept detritus, an example of which can be found at the Vatican Museums. “We wanted a kitchen that wouldn’t look bad if it got messy,” says the wife. Working closely with GRT and August, she and her husband evolved that historic precedent into a contemporary series of shapes, some abstract and some legible (a knife, a carrot, a wine glass). Rendered in scraps of stone or metal, their forms now appear to float in poured terrazzo.

“The way artists think about objects and architecture is as a constant work in progress,” says Mehta. “These are visual people with an incredible mental library of references.” Would Brown approve? Without a doubt. As the designer once wrote: “Whatever you do, use your own ideas. Don’t be afraid of them.”

#4950. Jenna Dewan Faced Criticism, a Partner Suspension, and a Shock Dancing with the Stars Exit. She’s Still Proud.

In an exclusive interview with Women’s Health, the actor reflects on her time in the ballroom.

Dancing with the Stars fans let out a collective gasp Tuesday night. Jenna Dewan and her partner, Val Chmerkovskiy, had earned one of the night’s top scores: 28 out of 30. They also won their dance-off against Tatyana Ali and Jan Ravnik, tying for second on the leaderboard with Amber Glenn and Pasha Pashkov. Then host Alfonso Ribeiro called Dewan’s name, eliminating her from competition.

“I had a great time, and I loved getting to share that with the world,” she said, moments after being eliminated. “All love.”

Not even 48 hours later, Jenna sat down with Women’s Health to share her story.

Jenna Dewan: Overall, it’s really been the saying, “Two things can be true at once.” There's so many different emotions, and it's kind of hard to even put into words because obviously doing a show like this is such an enormous undertaking, and it's physically, emotionally, mentally, spiritually intense. And so, when that's just finished, it's like a light switch goes off, and then you almost don't even know where you are.

I feel all kinds of things. I feel mostly proud. I feel really proud of myself, and I feel proud of the last dance we gave on the ballroom floor, and I feel obviously sad that it's over, that I don't get to give any more dances out there and create any more special pieces. So I think it's really both. I feel really proud of us, and I feel fulfilled, and I feel filled up, and I also feel sad that the journey of creating dances is over.

JD: When you are going through the show, it feels like any week could be you. The show is not necessarily a show about dance. It's not necessarily a competition about the best dancer. It's not about the best dance you give. It's so many other elements that go into it, which is great on a reality show front-end. It makes people tune in for all different kinds of reasons, but in doing that, you never know how it's going to go.

Once it started whittling down, it was the two of us. You kind of come to a place of like, “Okay, this could be our moment.” And I had a feeling—it was a tough week as far as some of the online noise, I would say, that goes with doing the show. So it definitely wasn't out of my mind that it could be a possibility.

Even before Jenna stepped onto the DWTS dance floor, viewers debated whether she belonged in the competition. How much prior dance experience a celebrity contestant can have—while still being considered a fair competitor—has long been a point of debate among viewers.

Jenna started her career as a dancer, where she toured alongside legendary performers, including Janet Jackson (she appeared in two music videos of the singer’s). In 2006, she landed her breakout role in Step Up, in which she played a classically trained ballerina. She was the host for television shows World of Dance and Flirty Dancing as well as a judge on Come Dance with Me. Many fans of DWTS cited Jenna’s impressive resume as an unfair advantage. “How is Jenna Dewan part of this? She was a professional dancer. Hello! So unfair already,” one X user wrote on the platform after the official season 35 cast announcement.

It should be noted, however, Jenna is not the first celebrity to join the show with previous dance experience—and it’s safe to say she’ll be far from the last. Stars like Heather Morris, Jordan Fisher, and Charli D’Amelio have all undergone criticism for joining the show in the past, despite having a substantial leg up among fellow contestants. Even Alfonso, the show’s current host and season 19 winner, had prior dance experience.

JD: I will say, through the history of watching the show and what I know about the show, is I've seen there be an issue with people with dance backgrounds, and that obviously there's a lot of opinions on whether we should even have people compete on the show that have dance backgrounds.

I definitely asked quite a few times, “Are you sure you guys want me on the show?” But the incredible people who do the show were really confident in expressing to me that this show had changed a lot, and that it’s not Dancing with the Stars Who Have Never Danced. They wanted a very dynamic cast with all different levels and strengths they bring to the ballroom. I knew it would be an uphill battle for me for sure, just from the patterns of the show and the loyalists of the show and how they feel. But ultimately, the gift of doing something just for myself was really intoxicating, and it won over any sort of doubts or hesitations I had.

JD: The hardest part of the show, I would say, is it really teaches you that joy and difficulty can exist together. There were so many highs of this journey, and then so many moments of frustration and negative noise and criticism that is honestly sad, because it tries to suck out the joy from the experience.

Even for myself with a dance background, it is vulnerable to put yourself out there in the ballroom every Tuesday and be like, “Hey guys, watch my dance and tell me what you think.” And then you have the whole other element of this social, online, noisy world that is almost like they're looking to tear people down, and I felt that that was an unfortunate shade to this show that didn't need to happen.

JD: Part of the journey of the show is interesting because for the first good couple weeks, you rarely see any of the other contestants, because you are really in your own rehearsal space, and you're just trying to get the dances down as fast as you can with your partner. I think it was rare seeing people in the hallways and connecting with some of the contestants. But yeah, we definitely would talk.

I think at one point Connor [Wood] made me laugh so hard because he said he had seen some of the online social negativity, and he said, “Everyone needs to go back to journaling.” I laughed so hard, because he is exactly right. At some point, we all thought it was okay to just get online and to be mean for no reason. We are real human beings that see this and feel this, and I think that that gets forgotten.

When we did the team dance, that was when we really were able to start bonding as the contestants, because we were finally together working towards one dance and in a room together. I absolutely love them, and we were all definitely talking about this and sharing our experiences with this, and how most of us at that point, I think, had taken it off our phone or turned off social media.

JD: Naturally, because of my dance background, there were really high expectations that I put on myself, and that the world was putting on me. I didn't even realize it was happening, but it was creating this feeling that I needed to let go of perfection and kind of just get comfortable being seen and just being there. I didn't need to prove anything.

I think I just realized how much of my life I spent wanting to get things right. And this experience really challenged me in that way, because what I realized is, you just can't grow if you're constantly worried about being perfect. I had to learn to be a little uncomfortable, to be critiqued again, to make some mistakes, to be vulnerable, and just let people see me in the middle of figuring something out. There's actually a lot of freedom in that, because I didn't have ballroom dance down perfectly by the time I got out there.

JD: I thought that every dance we did. I thought that during the week two jive, and it was intriguing to me that [the judges] were so critical of certain dances. However, when I look back, there was a lot of growth for me.

There was a whole element of this that was sort of discovering my strength in a new way because I have had three children, I've navigated huge life transitions, I've built a career, a family, a life, And now, I'm stepping into this show where I'm pushing myself physically, emotionally, creatively in a way that I haven't in years. So I think I just surprised myself with how strong I am, and not just physically, but emotionally.

Jenna also faced an unexpected complication when her partner, Val, was suspended for one episode of the show following a social media post he made about DWTS judge Carrie Ann Inaba. After Carrie Ann criticized his wife, fellow pro dancer Jenna Johnson, for including what she considered an illegal lift in her routine with Harry Shum Jr., Val posted, “You almost saw Fresh Prince enter the ballroom last night.” The now-deleted post was widely interpreted as a reference to the 2022 Academy Awards, when Will Smith slapped Chris Rock after making a joke about his wife, Jada Pinkett Smith.

Carrie Ann appeared to interpret the post as a threat. She shared a screenshot of a Page Six story about it on Instagram, writing, “So this is what we are doing now?”

On Oct. 17, DWTS production company BBC Studios said Val would not participate in the Oct. 20 episode. Jenna danced with pro Alan Bersten in his place.

JD: I said this, and I really mean this, that I did not foresee having as many challenges as I did that were thrown at me during my Dancing with the Stars journey. However, the challenges of it gave me an opportunity to walk my talk, and I always think that when that happens, you can go back to your true north, to what’s your intention for doing something. How do you want to show up as a human when you’re experiencing challenges? And I kept going back to my kids. When the mid-season suspension happened, and all these things were going on, I thought, “I have a real opportunity here. I can be annoyed and bitter and upset about this, or I can show up with grace and love and joy and focus on the moment.”

That particular week was about my mom and my dedication to her. And she was in town, thrilled to have this dance with me, and thrilled to experience this moment. And I thought that this is something she's going to remember. We're all going to remember my family forever. Let's focus on that, and let's not worry about all of this that's going on.

JD: I’ve seen that, and I feel like with just life in general, even outside of Dancing with the Stars, that is absolutely something that most females have experienced. I think there's been a great change in our world, but I think for years upon years upon years, women have had a fight against misogyny and inherent bias. Women at times in life are just held to a different standard than men, and I think that that is a real reflection.

We're expected to be exceptional, but also humble and confident, but not too confident, skilled, but not struggling enough to earn praise. There's all this pressure on women. It feels like sometimes if a woman is strong or capable, the expectations become that much higher. I think people are so busy expecting women to be extraordinary that we forget to celebrate them for being extraordinary.

JD: It's never too late for a new chapter in life. And I think if there was like, one thing I wanted people to take away from my whole journey, it’s that we're never finished becoming who we are, and it's never too late to choose your own passion in life and your own joy in life. There isn't some age where you stop growing and dreaming and trying new things.

Alyssa Cruz is the social media editor at Women’s Health, where she drives social strategy and helps shape the brand’s voice across platforms. Prior to WH, she worked at CBS News and palabra. Originally from Toledo, she is a proud alumna of Ohio University, where she received two bachelor’s degrees in journalism and Spanish. When she is not creating social content, you can find her trying different restaurants in New York City or planning her next trip.

#4948. Bitcoin price ignores Anthropic debut as ANT share price dips below $100

Bitcoin dropped toward a third retest of $62,000 as Anthropic debuted on the Nasdaq, hitting highs of $115 per share before dropping below $100.

Bitcoin shrugged off US tech-stock strength at Thursday’s Wall Street open as markets reacted to Anthropic’s $111.50 debut.

Data from TradingView showed BTC/USD falling to $62,250 as US stock markets headed higher.

BTC/USD one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Excitement around Anthropic initially fueled 0.6% daily gains for the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index, which subsequently turned red on the day, down 1% at the time of writing. That followed 1.4% daily upside for South Korea’s tech-focused KOSPI Composite Index.

Nasdaq Composite Index one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Trading of the company’s stock on the Nasdaq under the ticker ANT began at $111.50, 34% above its IPO price of $83, reaching a local high of $115 before dropping below the $100 mark amid ongoing volatility.

Anthropic shares one-minute chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Perpetual futures traders on Hyperliquid had already placed fair value at around $103, based on a company valuation of around $1.845 trillion. Reacting, trading resource The Kobeissi Letter noted that the opening had pushed the valuation above $2 trillion, putting it in sixth place among the world’s most valuable public companies.

Previously, Cointelegraph reported on the record-breaking IPO helping to renew market trust in the AI sector amid ongoing uncertainty over Nvidia following the ousting of its CEO Jensen Huang.

“With some of the tumultuous times here for Nvidia, a little bit of that trust has gone away from the retail investor so this is the way people I think are going to express themselves,” J.J. Kinahan, senior vice president of retail and alternative investments at CBOE Global Markets, told CNBC.

In his latest analysis, economist Mohamed El-Erian called the IPO “historic.”

“On the micro side: In five years, it has gone from a startup to one of the world’s most valuable companies. It’s the largest IPO ever. On the macro side: Lots worth monitoring, starting with the flow-of-funds implications,” he summarized.

#4946. Women Share the Best, Most Unexpected Products That Got Them Through Marathon Training

Because sometimes you don’t know you need something until it’s too late.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

There are some things you know you need before you start training for a marathon, like a good pair of sneakers. But some of the most useful marathon items? You don’t even realize how helpful they are until you’re deep in the thick of training.

We’re talking the non-glitzy, random products that make life easier when you’re spending multiple days running each week. For me, one of those things is canned salmon. I buy the stuff in bulk because when I get back from a long run, it only takes me 20 seconds to whip the stuff up with some salt, pepper, and mayo and serve it on crackers before I collapse on the couch for a few hours. (It’s a kinda-smelly, but ridiculously simple way to get protein fast.)

Curious what other unexpected finds made a difference, I asked women to share the products they never knew they needed before running a marathon but now can't imagine training without. Check out their answers…

There is nothing like race day, especially for your first marathon. And, for the most part, you know what you need: Your trusty sneakers, an outfit you’ve hopefully tested during training, your fuel, and your watch. Here are three more things that will help the day go by even better.

“Having your name on a shirt or bib is soooo fun because even strangers will cheer you on along the course! It will help you feel the true energy and vibe of the race.” —Claudette Sariya, 33, Honolulu ’25 (You can use iron-on letters from Amazon, or go through a company like Pens and Paces to commission your own bib.)

“Rally is a great way to feel supported by your loved ones who can’t make the race, or maybe you don’t have time to stop if you’re going for a personal best. At mile 10 of my first marathon, I was struggling a bit and my mom’s message came through my headphones—I hear her words every time I run a race now. Such a special concept that really enhanced my race day!” — Shannon Kauber, 25, NYC ’25, ’26, Chicago ’26

“After a race, because I have so many apps running (like Strava, Spotify, and Rally), my battery can be very low, and it sucks when I’m on four percent and looking for friends and family or want to take pictures, so it’s good to have a portable charger just in case. I’m less anxious knowing that I’ll be able to get in touch with people and that my phone is good to go. And if my phone dies randomly, then I’ll have backup juice.” —Sariya (Any charger will work, but Sariya has seen success with Anker brand ones.)

“I had my boyfriend meet me at the halfway point with a cold towel and it was a game changer. It made me feel instantly more awake and energetic.” —Tianna Soto, 32, NYC ’24, NYC Virtual ’26 (You can stay low-tech and put an old towel in the freezer, or you can opt for a cooling towel that helps you regulate your body temp.)

These products all help remove any uncomfortable rubbing and chafing from their gear to keep you comfortable over many, many miles.

“I keep it on my bathroom counter so I don’t forget to swipe at minimum inner thighs and inner arms for a training run. Long runs and race days get the full treatment with swipes under any and every seam, around the toes, waistbands, and shoulders under straps. I love traveling with the teeny travel version too.” —Jennifer Nied, 40, NYC ’17, ’19, Chicago ’18, London ’24

“The bigger ones saved me when I used to get these crazy arch blisters from when I first started running. I usually use those, but I’ve also used the smaller ones for toe blisters.” —Geneve Lau, 27, Marine Corps ’24, NYC ’25, ’26

“I swear by [these] soft little tubes that go over your toe to help prevent friction and pressure when you’re running or walking. I just started using them this year, and they’ve saved my pinkie toes during training and the marathon!” —Soto

Fueling is one of the most important parts of marathon training, and chances are you've already experimented with your fair share of gels. But sometimes, the foods and snacks you never thought to try end up making the biggest difference.

“I liked clementines as a whole food before moving to gels and chews. Clementine wedges are bite-sized so I wasn’t as worried about choking while running but they weren’t chewy, saving those textures for other fuel. They have water and sugar, though I never did the math on the amount of sugar compared to my needs while training (I know better now). During training, I would toss two whole clementines in my running hydration vest pockets and peel as I ran to occupy my hands/brain with something other than running. On race day, I pre-peeled the food and put the slices in a snack sized ziploc.” —Suzi Berkey Hanson, Chicago ’23

“As someone with endometriosis and a resulting sensitive G.I. system, taking in a high volume of carbs at any point in time is highly aggravating to my stomach and often causes high levels of nausea, bloating, and discomfort. So, I decided to switch to more solid foods that included a healthy source of fats and proteins mixed in with carbs. In this marathon training block, I would alternate Kind Bars with Honey Stinger Chews which sat much better with my stomach and made my race and post race recovery much more pleasant.” —Winnie Yu, 31, NYC ’23, ’26

“I ate one pretty much after every morning run because they were easy to throw in my bag and defrost on the way to work and they’re great for quick carbs. They were also great to eat before a long run because, again, they require basically no prep. When you’re marathon training and working a full-time job sometimes you just gravitate towards whatever is easiest and whatever fits into your routine best but still helps you meet your goals and this was mine” —Andrea Bian, 26, NYC ’25, ’26

Olivia Luppino is an associate editor at Women’s Health. She reports, writes, and edits content ranging from service stories to in-depth features and awards packages across all verticals, including health, fitness, sports, and beauty. While at WH, she’s traveled to Paris, France to watch Faith Kipyegon attempt a sub-4-minute mile and covered major races like Ultra Trail du Mont Blanc in Chamonix. Olivia is a RRCA-certified running coach who is especially passionate about helping people start their running journey and is training for her second marathon. Her works also appears in New York Magazine’s The Cut, PS (formerly Popsugar), and Salon, where she also did on-camera interviews with celebrity guests.

#4945. Why higher rates could slow Bitcoin buying at treasury firms

A treasury company can have years of dividends covered and still struggle to sell new shares on terms that make another Bitcoin purchase worthwhile.

Rising US interest rates could slow buying by Bitcoin treasury companies long before they have trouble paying their bills.

Strategy raised $244.7 million by selling common stock in the week ended Nov. 15. It spent $206.3 million of the proceeds buying back preferred shares and $33.4 million on 485 BTC.

At the time, its US dollar assets covered more than three years of preferred dividends and debt interest at current payouts. When Treasury bills pay more, investors can demand a higher yield to buy newly issued preferred shares.

For common stock, StoneX analyst Mark Palmer watches how much investors will pay above the value of the Bitcoin each share represents. At a premium, a company can issue stock and buy enough Bitcoin (BTC) to leave shareholders with more BTC per share. However, as that premium narrows, so does the gain.

#4944. 37 Best Housewarming Gifts to Get You Welcomed Back (2026)

Yes, the best housewarming gifts should ideally be useful, but that doesn’t mean you should completely abandon elements of charm and whimsy when you’re selecting the perfect item. Whether you’re bestowing something special upon a new homeowner or a pal settling into a new apartment, a vibe-enhancing and aesthetically pleasing present goes a long way to add warmth and personality to any new space.

With that in mind, we’ve rounded up the best housewarming gifts—many of which have blessed our own spaces—at various price points. You’ll find practical presents, such as throw blankets and kitchen tools; home decor items, like chic glassware; and a few indulgences, like caviar. Conveniently, several options also work as hostess gifts.

Here are some of our absolute favorite housewarming gift ideas for kicking off a whole new era of feeling at home, in an elevated way.

This lightweight set of borosilicate glasses has a playful raised-spot pattern. They’re blessedly dishwasher-safe, which matters a lot for glasses in daily use.

This high-quality reprint of a 1902 guest book is shopping writer Julia Harrison’s go-to housewarming gift. The book instructs guests to draw a pig rather than the more traditional practice of writing a note. This is “for most guests, a great relief,” says Harrison.

It’s not often that we come across a toolbox that is actually attractive. This model from Japanese brand Toyo sure is, and it’s just the right size for essentials, like screwdrivers, a level, and a measuring tape. It’s sure to get used by the end of the first week in every new home.

Kitchen towels get used harder than almost anything else in a new home. These are heavy cotton terry made in Portugal, with a jacquard pattern on one side and loops on the other. They’re thick enough to stand in for a trivet on many surfaces, which most kitchen towels can’t claim.

Gifting someone beautiful custom stationery is a thoughtful and useful way to make their new chapter that much more official. It’s one of the easier housewarming gifts to personalize, and you’re sure to smile when you receive a handwritten thank-you note.

This isn’t your grandpa’s soap on a rope. Ash’s revamp involves a spicy yet floral scent and a pop of color in its elegant red string.

For those who prefer to sip their spirits neat, these hand-blown mezcal glasses are a vibrant way to toast to the new digs.

Do the eternal (or aspirational!) host or hostess a favor, and introduce this AD-favorite coffee table book into their collection. Its pages are full of dinner-party inspiration, including interviews with tastemakers, like Laura Gonzalez, about their entertaining philosophy.

Any coffee enthusiast will treasure a high-quality French press, and we’re partial to this design from Le Creuset. The kitchen must-have is available in a number of colorways to suit neutral tastes or a more maximalist style.

Feed their caffeine addiction with style by showing up to a housewarming with a sleek-looking coffee maker and a bag of their favorite French roast.

Help the amateur chef in your friend group get a head start on their new home’s kitchen essentials. Flamingo Estate is known for its homegrown pantry wares; this oil-and-vinegar gift set includes a California-grown olive oil and persimmon vinegar. In a rush? This gift set comes conveniently prewrapped.

Steak knives don’t feel like an essential until you’re sawing through prime rib with a butter knife. We love this set for its approachable price point and handsome riveted wood handle. (And don’t worry, though inspired by a 19th-century French design, these blades are dishwasher-safe.)

These hand-painted candles elevate the traditional taper. Choose between three floral motifs to best suit their style and personality.

Take the stress out of floral arrangements with a ruffled set of bud vases. Each varies in height to make a dramatic array, whether filled with single stems or small bunches.

Senior shopping editor Nashia Baker loves this blanket, which she says is “incredibly fluffy, warm, and easy to care for.” The faux mohair throw feels soft, has elegant-looking plush tassels at either end, and it’s light enough to stay out on the sofa all year. It comes in a variety of colorways that will accent and elevate any living room.

Anyone with a predilection for kitschy-chic home decor will swoon over these delicate shell napkin rings from Gohar World. Gifting this set will likely seal your invite to their upcoming dinner party.

A bouquet is a classic gift on its own, but you can level it up by presenting it arranged in a stunning vase. This one from Zara Home features a graphic black-and-white stripe that perfectly contrasts a soft floral display.

The best incense holders look like intentional objects even when you’re out of palo santo. Made from raw sand clay, this piece closely mimics the luxurious look of marble while maintaining a playful shape that transforms your tabletop into a micro sculpture garden.

Every kitchen needs a salt dish, and this handcrafted set moves from the counter to the tabletop with ease. Just add a loaf of fresh bread and a good cutting board for the ideal food-prep setup.

If they’ve finally retired their mismatched plates, this is the replacement they need. The 12-piece set from Villeroy & Boch serves four in white porcelain that’s textured to look like matte slate. It would make an equally good wedding gift.

Cheaper napkins exist, of course, but how many resemble 12th-century geometric stone mosaics? This set of four from Cabana will become an heirloom. While hand-embroidered napkins might be a particularly kind touch, purchasing these is perhaps the more sensible choice if you’re on a tight timeline (and don’t know how to stitch).

The hand-blown glass bottle arrives filled with Le Soir hand and body wash, scented with cedar and tangerine. But it will look just as good by the sink once it’s refilled with whatever they buy next.

A custom caviar tin is the fanciest way to celebrate new beginnings. This one from CY Kitchen comes with a mother-of-pearl serving spoon to complete the experience.

Ensure a new homeowner’s greenery thrives with a shiny new watering can and mister gift set. The sculptural design is great for those who still want to prioritize aesthetics when tending to their plant babies.

Cheese boards are a longstanding go-to housewarming gift, and this oyster platter is a surprising and luxurious take on the dishware. It looks like it was plucked right off Gwyneth Paltrow’s kitchen wall and will definitely get a ton of use from a lover of seafood.

Channel a French café vibe with this elevated cutlery set from Parisian label Sabre.

For your coziest friend, a luxe throw blanket is the gift that keeps on giving—for movie nights, late mornings, and everything in between.

Winding stainless steel creates a surrealist botanical motif across the handles of these salad servers. They’re so good, we’re gifting them to everyone we know as housewarming gifts and as a wedding gift when the registry has been picked over.

For the friend who moved solely for a better kitchen, a handblown glass cake stand can almost guarantee that you’ll be the first on call when it’s time to taste-test those new recipes.

For the fashionable host or hostess: This tablecloth takes plissé pleats out of their wardrobe and puts it onto their table.

Little comes in handy as much as a tabletop lighter. Suddenly, there’s no need to rifle through drawers, searching for matches to set the ambience. This sculptural ceramic option looks intentionally placed wherever it sits and makes for a great conversation piece.

For all the champagne toasts to come, SLJ’s coupe set feels special enough to mark any big occasion or milestone. Most people already move in with a set of wine glasses, making coupes the perfect gift.

#4942. Bitcoin Enters Death Cross as Anthropic IPO Fails to Lift Wall Street

Bitcoin Enters Death Cross as Anthropic IPO Fails to Lift Wall Street

Bitcoin traded around $62,640 Thursday, down 0.6% on the day, as Anthropic's record-breaking stock market debut failed to reverse a broader selloff in risk assets.

The cryptocurrency has lost roughly 28% from its late-September peak near $87,000, and doesn’t seem on its way to stop anytime soon.

Anthropic began trading on Nasdaq under ANT after pricing 980 million shares at $83, raising $81.34 billion. Its stock opened at $111.50, up 34%, and briefly pushed the Claude maker's market value past $2 trillion, as Decrypt reported.

The enthusiasm stopped at Anthropic's ticker.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

By 12:18 p.m. ET, the Nasdaq Composite was down 1.55%, the S&P 500 had lost 1.59%, and the Dow was off 2.03%. Investors remain concerned about expensive AI infrastructure and whether the spending will deliver adequate returns. The weak reaction beyond Anthropic undercut hopes that a blockbuster IPO could revive appetite for beaten-down technology stocks.

Crypto's weakness predates Thursday's listing. U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs have shed roughly $2.9 billion this month, including $306.4 million Wednesday, amid rising Treasury yields and expectations of further Federal Reserve rate hikes.

Some traders argue the IPO has diverted capital from crypto, but that remains unproven. As Decrypt previously noted, Bitcoin and AI stocks have declined together, suggesting a wider retreat from risk rather than a simple rotation into Anthropic shares.

Bitcoin's daily chart remains decisively bearish. At around $62,640, BTC trades below both its 50-day and 200-day exponential moving averages (EMAs), with the shorter average beneath the longer one.

This is a bearish alignment commonly associated with an EMA-based death cross. Traditionally, a death cross occurs when the 50-day simple moving average crosses below the 200-day average, signaling deteriorating shorter-term momentum. The EMA variation responds more quickly to recent prices. The crossover was confirmed today.

Momentum reinforces the picture. The relative strength index, or RSI, reads 21.3, well below the 30 threshold traders associate with oversold conditions. That can precede a relief rally, but oversold assets can keep falling.

The average directional index, or ADX, stands at 41.2. ADX measures the strength of a trend, not its direction, with readings above 25 generally indicating a strong trend. Here, the negative directional indicator exceeds its positive counterpart, confirming sellers have the advantage. A high ADX reading alone does not mean a reversal is imminent, even with RSI already deep in oversold territory.

Bitcoin has also been making lower highs and lower lows since September, with November's sharp drop slicing through several support levels. A bullish recovery would first need to reclaim $66,512, followed by resistance around $69,292 and $71,539. Above those levels, $73,785 and then $76,984 would present tougher tests of the prevailing bearish structure.

On the downside, the chart marks immediate support near $62,019, almost directly beneath the current price. A decisive daily close below that level could expose the psychological $60,000 threshold, followed by the chart's next Fibonacci extension around $56,841.

The RSI leaves room for a bounce, but the EMAs and ADX show how much work buyers still face. A move through $66,512 would open a test of $69,292; a close under $62,019 would instead put $60,000 back in focus.

#4938. Danielle Frankel Reimagines the 21st Century Bridal Store

After nearly 10 years in business, fashion designer Danielle Frankel Hirsch has opened a 12,000-square-foot location in Tribeca with interiors set to change the bridal game. To create the new space, which opened in early November, Frankel Hirsch worked with AD PRO Directory designer Augusta Hoffman and Studio Partners Architecture.

The nearly two-year design process is complete, and the Danielle Frankel bridal salon is now ready for appointments. “We’re finally at the point where my heart and my head have been,” says Frankel Hirsch, who worked with Hoffman on her previous Los Angeles boutique and New York City atelier. “Because this is our third space together, we can almost speak in shorthand now.”

Frankel Hirsch on the staircase wearing a shirt she designed from Fortuny fabric. Hoffman used the same Fortuny print throughout the space for wrapping side tables, keepsake boxes on the shelves, as well as for the woven seats on barstools.

The unassuming entrance to Danielle Frankel on Hudson Street

In the reception room just inside the street entrance, the desk is custom, the chairs are antique, and the custom pendant is by Jamb.

Hoffman worked with the artist Miriam Ellner to create eglomise paneling surrounding the staircase. “The eglomise is one of my favorite things because when we were working with the artist we worked so intimately with her,” Frankel Hirsch says. “We worked on the placement the way that we do our laces. We kept going back and forth with the drawings because I wanted it to feel like our lace placement.”

On a recent morning, a bride and her wedding party were busy in the VIP suite making a final selection. The beaming group entered the space through an unassuming brown storefront entrance on Harrison Street, its two large glass windows encased in a sheer linen. From there they ascended a staircase up to a large gallery with over 30-foot floor-to-ceiling windows overlooking the streets below on two sides, and a hand-painted mural pattern above on the ceiling.

The interiors of the original 19th-century Queen Anne–style red brick building were transformed by Hoffman (full disclosure: I am writing Hoffman’s design monograph publishing with Rizzoli in Fall 2027) into a modern, elegant bridal boutique that honors the history of the original New York Mercantile Exchange, first established in the 1880s to keep the standards of the grocer business. “We wanted the space to stay pretty bright and focus on the architecture, but we needed a lot of seating,” Hoffman says. “There was a lot of pressure because we wanted it to feel like Danielle and we wanted to leave our mark on the space, but it’s also so beautiful as is and we didn’t want to have too many interventions that take away from that.”

The rug, vintage Dutch leather armchairs, and custom clothing racks create one of the four seated zones in the gallery area. The wall features antique mottled glass windows. “We wanted this wall to feel historically accurate,” Hoffman says.

The custom oak screen features shelves for displaying miniature gowns by Danielle Frankel. The shelves are lined in silver leaf. The antique stool is reupholstered in a Fortuny fabric.

The view of the gallery area from a second floor bridal suite. The vintage teak seating set is reupholstered in a fabric by Manuel Canovas and the glass cocktail tables and Fortuny wrapped side tables are custom. The custom window panels are by Augusta Hoffman Studio for Wovn Home and the custom curtain hardware are bronze leaves designed by Hoffman. Hoffman pulled the leaf design from the details on the columns.

What previously functioned as a large, open space with a small mezzanine, had little existing infrastructure–no electrical, HVAC, or plumbing—and is now an area where brides can plot as they make the most important decision for their wedding day. In the gallery, Hoffman created distinct zones where wedding parties wait before their appointment in a private fitting room.

Much of the furniture was sourced by Hoffman and Frankel Hirsch together on trips to Paris. Antique glass vitrines separate the space, which they included from the previous New York gallery and filled with Frankel Hirsch’s own family wedding photos as well as printed details brides can use for their fitting. One vitrine has a board layered with references of crinoline and interlining. Another has custom Danielle Frankel cufflinks. An oak folding screen lines one half of the room, with miniature gowns set on shelves lined in silver leaf paper. Custom parchment and cerused oak racks Hoffman designed to withstand the weight of a wedding dress line the floor displaying roughly 65 dresses from Danielle Frankel collections.

The reflection of the wall is seen in the mirrored structure designed to house the elevator bank and stairwell to the street entrance. A framed antique floral plaster relief is flanked with 1930s Italian wall sconces in patinated glass and metal.

The staircase and elevator bank within the mirrored structure. “We had to add the elevator for function and for code, and we were like ‘How do we take this existing architecture and put an elevator in the middle of the room?’” Hoffman says. “It feels super unexpected, versus trying to hide it, and you get to see more of the surrounding architecture and light in the reflection.”

The custom rug is topped with an antique stool upholstered in a Fortuny fabric, a 1950s chair, and a 19th-century English pine console table.

The view from the sitting area adjacent to a bar. The vintage teak seating set was among the first items Hoffman and Frankel Hirsch sourced for the space. The custom bar stool is woven with a Fortuny fabric.

The view from within the staircase to the gallery area. The custom center table is by Rafael Carneiro Triboli and the French chairs are from the 1930s.

Hoffman and Studio Partners Architects took what was formerly the mezzanine, originally separated from the below with only a single-bar metal railing, and made it into a full wall with antiqued mottled glass windows. In the center they constructed a cascading grand staircase, cleverly concealing the stockroom underneath, which leads upstairs to three private bridal suites. The hallways, painted in Wenge by Benjamin Moore, lead to the bridal suites, each designed differently and filled with antique and vintage furniture, custom carpets, lighting, and racks. The walls are painted in Presidente Roman Clay by Portola Paints, an earthy contrast to the white and ivory dresses, which are naturally the center of attention for visitors in each room.

When guests are finished in their suites, there’s a bar in the gallery space where wedding parties can sit and make the final selection. The wedding party that morning had selected the dress and were toasting champagne to celebrate. “It’s just as much about the hospitality and the warmth as it is about the gowns,” Frankel Hirsch says.

In the VIP bridal suite, which can be accessed through a discreet entrance on Harrison Street, the custom sofa is upholstered in Holland & Sherry’s Corbel in Henna. The artwork features fragments of 19th century Japanese brocade.

The midcentury armchairs are upholstered in a Watts 1874 fabric and flank a vintage oak table.

The rack, mirror, and ceiling pendant are all custom in the VIP bridal suite.

In the dressing area of the VIP bridal suite, the ceiling pendant is custom and the closet wall covering is by Watts 1874.

The 1930s lounge chair is reupholstered in a Fortuny fabric and the 1970s lamp is from Brazil.
