#4937. This Common Drug Was Just Linked to a 24% Lower Risk of Dementia

For all of the havoc dementia causes to a person's life, scientists are still trying to figure out exactly how to prevent it. While keeping your brain and body active and following a healthy diet seems to help, there doesn't seem to be a pill you can pop to lower your risk of developing the devastating disease. But new research suggests that a common osteoporosis drug might be worth investigating further.

It's a class of medications called nitrogen-containing bisphosphonates (or NBPs). While these are typically used to strengthen bones in people with osteoporosis, NBPs could have the added benefit of supporting brain health as you age, too.

It's important to stress this now: The researchers found a link between people who used NBPs and a lower risk of dementia, but they didn't prove that the medications themselves actually dropped the risk. Still, doctors say there could be something to this. "The association is intriguing," says Anna Hohler, MD, director of neurology for Northwell Health's Westchester Region, and chair of neurology at Phelps Hospital and Northern Westchester Hospital.

Here's what experts want you to take away from the findings.

Meet the experts: Ching-Lung Cheung, PhD, study co-author and associate professor in the Department of Pharmacology and Pharmacy at the University of Hong Kong; Clifford Segil, DO, a neurologist at Providence Saint John's Health Center in Santa Monica, California; and Anna Hohler, MD, director of neurology for Northwell Health's Westchester Region, and chair of neurology at Phelps Hospital and Northern Westchester Hospital.

The study, which was published in the journal Alzheimer's & Dementia, analyzed the electronic medical records of more than 120,000 patients aged 60 and older in Hong Kong. All of the participants had osteoporosis or fractures due to fragility (meaning, their bones had weakened).

Researchers discovered that people who took NBPs had a 16 percent lower risk of developing Alzheimer's disease and related dementias compared to those who didn't take medication for osteoporosis. But when they compared those who used NBPs to those taking other types of osteoporosis medications, the dementia risk was 24 percent lower.

The link seemed to be especially strong in women and patients who had hip fractures.

The researchers were inspired to look into NBPs and Alzheimer’s because the drug had been shown to improve cognitive function in previous animal models of neurotoxicity and Alzheimer’s disease, says Ching-Lung Cheung, PhD, study co-author and associate professor in the Department of Pharmacology and Pharmacy at the University of Hong Kong. “These findings led us to investigate whether their use might also be associated with a lower risk of dementia in humans."

While the study didn't prove that NBPs were responsible for the lowered risk, doctors have a few theories on what could be driving this.

For one, it’s possible that the link is more indirect, according to Clifford Segil, DO, a neurologist at Providence Saint John's Health Center in Santa Monica, California. NBPs improve bone density over time, which allows people to be more active, Dr. Segil says. "Broken bones do not cause dementia but do decrease activity," he says. (Being sedentary is a risk factor for dementia.)

There may also be something about NBPs themselves that could lower the risk of dementia, though. "Some researchers speculate that these medications could influence inflammation, immune pathways, or other biological processes involved in neurodegeneration," Dr. Hoehler says. But, she points out, more research is needed.

For now, doctors don't plan to prescribe NBPs in an effort to lower dementia risk. But Dr. Cheung says it should make people feel confident about using this class of medication for bone health. "Given the low uptake of osteoporosis treatment, people with osteoporosis should consider appropriate anti-osteoporosis medication, which may offer an additional benefit for brain health," he says. Even though osteoporosis drugs are commonly available, uptake has been historically low—studies have previously estimated that around seven percent of women with osteoporosis actually take medication for it.

Ultimately, doctors say that a clinical trial is needed to see if NBPs actually lower the risk of dementia. "If future studies confirm these results, they could open an exciting new avenue for Alzheimer's prevention or treatment," Dr. Hoehler says.

Carina Hsieh, MPH, is the deputy features editor of Women’s Health. She has more than a decade’s worth of experience working in media and has covered everything from beauty, fashion, travel, lifestyle, pets, to health.

She began her career as an intern in the fashion closet at Cosmopolitan where she worked her way up to Senior Sex & Relationships Editor. While covering women’s health there, she discovered her passion for health service journalism and took a break to get her Masters in Public Health. Post-grad school, she worked as a freelance writer and as The Daily Beast’s first Beauty, Health, and Wellness Reporter.

Carina is an alum of the Fashion Institute of Technology and the Yale School of Public Health. She and her French Bulldog, Bao Bao, split their time between Brooklyn and Connecticut. She enjoys reformer Pilates, (slow) running, and smelling the fancy toiletries in boutique fitness class locker rooms.

#4935. Ethereum’s Glamsterdam upgrade could slip to May 2027 over compatibility concerns

Ethereum developers are weighing changes to gas pricing proposals that could disrupt existing smart contracts, after contacting at least 50 affected companies.

Ethereum’s Glamsterdam upgrade could be pushed back as far as May 2027 if developers decide to modify its specifications to address smart contract compatibility issues, according to discussions at Thursday’s All Core Developers meeting.

Glamsterdam is a planned Ethereum hard fork aimed at increasing the network’s capacity and efficiency. However, blockchain security firm ChainSecurity warned in October that proposed changes to Ethereum’s gas pricing could disrupt existing smart contracts and potentially expose them to exploitation, prompting developers to postpone the Hoodi testnet fork, originally scheduled for Oct. 26.

The concerns center on gas repricing proposals EIP-8037 and EIP-8038. Researchers identified 13 real-world examples of potentially affected contracts in October, while a November investigation examined eight smart contract invariants that could be disrupted by the upgrade.

During Thursday’s meeting, developers said the Ethereum Foundation, ChainSecurity and security firm Dedaub had collectively contacted at least 50 affected companies. Three projects were classified as high impact, with fixes completed or expected by mid-December, while medium-impact projects reported timelines extending to Jan. 10.

Developers debated whether to retain Glamsterdam’s existing specifications or modify them to reduce compatibility risks, as changes intended to protect some contracts could disrupt others.

“Every spec change could, again, break new invariants that we are not aware of breaking yet,” one developer warned during the meeting.

Under the current specifications, mainnet activation could come as early as mid-January 2027. However, waiting until January to decide could push activation to late February or early March, or April or May if modifications are adopted, and no decision has been reached yet.

Related: Vitalik ‘pivots’ ETH to store of value, crypto suffers after US midterms: Hodler’s Digest

Separately, Ethereum’s next planned hard fork, Hegotá, could see its scope reduced as the network seeks to accelerate its transition to post-quantum cryptography.

During the meeting, developers considered postponing some Ethereum Improvement Proposals to prioritize post-quantum features, although several argued that reducing the upgrade’s scope would offer little benefit unless it also shortened the rollout timeline.

Developers also raised compatibility concerns over EIP-8151, a proposal designed to protect accounts migrating to post-quantum security. A preliminary review identified a potentially large number of existing smart contracts that could be affected by the changes, complicating the proposal’s inclusion in Hegotá.

No decision was reached on Hegotá’s scope, with client teams expected to submit their positions ahead of further discussions in two weeks.

#4931. ‘Dune: Part Three’: An Exclusive First Look at the Sets

Nothing about the cinematic language of Dune is small—director Denis Villeneuve’s film trilogy does span multiple planets, after all. But throughout his work on the movies, including the upcoming Dune: Part Three, production designer Patrice Vermette has made sure to stay focused on the little details.

“My approach has always been with symbolism and semiotics,” Vermette tells AD. “It is my philosophy of design, because for Denis, empty design cannot exist. Things need to have a purpose. Need to have a signification.”

In conversation with Vermette about the sci-fi epic, examples of that core tenet come up often. Carefully designed details imbue hidden meaning in each set, creating a captivating, immersive on-screen world that earned the designer an Oscar for his work in the first Dune. The trilogy’s latest installment, Dune: Part Three, which lands in theaters on December 18, expands on that ethos. And for the final film in the series, the sheer scale of the sets was larger. “It was our last chance working in that world,” Vermette says. “It’s like in a fireworks [display], it’s the end, it’s the bouquet final, it’s like, ‘alright, let’s do it!’”

Robert Pattinson as Scytale, with director Denis Villeneuve.

Over 450,000 square feet of interior stages and 475,000 square feet of backlots were used for scenes shot in Budapest alone, with each set typically taking 14 to 16 weeks to build. (Filming also took place in Abu Dhabi and Iceland.) Even the sandworms that slither through the titular dunes got grander this time around: Instead of the relatively small platforms that were used to film worm-riding sequences as in the previous films, the production team built a 300-foot-long by 65-foot-wide sandworm in the middle of the desert.

Like in Dune: Part Two, Vermette was challenged to create entirely new sets for this installment, which adapts Frank Herbert’s 1969 novel Dune Messiah. One of the new settings viewers can expect is Wallach IX, the home of the powerful and secretive Bene Gesserit sisterhood. Throughout their planet, Vermette incorporated stone etchings that resemble human cells seen through a microscope, referencing the Bene Gesserit’s breeding program that has gone on for millennia. Mother nature also informed the architecture. “When you arrive on the planet, the buildings are basically eggs of a spider, like crops of spider eggs as you find them in nature,” Vermette explains. “Ecology has always been a huge source of inspiration for me, and also for the storytelling it gives—you know, they’ve got their web across the universe, for the genealogy they create.”

Another new setting is the Veteran’s District, where the Fremen, the native inhabitants of the desert planet Arrakis, retire after fighting in the catastrophic holy war spurred by Paul Atreides, aka Muad’Dib (Timothée Chalamet). In the previous films, the Fremen lived in communal residences lined with hand-carved stone that recorded their history and culture. Returning from the battlefield, they find mass-produced housing units instead of the “green paradise” they were promised would await them. Made of ceramic instead of rock, with stamped-on rather than hand-carved markings, the Veteran’s District represents an artificial facsimile of their former homes. “And when you put all these units together, and you look at the rooftops, it’s there to represent sand dunes,” Vermette explains. “All these triangles align [to look like dunes], but it’s fake.”

Another circular doorway represents an all-seeing eye near the lush Imperial Gardens, which illustrate Paul’s extreme wealth and control of resources—including water—on the dry planet of Arrakis.

There is a green paradise on Arrakis, just not for the Fremen. Lush gardens grace the planet’s Imperial Palace, a massive inverted pyramid with a large circle cut out of it—a reimagined version of the all-seeing eye. “I got inspired by the title of the book, Messiah, and from the fact that Denis, just like Frank Herbert, wanted to make sure that Paul Atreides was not going to be seen as a hero, and it’s a tale of being cautious about messianic figures,” says Vermette. Similar representations of the all-seeing eye are found throughout the palace, sinisterly implying that Muad’Dib is always watching.

In actuality, Muad’Dib’s personal quarters are completely isolated from the rest of the palace. His wife, Irulan (Florence Pugh), must find her way through a twisting mass of hallways to reach him. “Basically, she’s walking into the psyche of Paul,” Vermette explains. “She goes into a void, and then it’s all these mazes of one’s mind.” When we get to the end of the maze, we see that Paul’s inner sanctum resembles a humble desert camp. Not unlike the Fremen, his dwelling has been reduced to an echo of what once was. He is “living as a recluse because he doesn’t trust anybody,” Vermette explains. “He wants to go back to a place where he felt the most comfortable, which was the desert. So he recreated that space.”

“Denis really wanted that set to be the golden cage of Irulan,” Vermette says of these private chambers. “She’s married to the emperor, but she’s not free at all, she’s a prisoner.” The window is shaped similar to an infinity symbol, but is covered in metal screens. “It’s like eternity, but eternity is blocked. It’s like a cage.”

The entrance to the maze within Muad’Dib’s inner sanctum. Vermette designed 40 maze wall panels that needed to be moved into various configurations to shoot the scene, which was filmed in less than a day. Eight people were each assigned a portion of the 12-foot-high panels and learned a choreography of sorts, quickly moving into each configuration until they could do each one in five minutes or less. “We rehearsed and rehearsed, just like dancers or musicians; they knew exactly where to fit their pieces” within the roughly 14,400-square-foot space, says Vermette.

On the planet Wallach IX, the office of Bene Gesserit Reverend Mother Mohaim features stone carvings inspired by human cells under a microscope.

The Imperial Palace throne room took 20 weeks to build. Behind Muad’Dib’s emerald throne, the windows are shielded with metal cast screens inspired by Montreal’s Saint-Ambrose Church, which was built in 1925 by architect Ernest Cormier.

The all-seeing eye is a repeating symbol Vermette inserted throughout the Imperial Palace, including a representation seen here as the circular entrance of Paul’s Keep.

The architecture of this Imperial Palace corridor has slanted walls to reflect a pyramid-shaped exterior and flying buttress support structures inspired by Gothic-style cathedrals.

#4929. Mel Robbins Started Lifting Weights in Her 50s. Here’s What Her Gym Routine Looks Like

The podcast host breaks it down on the latest episode of Strong Like.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

Mel Robbins has always been a mover—running, hiking, skiing, Pilates, yoga, you name it—but it’s only been recently that the 58-year-old has added strength training to her fitness regimen. She credits Dr. Stacy Sims (a Women’s Health advisory board member) and Dr. Vonda Wright, both guests on The Mel Robbins Podcast, with finally persuading her to lift weights.

“They kept saying, when we reach a certain age, 40 and up, for us to start resistance training and with heavier weights,” says Mel, who had to overcome a few fitness myths to finally prioritize pumping iron. “I was nervous I would bulk up. That's a joke, because you know how much you need to train to actually bulk up? I wasn't going to be doing that.”

So what exactly does Mel do in the gym? She walked WH through a typical session for the latest episode of Strong Like.

Before Mel’s feet ever touch a mat, she makes sure to jot down—with actual pen and paper!—what she intends to do at the gym. “This will ensure that you actually do it, because if you're relying on this noggin up here, it ain't happening,” she says.

With her to-do list by her side, Mel begins her session with a warm-up. She runs through a batter of stretches that include inchworms (with an added push-up—go Mel!) and lunges with a twist, before moving on to a banded overhead press to mobilize her shoulders. “I've stopped judging these little bands,” Mel says. “These little suckers look like nothing. Let me tell you, there's a whole lot of something in these bands. You'll be shocked at how much gain you can get and how great this is for you.”

With her warm-up complete, she moves on to the workout, shifting the bands from her arms to her legs for a duck walk. Mimicking the water fowl, she moves forward and back, side to side, keeping outward pressure on the bands.

Next, she tackles one of her favorite moves, a reverse lunge with medicine ball twist. “It's a compound movement that works mobility, your core, your arms, your legs, everything in one movement,” Mel says. “It's super-simple, painful, and awful, and it works like a charm.”

She moves on to the upper body with chest presses, though she has a hack for lighting up even more muscles in the process. “If you want to maximize what you're doing here, suck in your stomach and clench your butt, because then it's not just arms; it's a whole-body thing,” she says.

Finally, she concludes with a snatch, joking: “You can work out a lot of aggression at the world doing [these].”

Ultimately, says Mel, her approach to fitness is pretty straightforward: “I'm not in here because I want to look good in a bathing suit. I do, but that's not the motivation. I'm in here lifting weights because I want to live a long, vibrant, healthy life. I want to be dancing into my late 90s. I want to make sure I can pick up the suitcase and put it up in the overhead. I want to make sure that I am strong so my life is long.”

To hear more about Mel’s gym motivation, click play on the latest episode of Strong Like above!

#4928. 41 Best Gifts for Women on All Special Occasions (2026)

The best gifts for the women in your life are probably right under your nose. Consider the unique facets of their personality, their hobbies, and what lights them up. Is it watching countless episodes of Barefoot Contessa, dreaming about an upcoming trip to the Poconos, sampling every kind of Sephora mini, or curating a high-design interior vignette? No matter the answer, a great present should match their energy to ensure they end up with something that reflects their essence and also shows you’ve been paying attention.

We’ve compiled a curated list of gift ideas tailored to all sorts of interests to minimize the guesswork for birthdays, Mother’s Day, the holiday-gifting scramble, and every momentous occasion in between. Our picks run the gamut—from home decor gems and high-tech gadgets to skin-care gift sets and culinary staples—but are still selective enough to feel special during the big unboxing reveal.

Silk pillowcases not only do wonders for your skin and hair, but they also make your bedscape feel more luxurious. Gifting her a set is a guaranteed upgrade to her nighttime routine.

A special pair of champagne flutes to mark special occasions and milestones will become a treasured fixture in her barware collection. This cheerful duo features bubbly glass adornments that will pair perfectly with a bottle of her favorite bubbly.

Receiving any Le Creuset cookware is a win for anyone who feels most at home in the kitchen. This Dutch oven is a treat that will become a staple for home chefs, who can use to make endless roasts, stocks, casseroles, and more. So as long as you’re invited for dinner, it’s a win for you, too.

Author Lydia Davis is best known for very short stories, some of them a single sentence long, but her essays are where the reader can see how her mind works. This first volume gathers five decades of writing on reading, translating, Proust, early tourist photographs, and the city of Arles. Complete the gift by dropping her off at a café, where she can read it while people-watching and underlining passages. Don’t forget to buy her favorite coffee.

No gift guide would be complete without a cozy blanket, and we’re making a bid on this mohair one. The colorblocked number is comfortable enough to drape over her shoulders whether she’s snoozing or curled up with a book.

A tablecloth is a smart gift idea for the singular reason that it’s not something you’d really ever buy for yourself. It’s delightfully multiuse: covering the many drink rings on your walnut table, performing as a park blanket in a pinch, and—most importantly—giving your favorite gal a sense of cohesive calmness as she sets her table.

Whether or not she has the dreamy silverware to match, Sabre’s serving set with trendy burgundy handles will feel like a little luxury for plating cheese boards and opening bottles. Elegant and slightly retro, they’re versatile enough to accompany both bold and understated tabletop decorations.

Paper napkins are fine for any regular evening, but a hostess deserves a beautiful set of cloth linens. Borgo delle Tovaglie, the Italian linen house Valentina Muggia took over from her mother, makes these in soft-washed pure linen. The red colorway is embroidered with an ivory floral motif that makes even takeout look planned.

“Few things are so favored in my home as this tabletop lighter,” says shopping writer Julia Harrison. “I got it on a whim, and then found myself using it routinely every night.” Perfect for incense, candles, burning cursed love notes, or enjoying a cigarette on the fire escape. Don’t forget the butane.

For the minimalist coffee lover who can’t resist a little chrome, Louise Roe has just the cup-and-saucer set to slide under the tree.

Enza Fasano works in the Apulian pottery tradition of Grottaglie, a town in Puglia, Italy. Her Guido cup and saucer bring the relaxed sense of a Southern Italian breakfast table to an ordinary weekday morning. The jade glaze is a staff favorite, and the set mixes with the line’s other colors if she wants to build a collection. Nota bene: It’s dishwasher-safe.

One thing about many a woman: She loves a pitcher. Not necessarily for pouring lemonade, but for the concept that she could, should the occasion present itself. These thick glass styles from Il Buco Vita double as great vases, too, making them a great hostess gift. They also hold plenty of water for her guests at one of her routine dinner parties.

If getting in a chapter or two of her current book du jour before lights out is a must, she’ll find a new best friend in this little task lamp. The head is magnetized, so you can direct light in pretty much any direction you choose. The sage shade has a notably relaxing quality.

No one has ever been charmed by a cardboard Kleenex box, which is what makes this such a satisfying find. Todd Nickey and Amy Kehoe designed the handmade lacquer tissue box with James Lowther of the Lacquer Company for a collection drawn from antiques they’ve collected over the years. The cover, made in Vietnam, slips over most standard tissue boxes. In khaki and chocolate, it will look at home on a nightstand or vanity long after cold season ends.

The year’s best photo deserves better than her camera roll. For the Ornament, Framebridge prints a picture you upload and sets it in a tiny frame on a green velvet ribbon, with a hidden pocket on the back for a note, then ships it in a vintage-style matchbox. It’s small enough to be a stocking stuffer and sentimental enough to hold its own among the bigger Christmas gifts.

A pair of candleholders from design giant Herman Miller (of Eames fame) is among the most affordable ways to say: “I think you have great taste.” If you feel inclined, you could tuck in a couple of her favorite scented candles to complete the gift.

This text that was pivotal in contemporary color research will surely become a favorite in her own book collection. Both visually appealing and full of knowledge, it includes 348 color combinations design nerds (like us) can use in their next project.

If she’s the type to plan an entire trip around a hotel’s wallpaper, a Cabana Magazine subscription delivers three issues a year, focused on travel told through photography, writing, and graphic design. She’ll want it on her coffee table year-round.

She can still travel with all of her lotions and potions, but this time, she won’t have to check a bag just to bring that special moisturizer. Keep Your Cadence makes beautifully designed, TSA-approved toiletry cases and containers that all lock together with magnets for easy transporting.

#4925. Trump’s SEC nominee Johnson backs formal rulemaking for tokenized stock trading

Former CFTC Commissioner and Democrat Kristin Johnson and ex-SEC aide Steven Levine appeared before lawmakers Thursday.

President Donald Trump’s nominees to fill two vacancies at the US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) faced questions about investor protections, regulatory oversight and financial innovation during a Senate confirmation hearing Thursday, including scrutiny of the agency’s approach to tokenized securities.

Kristin Johnson, Trump’s Democratic nominee, backed formal rulemaking to address concerns about the agency’s exemption for tokenized stock trading.

“I feel strongly that regulation is made better through the notice and comment period,” Johnson told the Senate Banking Committee in response to Democratic Senator Angela Alsobrooks. She added that she would welcome the opportunity to address issues covered by the exemption through that process.

The SEC’s Sept. 17 exemption lets qualifying platforms trade blockchain-based versions of US-listed shares without registering as securities exchanges under certain conditions. The order took effect immediately and invites comments on possible changes.

Johnson, a former Commodity Futures Trading Commission commissioner, appeared alongside Republican Steven Levine, a former aide to SEC Commissioner Mark Uyeda. If confirmed, they would bring the five-member commission to four members, with three Republicans and one Democrat.

The hearing comes as the SEC has scaled back enforcement activity and pursued regulatory changes intended to accommodate digital assets, including efforts to facilitate tokenized securities that have drawn scrutiny from lawmakers and market participants.

The measure has drawn objections from publicly traded companies, with 67 opposing the tokenization of their shares on the OKXICE platform, according to a Nov. 5 public notice.

Johnson also cited concerns about Anti-Money Laundering and Know Your Customer compliance, noting that objections to the exemption extended beyond investor protection.

Levine, meanwhile, said he had not closely studied the innovation exemption but understood it to be time-limited and include investor protections. He emphasized that Congress had authorized the SEC to grant exemptions from federal securities laws when doing so serves the public interest and remains consistent with investor protection requirements.

He pointed to exchange-traded funds as an example of financial innovation that initially developed through regulatory exemptions before becoming subject to broader agency rules.

Related: Robinhood’s Tenev warns US tokenization uncertainty deters ‘serious assets’

If confirmed, Johnson would become the SEC’s only Democratic commissioner, a development some in the crypto industry believe could strengthen the agency’s approach to regulation.

“Crypto isn’t a partisan issue, and the rules that come out of a bipartisan commission will stand on firmer footing,” Jessica Martinez, US policy director at digital asset infrastructure company Fireblocks, told Cointelegraph.

Martinez added that even minority commissioners can influence policy by challenging proposals and pushing for unresolved issues to be addressed before rules are finalized.

Senators have until noon Friday to submit additional written questions to the nominees, with responses due by 5:00 pm ET on Nov. 24, according to the committee’s closing remarks.

The nominations could then advance to a committee vote before being considered by the full Senate. Both nominees would require Senate confirmation before taking their seats at the SEC.

Magazine: How to preserve wealth and access funds during a national emergency

#4924. This 108-Year-Old’s Daily Routine Includes 2 Easy Leg Exercises You Can Do at Home

Susan Young Browne, who still lives independently, begins every morning with this movement.

Earlier this year, Susan Young Browne from Delaware celebrated her 108th birthday—the news of which travelled across the world. Everyone wanted to know the retired teacher’s longevity secrets. How did Susan, who had just renewed her driving license until 2033, retain such a good quality of life?

Well, movement, it seems, has played an integral part. Firstly, Susan, who still walks unassisted, told TODAY that she continues to attend chair dancing classes at a local community centre.

But she’s also committed to consistent, everyday movement.

Susan begins every morning with a 10-minute stretching, strengthening, and balancing routine.

With flexibility, mobility, strength and balance all being key markers of longevity, Susan’s routine is a reminder that workouts do not need to be long and complicated to have far-reaching benefits.

Another key part of Susan’s daily exercise routine that likely contributes to her impressive healthspan (the number of years spent living in good health and free from chronic disease)? Her focus on leg strength.

“I get up and stand against the wall and exercise my legs. I make sure my legs are exercised,” Susan told TODAY.

Not only do strong legs help us move freely and reduce fall risk, but a growing body of evidence also points to an association between lower-body strength and cognitive function—another key element of longevity.

One oft-cited study found that older adults with greater leg strength had 34% lower odds of low cognitive function than those with weaker legs. Meanwhile, a 10-year study of more than 300 twin sisters reported “a striking protective relationship” between leg power and cognition. The twin with greater leg power at the beginning of the study maintained their cognitive function better and showed fewer age-related brain changes over the following decade.

While the exact mechanisms behind this association remain unclear, among scientists’ hypotheses are that stronger muscles may improve blood flow to the brain, that resistance training may influence nervous system function, and that lower-body strength can help preserve walking speed and mobility—both of which have been associated with a lower risk of dementia.

Using a wall for support, Susan can be seen balancing on one leg, which is a simple, effective, single-leg exercise that supports strength and balance.

Another simple move that only requires a wall? As the name suggests, a wall sit.

“Defined as an isometric exercise—an exercise where the working muscle doesn’t change length during the exercise and the joint doesn’t move—wall sits are super safe to perform, and put very little stress on the spine,” personal trainer Aimee Victoria Long previously explained to Women’s Health.

Mainly targeting the quadriceps (the muscles at the front of your thigh that support knee stability and everyday functional movements such as walking, getting up from a chair, and climbing stairs), wall sits can also improve endurance, balance, core, and lower-back strength.

Another simple, no-equipment exercise that supports functional lower-body strength is the humble bodyweight squat. Plus, one study even found that doing 60 seconds of squatting exercises during long periods of sitting may help preserve cognitive function.

Hannah Bradfield is a Senior Health and Fitness Writer for Women’s Health UK. An NCTJ-accredited journalist, Hannah graduated from Loughborough University with a BA in English and Sport Science and an MA in Media and Cultural Analysis.  She has been covering sports, health and fitness for the last five years and has created content for outlets including BBC Sport, BBC Sounds, Runner’s World and Stylist. She especially enjoys interviewing those working within the community to improve access to sport, exercise and wellness. Hannah is a 2024 John Schofield Trust Fellow and was also named a 2022 Rising Star in Journalism by The Printing Charity.  A keen runner, Hannah was firmly a sprinter growing up (also dabbling in long jump) but has since transitioned to longer-distance running. While 10K is her favoured race distance, she loves running or volunteering at parkrun every Saturday, followed, of course, by pastries. She’s always looking for fun new runs and races to do and brunch spots to try.

#4918. BYD is launching this 5-seat luxury electric SUV next week with over 1,000 km of range

BYD’s luxury brand, Denza, calls it “the king of large 5-seater pure electric vehicles.” The new Denza N8 electric SUV will launch next week, offering an impressive CLTC driving range of over 1,000 km (621 miles), massive digital screens, and plenty of premium extras.

After opening pre-orders for the new 5-seat luxury electric SUV last week, Li Hui, Denza’s general manager, said the results so far “have been excellent.”

According to Li, the Denza N8 is “definitely the most anticipated luxury SUV of the year.” Using BYD’s second-generation Blade Batteries, the Denza N8 EV has a pure electric CLTC range of up to 1,003 km (623 miles) on a single charge.

That’s just as much as the N8 plug-in hybrid (PHEV) it’s replacing, which offered a combined CLTC range of 1,003 km (623 miles) on a full tank of gas and a full charge.

While Li confirmed last week that the new EV variant would go on sale in November, he didn’t give a specific date.

Now, it’s official. Denza announced on its Weibo page on Friday that the launch event for the Denza N8 EV will be held on November 12, 2026.

BYD is still offering a limited-time promo that includes a 5,000 RMB credit for those who place a reservation with a 2,000 RMB ($200) deposit.

The Denza N8 measures 5,150 mm long, 1,999 mm wide, and 1,820 mm high, with a 3,075 mm wheelbase. That’s slightly longer than the Kia EV9 and Hyundai IONIQ 9.

It’s available with a single rear-wheel-drive (RWD) or a tri-motor all-wheel-drive (AWD) powertrain. The RWD version uses a single motor producing either 320 kW (429 hp) or 370 kW (496 hp).

The tri-motor Denza N8 uses BYD’s e3 platform. The setup includes three electric motors: a 270 kW (362 hp) front motor and two 310 kW (416 hp) rear motors, for a combined output of 890 kW (1,193 hp).

Regulatory filings earlier this year revealed it will use either a 130.15 kWh or 105.79 kWh BYD Blade 2.0 battery, offering a CLTC range of up to 1,003 km (623 miles) and 800 km (497 miles), respectively.

The Denza N8 also features BYD’s latest Flash Charging technology, letting it recharge from 10% to 80% in just five minutes. It takes just nine minutes to go from 10% to 97%.

Inside, the five-seat electric SUV features a massive 30″ infotainment screen that spans nearly the entire passenger-side dashboard, plus a new 43″ (1.1 meter) panoramic head-up display (PHUD) at the bottom of the windshield.

Like most of its new luxury vehicles, the Denza N8 is loaded with BYD’s latest software and technology.

Added premium features include zero-gravity seating in all rows, a three-spoke steering wheel, wireless phone chargers, and ambient lighting.

The electric SUV is equipped with a roof-mounted LiDAR to support BYD’s God’s Eye 5.0 intelligent driving assistance system, plus its DiSus-A intelligent air suspension system.

BYD has yet to announce prices for the Denza N8 EV, but it will likely be slightly less than the N8L, an extended version that starts at 299,800 RMB ($45,000).

#4915. EVgo opens its first Tesla Superchargers, with hundreds more planned

EVgo has opened its first Supercharger station, and it’s in a Chicago grocery store parking lot. The site has 12 fast-charging stalls with both NACS and CCS plugs, and it’s the first of hundreds of Tesla Supercharger stalls EVgo plans to add across the US.

The 325 kW “EVgo Superchargers” are at Riverside Square & River’s Edge, at 3145 S Ashland Avenue. EVgo’s August announcement cited up to 500 kW for the Supercharger rollout.

The Chicago location features Magic Dock technology, which means both NACS and CCS vehicles can charge without an adapter. Drivers of compatible non-Tesla EVs can start a session through the Tesla app. If you drive a Tesla, the charging station shows up in the car’s navigation and Trip Planner. Other EVgo fast chargers with NACS plugs have been showing up in Tesla navigation since early September if you turn on third-party charging.

“By serving both Tesla and non-Tesla NACS drivers, we’re doubling EVgo’s addressable market and continuing to meet EV drivers where they are and where they want to charge,” said Badar Khan, EVgo CEO.

EVgo owns the Superchargers and self-brands them, and Tesla runs and maintains them. The first sites will be at Regency Centers shopping plazas with grocery stores.

Smart move on EVGo's part. More reliable charger at a lower cost than they can build themselves, and Tesla takes on all the maintenance and network operations for a slice of the sales.

EVgo is also adding NACS plugs to its regular fast-charging network, a rollout it started in 2025. The company aims to have 500 of those stalls across the US by the end of 2026, with Supercharger stalls counted separately. Over the next two years, it plans to put at least one NACS plug at every EVgo site built in 2023 or later.

#4912. AD’s Editor Picks: Gifts for Friends and Family Who Love Design

The holiday season is fast approaching, and we’ve rounded up our picks for the best gifts for friends and family with proclivities for great design—including the picky cousin who couldn’t decide on a sofa for months, your wellness-obsessed mother-in-law, the best friend who loves to host, and everyone in between. No matter what winter has in store, we’re committed to making sure you come out smelling as fresh as pine this gifting season. So to relieve some stress and cut out the guesswork, we’ve rounded up 29 AD editor-approved options that’ll show them how much they mean to you (or that might make a nice treat for yourself).

“Ever since I visited Milan this year, I have craved a cool way to serve up aperitivos stateside,” says senior shopping editor Nashia Baker. The Porta Natalia Criado Astrid Set—handcrafted in Italy—brings a creative edge to the senior editor’s cocktail hours, “all thanks to the geometric food bowls and sliding quartz stones that feel sophisticated yet playful.”

Shopping writer Julia Harrison “hemmed and hawed over these knives for two years” before finally pulling the trigger. She says there’s no other knife like it on the market. “Its craftsmanship speaks for itself, but the signature stripe handle does so much for an otherwise spiritless kitchen,” she says.

“Ninety percent of the meals I ate at home over the past year were eaten out of an Everyday Bowl,” admits commerce editor Audrey Lee. She likes that the design is a nice medium between a traditional bowl and a plate, and how the color options look stacked on her dinnerware shelf. “It’s also a gift that appeals to anyone who’s more picky about the plate than what goes on it.”

Did you know that AD has teamed up with craft supply superstore Michael's on a line of picture frames? Choose your own design adventure by opting for a sleek modernist, ornate maximalist, or vintage luxury-inspired moulding. “Custom framing a photo with sentimental value, like a family pic or a snap from a memorial trip, makes a great gift,” says AD commerce lead editor Abbey Stone.

“The vice grip that Sophie Lou Jacobsen's glassware has on me!” says Stone. The NYC-based designer masterfully imbues her pretty pieces with whimsy, making them the perfect addition for any open shelving or bar cart.

“Aesop is my answer to a foolproof gift for pretty much anyone,” says Lee, who’s given the brand’s sets to family members and friends both in and out of the holiday season. “And for anyone reading *wink, wink*, my personal favorite is any variant of the rind concentrate scent.”

This notepad is perfect for writing down any and all to-dos, to-buys, and to-remembers—exactly what Harrison intends for it. “This bedside notepad keeps me off my phone and away from blue light while I’m winding down,” she says. “It also makes my nightstand very literary-looking in the cold light of morning.”

Anyone wanting to buy a foolproof gift for commerce producer Lauren Arzbaecher should head to Anthropologie. “Call me basic, but I never tire of this glassware that regularly makes the rounds on TikTok,” she says. “The colored icons add a sense of personality to a daily drink—I’ve collected the croissant and lemon patterns so far.”

All your gift recipient will have to do is strike a match and light the wick to make them feel like they’re cruising down Highway 1. “Earthy and light, this Big Sur candle instantly transports me to the west coast with notes of fresh eucalyptus and magnolia,” says Baker.

Baker calls these sculptural martini glasses “the cherry on top” in her bar cabinet. The cheery pink-and-blue color palette transforms toasts into something more tasteful, and the little cherry on the stem is actually helpful for keeping your hand steady when raising the glass—“a true win if you’re clumsy like me,” says Baker.

“Certainly a splurge gift, though I think worth it for a piece of Lenny Kravitz’s design genius,” says Arzbaecher. This lamp from his recent CB2 collection takes all the airiness of a Noguchi paper lantern and adds a jolt of chrome and marble for something seriously sexy.

This tea library unfolds like a book, filled with green, black and oolong tea varieties, a warming gift for cozy nights in. “Every night my roommates and I exchange glances that mean: time for our nightly cup,” says Harrison. “We’ve yet to try one that we aren’t enamored with.”

Explore 1,000 of the world’s most thoughtfully designed objects with this eye-catching coffee table book from Phaidon. A great gift for any design enthusiast, this visually compelling read is filled with beautiful photography and interesting anecdotes about some of our favorite pieces from the Eamese, Alvar and Aino Aalto, Isamu Noguchi, and more.

“This Japanese-designed sewing machine strays away from utilitarian-looking models, so I don’t feel like I have to hide it away,” says Lee. The shopping editor also notices that the petite machine is more compact than previous ones she’s sewn with, but it’s still well-equipped to handle hemming her too-long skirts and other low-maintenance projects.

These espresso cups will be a bright spot in their caffeine routine. Each set is handmade by artist Florence Girod at her workshop in the south of France, so no two are the same.

We found the perfect dish to match her Van Cleef alhambra from Anastasio Home. It’s made of solid marble and is the perfect way to display her everyday jewelry.

This robe from Lake makes Harrison “feel like a hardworking mother of three living in a Pacific Northwest logging town, drinking black coffee all day long, and feeding my children baked beans”—a version of herself she’s aspiring to one day.

Pajamas discreet enough to be worn during sunlight hours will be the gift that keeps on giving. Harrison called this Olivia Von Halle set “impossibly high-quality.” While it’s most definitely a splurge, she insists they feel “like a million silk worms made it just for you.”

Mossy hues are an evergreen (pun intended) color scheme in Arzbaecher’s home. “This quilt adds tranquility to my bedscape. Plus, it’s just weighty enough to serve throughout multiple seasons.” It’s an ideal present for the friend that’s perpetually cold—or maybe even a nice winter bedding upgrade for yourself.

“These sheets feel as close to my aspirational vibe as an object can get—vintage, bohemian, heirloom quality,” says Harrison. “I want everything from this brand.” Made and embroidered in Jaipur using heritage techniques, it’ll feel like you’re being gifted an heirloom in the making.

Rest easy knowing that anyone who receives this breezy sheet set with cooling eucalyptus fibers will wake up on the right side of the bed. “The cool-to-the-touch nature of the material is ideal for hot sleepers, so I found the experience very nice,” tester Zoë Sessums told us.

“The Clementine Sleep mask’s pillowy soft material leaves me feeling refreshed by morning,” says Baker, so it’s a thoughtful gift for anyone who enjoys more luxurious sleep. She notes that it filters out light in her sunny bedroom “so sleeping in is the only thing on my mind.”

Ezcaray’s Matisse scarf feels like a fluffy, mini version of the wool throw blanket Baker curls up with on snow days. “I’ve survived too many NYC winters to underestimate the power of a good scarf,” she says. “This one is warm, beautifully designed, and the first thing I think about gifting friends who struggle when there’s a chill in the air.”

“I bumped into a friend who was modeling this sweater at the Eckhaus Latta x J.Crew party last fashion week and was instantly smitten,” says Lee. It’s made of 100% wool to stay cozy in the coldest winter temps, and the colorful palette makes it suit pretty much anything else in your wardrobe. “Be prepared to get stopped a million times from friends and strangers wanting to know where it’s from,” she warns.

These mules add print to a plain T-shirt and jeans outfit without overwhelming it. “Unlike other mules I’ve tried, these don’t seem to have the issue of flying off my foot with every step,” adds Arzbaecher. While AD’s producer is partial to the calf-hair version, there are also lots of suede colorways for a sleek alternative.

Lee reaches for this necklace-scarf hybrid from Kara Yoo at least once a week, since it can add flair to a basic sweater or looks sleek peeping out from a collared shirt. “The pendant can hang from different lengths, or you can loop the silk cord through just one side, so it feels like a different accessory every time,” she says.

The Petit Moyen Messenger from Clare V. has a permanent place in Lee’s packing list because of its versatile shape that can easily fit in overstuffed luggage. “Unlike some of my other bags, I don’t need to stuff my Moyen with my socks and T-shirts to keep its shape in my carry on,” she says. “It comes in many solid-colored options, but I’m partial to the two-tone checkerboard woven designs that have dimension.”

“Don’t sleep on Quince’s cashmere wearables,” says Lee. She told us this quarter zip looks like it could cost double its price tag—and who doesn’t like to get a little cashmere for Christmas? “The jewel tone-enthusiast in me is drawn to burgundy and storm blue, but truffle brown and ivory are great gift ideas for minimalists, and classic additions to any wardrobe,” she says.

Lee’s holiday wishlist also consists of silver or white gold charms that she can use to accessorize Mejuri’s chain necklace—a perfect gift in and of itself for jewelry lovers. “I was given this necklace earlier in the year and wear it multiple times a week. Since it’s easy to attach charms and pendants to the timeless silhouette, it feels like 100 necklaces in one.”

Lee can “hardly think of an occasion where a pair of these classic Pleats Please trousers by Issey Miyake aren’t appropriate.” She says they’re the perfect buy for the effortlessly cool, quiet luxury crowd since she’s worn them to formal events, to the office, and “on busy errand days with a T-shirt and sneakers—they’re that comfortable.”

With a unique open-ear silhouette, the nwm Ones challenge traditional headphone design using Sound Zone Technology that allows for complete auditory awareness—meaning your gift recipient can hear their own voice and surroundings while still listening to music. “These were hands-down my go-to headphones during the summer because they’re so breathable,” says Lee. She added that “it’s also really nice being able to hear your surroundings, especially if you live in a place like NYC.”

#4911. The 6 Best Pillows of 2026, Tested by Editors

The $190 Purple Harmony Pillow might be divisive, but it earned our best overall badge.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

As someone with frequent neck pain, I would give up my cozy sheets, white noise machine, and sunrise alarm clock if it meant I wouldn’t wake up with a stiff neck. (And that’s coming from someone who invests a lot in their sleep.)

"I think that a pillow can make a big difference in people that have a history of neck issues," says Leia Rispoli, MD, an interventional pain management specialist and physiatrist at DISC Sport & Spine Center. This is the very reason why I tend to lug my favorite pillow around on weekend trips.

There isn’t one pillow that works for everyone. Your best option depends on your sleep position, preferred firmness, body type, and whether you tend to overheat at night. To find the best pillows, our editors and testers slept on popular models from Purple, Casper, Coop, Saatva, and other leading sleep brands. We evaluated each one for comfort, support, temperature regulation, adjustability, ease of care, and value.

I love the Purple Harmony Pillow so much that I try to convert nearly everyone who mentions sleep within earshot of me. I even pack it for overnight trips. But it does feel unusual at first. Inside, a springy, ventilated latex core is wrapped in Purple’s GelFlex Grid, a flexible, honeycomb-shaped layer that bends under the weight of your head and springs back when you move. Together, these materials make the pillow feel buoyant rather than sink-in like memory foam.

I tested the medium, 6.5-inch loft, meaning the pillow’s height before it is compressed. It provided enough height to support my head and neck while I slept on my side without feeling rigid. The materials give slightly beneath heavier areas, which helped reduce uncomfortable pressure around my head and neck. As someone who frequently experiences neck and back pain, I consistently felt comfortable and supported while sleeping on it.

Purple recommends the low, 5.5-inch version for most stomach and back sleepers. The medium and tall versions are generally better suited to side and combination sleepers, who often need more height to fill the space between the head and mattress. Body size and personal preference will also affect which height feels most comfortable.

The Harmony was also one of the coolest pillows I tested. Air circulates through the ventilated latex and open honeycomb layer instead of becoming trapped inside a solid block of foam. Its stretchy outer cover is also designed to draw sweat away from the skin so it can evaporate. Even on warmer nights, I rarely felt the need to flip the pillow over in search of a cooler surface.

After nearly three years of regular use, my pillow has not developed noticeable lumps or gone flat. Its durable build helps justify the high price, but there are compromises. The pillow is unusually heavy, and some sleepers may notice the grid’s texture through the cover. Purple’s 30-night trial is also shorter than the trial periods offered by many competing brands.

Still, no other pillow I tested delivered the same combination of responsive support, cooling, and long-term shape retention. I would purchase it again without hesitation.

Women’s Health art director Christine Giordano typically sleeps with two or three pillows, but the Casper Original gave her enough support to use fewer. “This pillow gave me the support I needed while still feeling light and airy,” she says. “It was breathable and didn’t clump, keeping me cool and comfortable at night.”

The Casper uses a pillow-within-a-pillow design: A firmer inner cushion provides structure, while a softer outer layer adds cushioning. Both layers are filled with polyester microfiber, a synthetic material made to feel light and fluffy like down. The construction prevented Giordano’s head from sinking too deeply while still giving the pillow a plush feel.

Giordano tries to sleep on her back but usually shifts onto her left side during the night. The pillow remained comfortable and supportive in both positions, making it a strong choice for combination sleepers who alternate between their backs and sides. However, stomach sleepers or people who prefer a flatter pillow may find its approximately nine-inch uncompressed height too lofty.

The breathable cotton cover and airy synthetic fill also prevented the pillow from feeling stuffy during testing. It’s not as breathable as a dedicated cooling pillow, but Giordano did not notice heat buildup or need to flip it over for a cooler surface.

The entire pillow is machine-washable, which makes it easier to clean than models with foam or latex cores. Its soft filling may need occasional fluffing to maintain its shape, but Giordano did not notice clumping during testing.

At under $100, it is one of the more affordable pillows we tested. Giordano liked it enough that she replaced some of the other pillows in her home and purchased additional Casper ones for her family.

Women’s Health executive health and fitness director Jacqueline Andriakos, CPT, calls the Coop Original Adjustable the most comfortable pillow she has tried. “I’ve never been a pillow snob or had major issues with basic pillows from Amazon, but I noticed that this was far more comfortable than other, cheaper pillows I’ve used,” she says.

The pillow is filled with a blend of shredded memory foam and microfiber. Memory foam molds around the head and neck, while the soft synthetic fibers keep the pillow from feeling like a dense, solid block of foam. Unlike a pillow with a fixed height, the Coop can be unzipped so you can remove filling for a flatter feel or add more for extra support. The process can get messy, but that adjustability makes it easier to tailor the pillow to your body and sleep position.

Coop offers the pillow in classic, crescent, and cut-out shapes. Andriakos tested the classic and cut-out versions while switching between side and stomach sleeping. She preferred the cut-out, which has a curved indentation that creates room for the shoulder and allows the pillow to sit closer to the neck.

“I liked that it could wrap around my neck and shoulders, and I could almost cuddle it like a body pillow with an arm threaded underneath,” she says. Although the shape can look slightly awkward inside a standard pillowcase, it was super comfortable when she slept on her side.

The pillow felt plush, but Andriakos’ head did not sink so deeply that her neck felt unsupported. She also found the outer cover breathable, although sleepers who regularly overheat may prefer a pillow specifically designed for cooling.

Because its height can be adjusted, the Coop can work for side, back, stomach, and combination sleepers. Side sleepers will generally need more filling to keep the head level with the spine, while stomach sleepers may prefer to remove enough filling to create a flatter surface. Finding the right amount may require a few nights of experimentation, but Coop’s 100-night trial gives you time to make those adjustments.

It would take an exceptional pillow for me to consider replacing my Purple Harmony, but the Saatva Latex Pillow gave it serious competition. Although both pillows contain latex, they feel noticeably different. Purple uses one solid, springy latex core, while Saatva uses shredded pieces of latex surrounded by a soft layer of fine synthetic fibers. This gives the Saatva a plusher, more traditional feel, but it has enough structure to support the head and neck.

The pillow feels dense when you first pick it up, but my head settled gently into the soft outer layer without sinking too deeply. The shredded latex inside quickly returned to its original shape when I moved, so the pillow stayed supportive as I shifted positions. As a side sleeper who frequently experiences neck pain, I did not wake up with additional stiffness.

Saatva offers two heights. The standard version measures approximately four to five inches and is designed for back and stomach sleepers who generally need a lower pillow. The high-loft version measures approximately six to seven inches and is better suited to side and combination sleepers who need more height between their heads and mattresses.

I also appreciated that the pillow remained breathable throughout the night. The small spaces between the pieces of shredded latex allow air to circulate, while the cotton cover feels cool. It does not create the immediately cold sensation of a cooling pillow, but I did not notice uncomfortable heat buildup.

The outer layer is filled with microdenier fibers, which are very fine synthetic strands designed to imitate the light, fluffy feel of down. You can machine-wash this outer layer and its cotton cover, but the inner latex core must be removed and kept out of the washing machine.

At $165 for a standard/queen size, this is one of the more expensive pillows we tested. Saatva also provides only 45 days for returns, which is shorter than the trial periods offered by several competitors. Still, its combination of plush cushioning, responsive support, and breathable materials makes it an excellent choice for sleepers who want a luxury-hotel feel that’s still supportive.

For hot sleepers, a breathable pillow can be the difference between a good night’s sleep and a night spent tossing and turning. The Slumber Cloud UltraCool stood out because its cooling effect was immediately noticeable during testing.

Women’s Health managing editor Laura McLaughlin described the sensation as “a little sci-fi” because the surface felt immediately cool when she rested her head on it. The cover contains Outlast, a material designed to absorb excess body heat when you become warm and release it as your skin cools. This helps prevent heat from collecting around your head and face, although the effect may be less noticeable beneath a thick pillowcase.

McLaughlin also found the pillow easy to combine with others. “It’s great for layering with other pillows to get just the right height,” she says. “It’s neither too dense nor too high to use in combination with others already in place.” That makes it especially useful for sleepers who want additional height without placing a rigid pillow beneath their heads.

The interior is filled with polyester down alternative, a synthetic material designed to imitate the airy feel of down. It is available in soft/medium and medium/firm versions, allowing sleepers to choose how plush or structured they want the pillow to feel. The softer option will generally suit stomach sleepers or anyone who prefers more sink while the firmer version may provide better support for back and side sleepers.

The entire pillow can be machine-washed, a convenient advantage over pillows with foam or latex cores. However, synthetic filling can begin to clump after repeated use and washing, so it may require regular fluffing to redistribute the material.

Although the UltraCool does not provide the customized support of an adjustable pillow, it delivered the most noticeable cooling sensation of any model we tested.

The Brooklinen Down Alternative Pillow is built for sleepers who want the fluffy feel of down without actual feathers. It’s filled with recycled poly microgel, a cluster of fine synthetic fibers that creates a light cushioning similar to down.

Unlike many pillows that come in only one firmness, this one is available in soft, medium, and firm versions. Brooklinen recommends the soft pillow for stomach sleepers who generally need less height beneath the head; the medium for back sleepers; and the firm for side sleepers who typically need more support to fill the space between the head and mattress.

Its cotton sateen shell feels smooth and has a subtle sheen. The pillow is also certified by OEKO-TEX Standard 100, which means its materials have been tested for a list of regulated substances. That certification does not evaluate the pillow’s comfort, durability, or environmental impact.

Brooklinen allows most products to be returned within 365 days, although it deducts a $9.95 return fee from the refund. That’s one of the most generous return windows we’ve seen.

The pillow can be machine-washed in cold water and tumble-dried on low with wool dryer balls until completely dry. As with most pillows filled with loose synthetic fibers, it may require regular fluffing to redistribute the fill and preserve its shape.

A pillow’s height, or loft, should keep your head and neck aligned with your spine. Side sleepers generally need a taller pillow, back sleepers a medium-height pillow, and stomach sleepers a low pillow. Your mattress matters, too: The more deeply your shoulders sink into it, the less pillow height you may need. Most of the pillows we tested fell in the five- to nine-inch range, offering different levels of support depending on their material and sleeping position.

Side sleepers often benefit from a medium-firm or firm pillow that maintains its height. Back sleepers generally need medium support while stomach sleepers may prefer something softer that compresses easily. Because firmness labels vary by brand, consider the pillow’s material and height as well.

“There isn't one brand that's the magical brand for everyone,” says Rispoli. Look for a pillow that supports your head without tilting your neck sharply upward or downward. Adjustable pillows can help you fine-tune the height. Consult a health care professional if your pain is persistent, severe, or accompanied by numbness or weakness.

Memory foam molds around the head but may retain heat. Latex feels springy, breathable, and durable. Down is soft and airy but may lack structure while synthetic down alternative is usually easier to wash but may flatten over time. Hybrid pillows combine materials to balance cushioning and support.

Look for ventilated latex, shredded filling, or another construction that allows air to circulate. Some covers also absorb excess body heat or feel cool, although a thick pillowcase may reduce the effect. People who sleep cold may prefer denser foam or plush down filling that holds slightly more warmth.

As a sleep writer, I’ve tested all kinds of sleep accessories, from mattresses to eye masks, but pillows are probably my most-tested product category. (Unfortunately, I once tested so many pillows at the same time that I injured my neck, but I’ve learned my lesson.) To find the best pillows available online, I selected popular picks from leading sleep brands, like Purple, Casper, Saatva, Pluto, and more, for testing. Some helpful testers and I have slept on these pillows for dozens of nights to determine whether they’re worth the money, and our top pick has been my favorite pillow to sleep on for nearly three years. From flat pillow lovers to hot sleepers, our diverse crew of sleepers tried out eight pillows of various styles, materials, and price points.

He started his magazine career as an accessories assistant at Vogue, and has since held editorial roles at Tatler Asia and The Manual. When he’s not online shopping, you can find him on long walks with his two corgis.

#4908. 15 Airbnbs With Indoor Pools & Jacuzzis (2026): Top Summer Escapes

Airbnbs with indoor pools (especially the heated kind) are the kinds of “needle in a haystack” rentals that can save a vacation from feeling completely miserable in the coldest months of the year, or heighten the excitement of the enclave you’ve secured for a week of summer bliss. And though many of your standard Airbnb rentals will offer guests some generous amenities—a plush bed, a functional kitchen, and a high-pressure shower immediately come to mind—only a very select few allow you to book a pool that’s all yours (not shared, or fought over, by a bunch of resort guests). Better yet: There’s no “pool closes at 9 p.m.” signs.

But here’s the catch: The gap between a great indoor-pool rental and a truly tragic one is vast. We’re talking sleek, glass-wrapped sanctuaries on one end—and, on the other, a sad, chlorinated afterthought hiding in a basement with fluorescent lighting and 1994 motel energy. The good ones, though, have the power to allow you to lock in your vacation and tune everything else out. From a cedar-lined pool room with views of the surrounding forest to modern desert escapes where you can watch the sunset turn the surrounding mountains pink at sunset from your floaty, these pools make for an incredible retreat year-round, weather be damned.

Perks: Turkish steam bath, sauna, massage room, private chef, butler service, private backyard, security system

If your ideal vacation involves frescoed ceilings, crystal chandeliers, and a butler to fetch you juice that’s been freshly squeezed from the garden's citrus trees, Villa Dorata in Positano delivers. This five-bedroom Baroque-style villa is gloriously over the top, with jewel-toned interiors, gilded art, elaborate tilework, and terraces overlooking the Amalfi Coast. The private spa is an attraction in its own right, complete with an indoor swimming pool, Turkish steam bath, and massage room. Outside, lush gardens, fountains, and citrus groves provide plenty of places to enjoy the Mediterranean sunshine. There are even two traditional Italian pizza ovens for alfresco dinners. Daily breakfast, housekeeping, and butler service are included, so guests can spend less time managing the logistics of a group vacation and more time enjoying the villa's considerable extravagance. From $5,078 per night.

Perks: Jetted bathtub, private backyard, fire pit, outdoor furniture, pet-friendly, security system, free parking

Swimming season never ends at this three-bedroom home outside Binghamton, New York. The heated indoor pool occupies its own glass-lined space, supplying views of the surrounding trees and simulating the feeling of being outdoors. The rest of the house features vaulted ceilings, bamboo floors, and a contemporary mix of furnishings. Floor-to-ceiling windows let in plenty of natural light into the main living areas, and an expansive deck extends the entertaining space outdoors. There's also a balcony for morning coffee, a propane fire pit for chilly evenings, and a jetted soaking tub in the primary suite. With room for eight guests and a pet-friendly policy, it's a practical choice for a group getaway, particularly when the weather makes an outdoor pool considerably less appealing. From $425 per night.

Perks: Soaking tub, sauna, two fireplaces, private deck

The Catskills have no shortage of cabins with fireplaces and woodland views, but few come with their own indoor swimming pool. This four-bedroom getaway near Callicoon has all three, plus an infrared sauna for the ultimate wellness-centric stay. Cathedral ceilings, exposed timber, and two wood-burning fireplaces give the interiors a classic mountain house look, while oversized windows keep the surrounding three acres of forest in view. Outside, a path leads down to Callicoon Creek, and a generously-sized deck and fire pit make the most of the secluded setting. The house sleeps 10, so there's room to bring a crowd without having to share a hotel pool with strangers. From $410 per night.

Perks: Hot tub, spa deck, sun patio, grilling area, gourmet kitchen

There are indoor pools, and then there are homes built entirely around them. This sprawling five-bedroom retreat centers on a private heated mineral pool and hot tub housed in a dedicated spa space, turning a weekend getaway into something closer to a wellness retreat. Elsewhere, the home feels equal parts time capsule and thoughtful renovation. Original details—including a vintage intercom system, wood-clad ceilings, and dramatic stonework—sit alongside updated bathrooms and an eclectic mix of vintage and contemporary furnishings. Thoughtful extras—from a grand piano and art supplies to a gourmet kitchen and eight smart TVs—make it particularly appealing for multigenerational gatherings. From $945 per night

Perks: Indoor fireplace, private patio, dedicated parking, game room, wine cellar

From the outside, this townhouse looks like a regular Denver-area home. Inside, however, it’s organized around one genuinely unexpected element: a full-size indoor pool set beneath a ceiling of skylights and surrounded by greenery. A fireplace just off the pool deck gives swimmers a place to warm up between dips (or flips off the diving board). The rest of the house is built for groups who plan to stay put. Exposed brick and wood-paneled walls give the interiors a grounded, slightly retro feel, while oversized bedrooms and bathrooms make it comfortable for longer stays rather than quick weekends. A wine cellar and wet bar sit near the pool room, clearly intended for nights that stretch on, and a separate game room—complete with a pool table and shuffleboard—adds another place to gather. From $726 per night.

Perks: BBQ, fire pit, heated indoor pool, washer and dryer

The Invisible House, with its mirror-clad façade, is a well-known landmark in its area of the Joshua Tree Desert. Designed by architect Tomas Osinski, the home quite literally reflects its surroundings while an open plan setup connects one end of the space to the other—encompassing a lengthy 100-foot indoor pool, four bedrooms, and common areas dotted with modern furnishings from the likes of Piero Lissoni and Pierre Paulin. Staying here ensures a prime place for trekking out on hikes, while indoors you can take in wraparound views of the landscape while you swim laps in the deep end. From $2,333 per night.

Perks: Two game rooms, multiple smart TVs, fire pit, disc golf, covered balcony

Perks: Fireplace, breakfast nook, porch, farm animals

For a pastoral adventure with some thoroughly modern comforts, book this inn located on a farm 45 minutes out from Portland, Maine. Here, you’ll be occupying the cozy second floor suite, which offers a living room with a fireplace on cold winter nights and polished sleeping quarters. Since the inn sits on a working farm, you can also commune with the cute sheep and goats on the property, which spans 100 acres for walking and exploring the local fields. From $183 per night.

Perks: Game room, fireplaces, furnished deck, fire pit, movie projector

If your idea of a perfect mountain getaway involves never hearing the words "I'm bored," this Poconos rental has you covered. The four-bedroom home pairs a private heated indoor pool and spa with a long list of rainy-day diversions, including a dedicated game room stocked with foosball, ping-pong, shuffleboard, and a poker table, plus a media room with surround sound and an Xbox. Upstairs, the vibe is bright and contemporary, with an open-plan living area, two gas fireplaces, and a renovated kitchen anchored by an oversized dining table that seats 12. When the weather cooperates, the fun spills outdoors, where you'll find two furnished decks, a fire pit, and access to the community's lake, sports courts, and seasonal pools. From $900 per night

Perks: Mountain views, electric car charger, laundry

Colorado is a state known for its epic ski resorts and wintery locales, but in the city of Colorado Springs, all kinds of adventures await when you book this Southwestern-style home that feels all but plucked out of the desert. The jewel of the home is its long stretch of indoor pool that heats up to 90 degrees Fahrenheit so you can break a sweat even when it’s in the single digits outside. Spread across multiple levels that include plenty of terraces for enjoying the great outdoors, you can also get up and close and personal with nature on any of the surrounding hiking trails (including the ones that snake through Pulpit Rock Park and Garden of the Gods). #4904. Hyundai’s $359 IONIQ 5 lease looks like a deal, but don’t overlook its bigger sibling

The Hyundai IONIQ 5 is the most popular EV in America outside of Tesla, but its larger three-row sibling looks like the better deal right now.

Through September, the Hyundai IONIQ 5 was the third top-selling EV in the US, behind the Tesla Model Y and Model 3.

Hyundai’s electric SUV is outselling rivals like the Chevy Equinox EV, Ford Mustang Mach-E, and Toyota bZ.

Why? For one, it’s one of the most affordable EVs in the US, starting at about $35,000, and Hyundai has been offering some of the most attractive lease deals.

After the $7,500 federal EV tax credit expired at the end of September last year, Hyundai responded by reducing prices on the IONIQ 5 by up to nearly $10,000 on some trims.

The 2027 Hyundai IONIQ 5 starts at $35,250. That’s for the standard-range IONIQ 5 SE with an EPA-estimated range of up to 245 miles. Upgrading to the extended-range SE RWD model adds $2,500, starting at $37,750, and offers up to 318 miles of range.

Hyundai’s three-row electric SUV, the IONIQ 9, starts at $58,955, or nearly $24,000 more than the base IONIQ 5 SE.

On Hyundai’s website, the 2027 IONIQ 5 SE Standard Range is available to lease from $359 a month. The offer is for a 24-month lease with $4,999 due at signing for an effective cost of $567 per month.

The extended-range Hyundai IONIQ 5 SE RWD can be leased from $369 a month. The offer is also a 24-month lease with $4,999 due at signing, for an effective cost of $577 per month.

Hyundai’s larger IONIQ 9, meanwhile, is available to lease from $419 a month. That deal also includes $4,999 due at signing, but is for a 36-month lease, so the effective cost is $557 per month.

Using Hyundai’s payment calculator, it would cost about $412 a month to lease the 2027 Hyundai IONIQ 5 SE for 36 months with $4,999 due at signing. With an effective cost of $550 per month, it’s only $7 less than the three-row IONIQ 9.

The Hyundai IONIQ 9 offers more range than its smaller sibling, with an EPA-estimated range of up to 335 miles for the base SE trim.

The IONIQ 5 is a midsize electric SUV, about the same size as a Tesla Model Y, with two rows and enough room for five passengers. Hyundai’s larger IONIQ 9 offers three rows of seating and six- or seven-seat configurations.

With an added row of seating, the IONIQ 9 offers up to 163.54 cubic feet of interior passenger volume, compared with the IONIQ 5, which offers up to 106.5 cubic feet.

While the IONIQ 9 may be the better deal right now, the IONIQ 5 is still a good fit for those looking for a smaller, more efficient SUV that doesn’t necessarily need the added space.

In comparison, the standard Tesla Model Y RWD is listed for lease starting at $499 a month with an EPA range of up to 321 miles. That’s for a 36-month lease with $3,000 down.

Using Tesla’s payment calculator, if you put the same $4,999 down for a 24-month lease like the IONIQ 5, the Model Y RWD’s monthly rate is $549.

Which one would you choose? The IONIQ 5, IONIQ 9, or Tesla Model Y? Let us know why in the comments.

Both the IONIQ 5 and IONIQ 9 are built at Hyundai’s Metaplant America outside of Savannah, Georgia.

I really haven’t seen much value in leasing. I’d rather buy the car outright using cash, with the goal of keeping the car for 10+ years.

My family recently bought a Hyundai IONIQ 5, and it is an outstanding car. Loaded with great features — both on the EV side and then for safety and comfort.

When we toured the facility earlier this year, Hyundai told us that its electric SUVs are attracting new buyers to the brand at a higher rate than its gas or hybrid vehicles.

The IONIQ 5 had a 69.8% conquest rate last year, while the IONIQ 9 had 64.3%. Hyundai’s Elantra N ranked third (61.9%), followed by the Palisade (61.2%) and Kona Electric (60.9%).

#4902. Kacey Musgraves Is Hanging Her Cowboy Hat in Mexico These Days

Kacey Musgraves may be a Nashville staple with deep Texas roots, but the country star is calling a new locale home lately. The “Rainbow” singer, who has resided in the Music City for many years, revealed in a recent interview that she now lives in Mexico “most of the time.”

Musgraves told People that the change of scene “ended up just completely changing my life and inspiring me so much. I’m so in love with so many aspects of it, and it’s really taught me a lot.” She explained that the transition took place over a year and a half ago when “this beautiful cosmic thing” drew her down to Mexico. Living there with her Australian cattle dog Pepper, the singer-songwriter found comfort in a slower pace of life, which has allowed her to be barefoot often and to spend plenty of time outdoors. The relocation even moved Musgraves to release a sister album to her most recent, Middle of Nowhere Vol. 2: Tex Mex Tapes, which will feature four songs in Spanish.

Musgaves previously opened up about her wanderlust and the possibility of leaving Nashville for the bright lights of New York City. “Girl, I’m always browsing Zillow late at night, acting like a fool—it’s my favorite,” she told People in 2024. “I wouldn’t rule [moving] out, but I do have a community in Nashville that I know that I couldn’t find anywhere else.”

Back in 2022, the country queen and her 3,500-square-foot Nashville home graced AD’s May 2022 cover. She purchased the property in 2020 during a time of big changes in her life, which included her split from singer-songwriter Ruston Kelly. Still, she made it clear that the blank canvas was no “divorce house.”

“I wanted a place that felt like me,” she said, “where I could express myself without having to think about another person and what they might want. This felt like a new beginning.” Musgraves went on to sell that space and ultimately bought a “cottage” in the Tennessee forest.
