#4901. Can Testosterone Add Inches to Your Penis?

There are a lot of misconceptions about what testosterone boosters can and can’t do.

Welcome to Testosterone HQ—Men's Health's guide to the exciting, complicated, and revolutionary world of testosterone. For everything you need to know about T, click here.

IF TESTOSTERONE HELPS increase your penis size during puberty, surely taking TRT will help you get a bigger package?

Unfortunately, testosterone works a bit differently once you’re out of your late teens. Here’s what to know about that.

Testosterone, which is produced by your testicles, “plays a key role in the development of characteristics like libido, and growth of the penis and testicles,” says Jamin Brahmbhatt, MD, urologist at Orlando Health. The hormone surges in your body during your tween and teen years to achieve all of these changes (along with deepening of the voice, facial hair, and so on).

The “blast” of testosterone is usually over by your mid- to late teens. Reproductive urologist Paul Turek, MD, says this is because fertility is achieved as the testicles and penis reach adult size.

YOUR PENIS DOES go through some interesting changes as you age, but it doesn’t get bigger.

After testosterone stops surging in your late teens, your sexual development is set for life. Genetics are the main reason your penis grows to its permanent size. “You’re born with the size already determined,” says Dr. Brahmbhatt.

Now testosterone is still being produced in your body when you’re an adult, but it won’t have any further effect on penis size. That biological fact holds true no matter your penis length. One study done by researchers at the University of Utah School of Medicine found that stretched penile length did not have any association with testosterone levels in adult men.

Since the penis consists of spongy tissue, not muscle, there’s no way to build it up on your own. “There are no safe and effective methods for permanently increasing penis size,” says Dr. Brahmbhatt. Penis pumps might help with stronger erections, but they do nothing for overall size.

WHILE TESTOSTERONE HAD an effect on your penis length early on, more won't help now. It might even hurt to take any sketchy T boosters or penis enlargement pills that promise to increase your girth or length. You have no idea whether they’re safe to take or whether they contain harmful ingredients.

A Stanford University study found that the average erect penile length has grown 24 percent worldwide over the past 29 years. The researchers report this is not due to an increase in testosterone levels.

Any change in development in a short amount of time is concerning. One of the authors of the study noted that chemical exposure could be interacting with the hormone makeup of guys—which may also explain lower sperm counts, increases in testicular cancer rates, and lower T levels in men nowadays.

The bottom line is that size doesn’t matter. Your penis health—especially function—does. If you notice any changes in how your penis looks or functions—patchy spots, bumps, curves—definitely don’t ignore them.

And while it’s natural to be curious about how your penis stacks up with other guys, don’t worry. The average penis length is not as big as you think.

Lisa is an internationally established health writer whose credits include Good Housekeeping, Prevention, Men’s Health, Oprah Daily, Woman’s Day, Elle, Cosmopolitan, Harper’s Bazaar, Esquire, Glamour, The Washington Post, WebMD, Medscape, The Los Angeles Times, Parade, Health, Self, Family Circle and Seventeen. She is the author of eight best-selling books, including The Essentials of Theater.

#4900. Apollo’s newest electric scooter is also its lowest-cost yet

Apollo has spent the last several years carving out a spot for itself toward the more premium end of the electric scooter market. Now the company is trying something different: going after the mass market with its least expensive model yet.

The new Apollo Dash carries a $699 MSRP, but is launching at $599 as part of the company’s Black Friday sale running through November 30. More importantly, Apollo says the Dash isn’t intended to simply be a stripped-down budget scooter, but rather a way to bring much of its existing technology down to a lower price point.

The scooter uses a 48V electrical system powering a 600W nominal, 1,200W peak-rated Bosch motor, with a top speed of 20 mph (32 km/h). Its 480Wh battery is rated for up to 25 miles (40 km) of range in Eco mode.

Apollo also claims the 48V architecture avoids limiting motor power to anemic levels as the battery drains, meaning riders shouldn’t experience the same increasingly sluggish performance toward the bottom of the battery that can occur on some lower-cost scooters.

At 43 lb (19.5 kg), the Dash is reasonably portable, though getting the weight and price down comes with an important compromise: there’s no suspension. Apollo also caps rider weight at 220 lb (100 kg), and points heavier riders or those regularly tackling rough roads and hills toward the more expensive Apollo Go instead.

Despite the lower price, there’s still a surprisingly long feature list. The Dash gets IP66 water resistance, self-healing tubeless tires, regenerative braking, built-in turn signals, Apollo’s Beam stem light, NFC unlocking, Apple Find My integration, and connectivity with the Apollo smartphone app.

Apollo says it put the frame through 10,000 km (6,200 miles) of durability validation before shipping production units. A beta test program also accumulated 1,002 miles over 144 rides without any reported punctures or maintenance issues. Beta riders averaged 19 miles (30 km) per ride while finishing with 29% battery remaining, according to Apollo.

Perhaps the bigger story here, though, is where Apollo is positioning the Dash. The company says 52% of its customers previously owned scooters from brands including Segway, NIU, and Xiaomi, but historically moving up to an Apollo often meant crossing the $1,000 mark.

At $599, the Dash appears designed to get those riders into Apollo’s ecosystem much earlier – while also putting the company squarely into competition with the mass-market scooters it previously sat above.

And for those wanting a bit more power or features, Apollo’s more upmarket models are on sale now as part of early Black Friday promotions.

#4896. Amazon set to deploy more than 3,000 TATA electric delivery trucks

Amazon isn’t just electrifying its American delivery operations. The virtual big box stores that Bezos built understands the benefits of EVs as well as anyone, and they’re working hard to deploy electric vehicles everywhere they can. And now, that global push is extending to India.

Indian automaker TATA Motors has announced a deal with Amazon to deploy 3,000 electric trucks across Amazon’s transportation network in India. When it goes live, it will be the largest electric truck deployment by in India and mark Amazon’s most significant move to extend its Indian fleet electrification beyond last-mile logistics.

“India is accelerating towards electric mobility and the realization of Viksit Bharat 2047,” says Shri H.D. Kumaraswamy, Hon’ble Union Minister for Heavy Industries and Steel, Government of India. “The collaboration between TATA Motors and Amazon shows what that looks like in practice: sustained demand from a nationwide logistics network met by electric trucks manufactured in India. Through PM E-DRIVE, the Government will continue to support this transition, and I look forward to more such efforts in powering green logistics.”

In addition to being a big deal for TATA, the 3,000 electric truck deal represents a serous bump to India’s deployed electric truck fleet, and are a major step towards the wide-scale electrification of freight transportation across the country – that’s not just good for Amazon’s fuel bills, it’s good for everyone and everything that breathes air, too.

“Electrifying our logistics and transportation network is an important part of Amazon’s goal to achieve net-zero carbon emissions by 2040,” says Abhinav Singh, Vice President, Operations, Amazon, APAC, Middle East, Africa and Turkey. “We have made significant progress in India, with more than 15,000 electric vehicles operating across 500-plus cities. To enable this transition, we are also supporting the development of infrastructure and solutions that make EV adoption easier for our carrier partners and drivers. We are now setting a bold vision to scale electric trucking, addressing one of the toughest challenges in decarbonizing transportation. Our collaboration with TATA Motors marks an important step towards realizing this vision. By combining our strengths, we aim to accelerate the deployment of electric trucks across more routes, starting with intracity operations and expanding to India’s major freight corridors.”

The truck Amazon will be deploying in these record numbers is a version of the Tata Ultra E.7, a 7.5-tonne (~16,000 lb.) straight truck powered by a 240 kW (~320 hp) electric motor drawing power from a 96 kWh LFP battery pack. The truck’s makers claim a loaded range of up to 130 km (~80 miles) on a single charge, with DC fast-charging that’s capable enough to keep the trucks charged up between deliveries.

Amazon’s new TATA electric delivery trucks will support the transport of goods between warehouse facilities – which means traveling across cities and along India’s national freight corridors.

#4894. What to Wear to Thanksgiving: 6 Outfit Ideas for Every Type of Gathering

From dressier holiday looks to comfy and stretchy.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

With Thanksgiving just around the corner, I’ve already started thinking about two very important things: what’s going on the dinner table and what I’ll wear. I’m especially looking forward to my mom’s baked mashed potatoes (a family favorite) and the homemade mac and cheese I’ll be bringing along. But, especially as a style writer, half the fun of the holidays is having a good excuse to put together a good outfit.

My family’s Thanksgiving gatherings tend to be pretty casual, so I’m not one to show up in anything overly dressy or uncomfortable. I’m usually somewhere in the middle, opting for an outfit that looks like I made an effort but still lets me enjoy a second helping of stuffing without worrying about a too-tight waistband. Think a great sweater with loose-fitting jeans, a knit dress with boots, or even a smartly-styled leggings outfit. Because as much as I love dressing up for the holidays, I also want to enjoy post-dinner card games or sit comfortably on the couch afterward.

If you’re approaching Thanksgiving dressing the same way, I’ve rounded up six outfit ideas to help get you inspired. I’ve included a few options perfect for a Christmas card photoshoot, along with more laid-back looks that are just as fitting for a casual family gathering. No matter what your holiday plans look like, these outfits strike that happy medium between looking good and feeling comfortable. Now, if you’ll excuse me, I have some mac and cheese to make.

In my opinion, there’s no outfit more perfect for the holidays than a sweater dress and boots. It’s cute enough for family photos but loose and soft enough to keep you comfy all day (especially if you kick the boots off at the door). Grab a long-sleeve knit dress that hits somewhere between mid-thigh and the knee and pair it with some fleece-lined tights and your fave knee-high boot. A midi or maxi dress with an ankle bootie works too.

Start by pulling out a denim jacket and denim skirt. From there, add a plain white T-shirt. I love brown for the fall, so I wore my version of this outfit with brown boots and a matching brown belt and bag. I love this combo because it looks super stylish, but I don’t feel overdressed or uncomfortable when I wear it. Don’t have a denim skirt or jacket? No problem. You can recreate this look with a leather jacket, jeans, or any neutral mini skirt.

Start with a pair of satin or silk pants and pair them with a sweater and ballet flats in the same shade. This outfit has been one of my go-tos lately because it’s so comfortable, but the monochrome palette and shiny finish of the pants make it look like I tried way harder than I actually did. You can even make it feel a little dressier by swapping the flats for a pair of pointed-toe ankle boots.

It doesn’t get comfier than leggings and slippers. For ultra-casual gatherings when you just want to focus on family and food without looking frumpy, just style yours a little smarter. Go for a pair of leggings with minimal seaming (especially a pair without a front seam), which makes them look sleeker. Then grab a dressy-looking oversized sweater—something with a boxy cut, a turtleneck, or a nice knit. I find that my tweed stitch J. Crew Rollneck Sweater is perfect for this look. Finish it off with a neutral slipper and a pair of scrunchy socks.

Crz Yoga SculptAir No Front Seam Leggings

Opt for a pair of baggier jeans for this more casual outfit, which will be way comfier than a restrictive skinny or straight-leg jean. A looser pant also pairs better with a cozy boot, like this platform ankle boot. Finally, throw on a long-sleeve top or sweater with a cute vest for an outfit that feels very fall.

Banana Republic The Icon Classic Wide-Leg Jean

Okay, you caught me—I actually wore this outfit for Christmas last year instead of Thanksgiving, but it definitely works for either holiday! For this combo, start with your favorite skirt and a turtleneck top. Then top it off with a relaxed-fitting blazer (I love this houndstooth blazer, but any pattern or solid color will do). On the bottom, throw on a pair of tights and a knee-high boot.

#4893. Shirley MacLaine at Home: 10 Photos of the Golden Age Great’s Life Behind the Scenes

“A house is really a life…one you’ve had or one you want to have. A new dream.” So wrote Shirley MacLaine on the first page of her memoir Sage-ing While Age-ing. And she should know. The dancer-actor-author has lived everywhere from the family home at 930 North Liberty Street in Richmond, Virginia, which she shared with her parents and younger brother (none other than Warren Beatty) to Manhattan apartments, Malibu beach cottages, and New Mexican desert ranches. And that’s just in this lifetime; MacLaine believes she has come around again through several incarnations, even writing books about her experience as a nongendered being in the Lemurian era, and another go-round on the lost continent of Atlantis.

In the 10 images below, we focus on MacLaine’s current incarnation, stepping into the Oscar-winner’s homes to see the star of Steel Magnolias and Downton Abbey at work, at play, and dancing on air.

MacLaine relaxes with a magazine in 1954.

At 20, MacLaine was a chorus girl on Broadway and the understudy for Carol Haney in The Pajama Game. When Haney twisted her ankle, MacLaine (who had changed her last name from Beaty to an alternate spelling of her mother’s maiden name, MacLean), replaced her for two weeks. Alfred Hitchcock spotted her and cast her in The Trouble with Harry, and Hal Walis signed her to a five-year studio contract, which led her to move to California. This 1954 shot shows her reading a magazine at home in Malibu, where she rented a tiny beachfront apartment. “Each home was a palace and a new jolt. They loomed on cultivated hilltops or nestled in L-shapes and U-shapes on shaded slopes, jewel-like habitations set in acres of jade carpet,” MacLaine recalled in her memoir Don’t Fall Off the Mountain. “We stared at the fairyland until our eyes hurt and our sense of reality reminded us that we were flat broke.”

When MacLaine moved from New York to California, she writes in Don’t Fall Off the Mountain, “sunshine bled from every corner and the dazzling white buildings gave me a permanent squint.” The happy gaze is on full display here in on the beach in front of the Malibu one-bedroom she and husband Steve Parker rented, which was not white, but a weathered wood cottage on stilts.

MacLaine and Parker, making the most of their outdoor shower in 1955.

“When I met Steve Parker, the attraction was immediate,” MacLaine writes in her photo-driven memoir The Wall of Life. She first encountered the businessman in New York, where, before he proposed, he informed her he was “undercover with military intelligence and work in Japan regularly, as well as other places throughout the world.” Here, Parker sneaks up on MacLaine in the one-bedroom, one-bathroom Malibu pad’s bonus outdoor shower.

The newlyweds on their home’s deck in Malibu in March, 1955.

This 1955 shot captures a rare moment of togetherness for MacLaine and Parker at their Malibu beach house. He often lived in Japan while she made films. “I’m not sure I would say that Steve and I had a happy marriage, but it was a satisfied one,” MacLaine wrote in The Wall of Life. “He had a mistress in Japan and I had whoever I was with.” But in their SoCal haven, they experienced their first taste of married life. “I was up every morning at four-thirty trying to be a housewife—ironing cleaning, and experimenting with the cookbook,” MacLaine wrote in Don’t Fall Off the Mountain. “At six Steve drove me to the studio, where we shot the final sequences of The Trouble with Harry.”

MacLaine dancing on the beach in front of her home in Malibu.

MacLaine, shown here leaping on the beach in front of her home, trained as a dancer since age three. “As I recall my life, I have come to understand that the physicality of dance was the most influential force,” she wrote in The Wall of Life. Here, MacLaine shows of her skills at the rental property she described in Don’t Fall Off the Mountain as “a tiny, one-bedroom apartment in a building set high on pilings that shook with each wave that crashed beneath it.”

This 1955 publicity shot shows MacLaine studying a script under Parker’s adoring eye in their Malibu home. While the residence’s knotty pine walls were peak mid-’50s rec room decor, MacLaine recalls Hollywood looking down on her living arrangements as her star was on the rise. She imagines the whisperings about her in Don’t Fall Off the Mountain: “She’s STILL living in a one-room shack at the beach. She’s STILL married to that man, what’s his name?”

MacLaine plays with her dog at home in 1956.

In this March 1956 photo, MacLaine plays with her boxer at home in Malibu. Six months later, on September 1, her daughter Sachi Parker would be born, inspiring MacLaine to move her new family to larger digs “in the Malibu colony with a spare room for Sachi’s new nurse-caretaker,” she wrote in The Wall of Life. “Our first year together felt like we were beach camping. I never wondered if the lifestyle was good for her, and she was never sick.” In 1963, architect Cliff Mays built an eight-unit Malibu apartment building where MacLaine would claim the top floor, which boasted six bedrooms, four baths, and a deck overlooking the ocean. She retained the property for 30 years until relocating to New Mexico, telling The Los Angeles Times, “I feel the high desert calling me.”

Shirley MacLaine in full 1970s style: maxidress, long hair. and aggressive shag carpet.

In 1972, MacLaine was spending most of her time in an apartment at 400 East 52nd Street after filming her short-lived TV series Shirley’s World. The building is part of the Southgate complex, which opened in 1931, but MacLaine did not adhere to the edifice’s Art Deco style—note the thick shag carpeting and the East Asian–inspired art on the walls. Dancer Tommy Tune bought the penthouse of the building after visiting MacLaine in her apartment.

Describing her life in New York in the 1970s, MacLaine wrote in You Can Get There from Here that “on any given day, I would mosey into Serendipity for a frozen mocha, plunder the skirt shop at Bloomingdale’s, walk to the Village and back until I reached Beekman Place at sunset and breathed in the smell of the East River.… I wanted to sit in my apartment for hours, watching all the news shows, and eating cinnamon buns from the automat.”

In this 1980 photo of MacLaine at the Southgate, her hair is different but the floral sofa remains as it was in 1972. In the years between 1972 and 1980, “I did just three films. I don’t think I got any good scripts in that period, but I didn’t care and was honestly more interested in seeing the world and understanding what was going on in it.” She made a documentary in China, campaigned for George McGovern and Jimmy Carter, and did live shows in New York and London. She was drawn back to Hollywood to make The Turning Point, and won an Oscar for her work in 1983’s Terms of Endearment.

Today, MacLaine’s New Mexico ranch is on the market, and, at age 92, she has been spotted out and about in her old stomping grounds: Malibu.

#4891. Honda just showed off its first high-end electric mountain bike

Honda makes just about everything with a motor, from lawnmowers and generators to cars, motorcycles, and even airplanes. Now the Japanese giant is adding another electrified vehicle to the list – a premium electric mountain bike.

Unveiled at EICMA 2026 in Milan, the new Honda RN0 marks the company’s first entry into the premium electric mountain bike market. And unlike many automotive-branded e-bikes that amount to little more than a licensing deal and a familiar logo slapped on someone else’s frame, Honda appears to have actually put some engineering muscle behind this one.

The RN0 was jointly developed with German high-end e-MTB manufacturer ROTWILD and features a proprietary carbon monocoque frame and distinctive gull-arm swingarm inspired by Honda’s motorcycle chassis development experience.

There’s actually some real history behind that connection. Honda experimented with mountain bike development in the early 2000s, culminating in its wild RN01 downhill racing bike. In 2005, that project won the overall series championship in the UCI Mountain Bike Downhill World Cup. Honda never turned the RN01 into a consumer bicycle, but more than two decades later, the RN0 is effectively picking up where the company previously left off.

The roughly 24 kg (53 lb) RN0 is set up as a multi-role electric mountain bike, featuring 160 mm FOX 36 FLOAT Factory Kashima front suspension paired with a FOX FLOAT X Factory Kashima rear shock. DT Swiss H1900 SPLINE wheels use a mullet setup with a 29-inch wheel up front and 27.5-inch wheel in the rear, while Shimano DEORE XT four-piston brakes handle stopping duties.

Those are some quality components, but the drivetrain may be even more interesting.

Honda is using Shimano’s new 2.3 kg drive unit, rated for up to 100 Nm of torque and 750 watts of maximum output, paired with a sizeable 720 Wh battery.

Shimano’s AUTO ASSIST can automatically vary motor assistance based on terrain and rider input, while AUTO SHIFT handles gear changes to maintain an appropriate cadence.

There’s even an Assist Carryover function that briefly continues providing power after pedaling stops, potentially making it easier to clear rocks, roots, and other technical obstacles that normally would see the assist grind to a halt right when it is needed most.

A 2.2-inch color display is integrated directly into the top tube, and there’s USB-C charging plus Shimano app connectivity for adjusting motor settings and electronically locking the bike.

Honda hasn’t yet announced pricing in its release, but with a carbon chassis, Factory-level FOX suspension, XT brakes, and premium Shimano electronics, don’t expect this to be the everyman’s electric bike.

If anything, the RN0 looks much more like Honda is finally bringing its motorcycle engineering mentality back to mountain bikes – this time with a battery and motor along for the ride.

#4890. I Analyzed 30 AI ‘Doctors’ on Social Media and Couldn’t Believe What I Found

The accounts are real. The medical advice is concerning.

I HAVE YEARS’ worth of poop in my intestines.

At least, that’s what a doctor on social media told me. He was wearing a white coat. His diplomas hung on the wall behind him. Ominous music played in the background while he performed a graphic demonstration about how one product can clean my gut of all that fecal matter, leaving me feeling healthier, happier, and less “toxic.”

There’s just one problem: The doctor doesn’t exist. He was created by generative artificial intelligence to sell a product.

You’re probably thinking that you’d never fall for such an obvious scam, but AI-generated content is growing increasingly sophisticated. And unless a social post comes labelled as “A.I. content,” you might never know these virtual characters dispensing health advice aren’t human.

To get a sense of how widespread this issue is, I searched TikTok and Instagram for AI-generated health content featuring fake doctors and other medical professionals. I wanted to see what sorts of information they were giving, if they were selling products, and if there was a transparent disclosure of the use of AI to create that content.

I quickly identified 30 accounts that featured AI-generated doctors and health influencers. Some of these accounts were not marked as AI content. Others had the blue Meta verification check that’s supposed to mark an account as authentic and credible. But how can someone be authentic and credible if they don’t exist?

What I found was shocking—but there are still a few critical tells you can use to sniff out a scam.

TAKE, FOR EXAMPLE, the curious case of James Anderson.

In September 2026, an Australian fact-checking company discovered hundreds of Instagram ads featuring an AI-generated surgeon by that name. Anderson was supposedly from New Zealand, Australia, and even Toronto, depending on the ad. In Canada, the content identified him as Dr. James Anderson, MD, FRCSC (Orth). FRCSC is Fellow of the Royal College of Surgeons (Canada), a regulated credential that was clearly being hijacked.

His pitch was simple and hopeful: Instead of surgery for your sore shoulder, you should consider a device called Mendable Shoulder. The device, allegedly developed by Anderson and a “team of biomedical engineers,” could heal your pain in just 12 minutes a day, according to the pitch. It cost $119, but was cheaper if you bought three. Some of these ads claim that Anderson works at North Toronto Shoulder Clinic, which, as someone who actually lives in North Toronto, I’ve never heard of—because it doesn’t exist.

Whoever created Anderson dreamed up an entire persona for him, complete with a convincing appearance, fabricated credentials, made-up experience, and fake testimonials.

Even real doctors aren’t safe from having their names and likeness scraped by these companies to sell products. A recent investigation by CBC News found hundreds of online videos using deepfakes of real medical professionals whose faces and voices had been stolen and manipulated to make them appear to endorse products and services that they’d never recommended.

NOT ALL THE accounts I studied relied on fake credentials or white coats and fabricated diplomas. Some featured elderly, AI-generated characters who were allegedly sharing “ancient remedies” and old “secrets” from other cultures.

A large proportion of these supposed “remedies” were absurd or even sexist, calling breasts “hooters” or showing a model of a vagina with “worms” coming out of it. All of them told viewers that modern medicine had forgotten about—or hadn’t yet discovered—this special cure. One simple remedy can solve all of your health problems, was a common refrain. Some of these accounts linked to Amazon pages selling supplements or books. Others were clearly going for clicks and views. Which brings up the next red flag...

THE MOST SURPRISING detail I noticed was how popular these accounts appeared to be. Several had follower counts in the millions, which only serves to increase their perceived value and credibility.

Although some of the engagement on the posts probably came from bots (or at least, I want to believe that they are), there had to be some real people in there, too. And real people are certainly encountering this content, and potentially using it to help make decisions about their health. But don’t mistake popularity for authority.

MY INFORMAL SEARCH wasn’t a scientific study. But larger investigations suggest that AI-generated experts are an increasingly sophisticated vector for health misinformation.

In 2026, Spanish fact-checking organization Maldita found 250 AI-generated health accounts reached more than 53 million followers across seven languages.

They also identified 25 Instagram accounts impersonating medical professionals. Collectively, these accounts had more than 3.6 million followers and had accumulated more than 397 million video views.

So remain skeptical, especially when a so-called “expert” or “professional” talks about “ancient remedies,” supplements, or treatments that involve basic foods that are found in your kitchen.

And if they offer a link to buy supplements from their Amazon store, back away. If it sounds too good to be true, or even just seems “off,” ask your doctor or another IRL health professional.

Abby Langer, RD is the owner of Abby Langer Nutrition, a Toronto-based nutrition consulting and communications company

#4887. 5 American, 6 Canadian: Workhorse W56 arrives in the True North

Electric truck brand Workhorse is bringing its W56 Class 5 and 6 electric trucks to Canada thanks to a new agreement with a Canadian dealer group and fleet management provider offering truck and trailer sales, parts, repair, and 24/7 roadside assistance and towing services across the Northern nation.

Building on years of electric step van deployments in Canada and underscoring the company’s commitment to Canada as a growth region, the new dealer agreement with Tristan Fleet Management marks the first-ever Canadian dealer partnership for Workhorse.

“Canadian fleets need proven electric trucks that deliver on quality and performance in any condition,” said James Griffin, Chief Revenue Officer at Workhorse. “The W56 has already proven itself on this front in real-world fleet service across Canada. Tristan’s strong reputation, its extensive network, and ability to provide around-the-clock roadside support will be extremely valuable as Workhorse continues to grow our presence in the country.”

Workhorse has been selling and supporting electric trucks in Canada since 2021 through its partnership with Purolator, a Canadian-owned leading integrated freight, package and logistics solutions provider that has grown alongside the rapid expansion of online shopping for everything from groceries to car parts and road-ready motorcycles has also seen the expansion of last-mile delivery contractors. These fleet operators and drivers are essential workers who keep the online economy rolling, and they are getting positively pummeled by this year’s record fuel prices, a product of America’s latest “dumb oil war” in Iran.

And, after Workhorse’ fleet logged its 20 millionth mile driven with zero tailpipe emissions, you could hardly claim that it’s an unknown quantity – a fact that Tristan understands.

“Our customers have been exploring electrification for some time now,” explains Daniel Barbu, Tristan’s Vice President and General Manager. “With generous incentives from Écocamionnage, as well as the current extremely high cost of fuel, the business case for going electric has never been stronger … we’re looking forward to helping Workhorse expand its customer base across Canada and supporting our customers meet their own goals for sustainability and vehicle performance.”

Tristan operates out of seven locations throughout Québec and Ontario. In addition to its Workhorse offerings, the company is also an authorized Michelin tire, Cummins engine, and Vanguard trailer dealer.

#4883. Yamaha’s new electric scooter takes aim at Europe’s 125cc commuters

Yamaha is expanding its electric scooter lineup with the new XENTER E, a 125cc-equivalent electric scooter designed specifically with European city riding in mind.

Unveiled around EICMA 2026 in Milan alongside the new NMAX E, the XENTER E is a high-wheel scooter riding on 16-inch wheels front and rear.

That’s an important distinction in European cities, where larger wheels can make quick work of the cobblestones, potholes, and tram tracks that can turn smaller-wheeled scooters into teeth-rattling experiences.

The XENTER E uses Yamaha’s new eAxle powertrain, combining the motor, controller, and reduction gearbox into a compact frame-mounted unit. The motor produces 7.5 kW (10 hp) of peak power and 33.3 Nm of torque, putting the scooter into Europe’s A1 license category.

Mounting the motor to the frame also means Yamaha can use a more conventional motorcycle-style swingarm rather than hanging the motor on the rear suspension like many scooters. That reduces unsprung weight and should help improve handling and ride quality.

Power is supplied from an integrated 3.5 kWh battery mounted low in the chassis. Yamaha claims 108 km (67 miles) of range on the WMTC Class 1 cycle, though real-world range is expected to be somewhat lower.

An 800W onboard charger can refill the battery in around 5.5 hours from a public charging station. Yamaha will also offer a cable for charging from a household outlet.

Other niceties include three riding modes, traction control, regenerative braking, a reverse mode for maneuvering at low speeds, smart-key operation, and a 5-inch TFT display. Smartphone connectivity adds navigation, battery information, maintenance reminders, and parking-location tracking.

For about four days now, I am thinking this could be my next ride. A 4 or 4,5 Kwh battery would be a real nice. But for now, it fits perfectly in my daily commute and I could trade in my 2024 Honda supercub. However, € 7800 is too much.. for this kind of money, or just a tad more, there are several low mileage BMW's CE04 on the market.

There’s also enough underseat storage for a full-face helmet, depending on its size, plus USB-C charging and provisions for accessories such as a windscreen and 34-liter top case.

The XENTER E shares its powertrain and battery with Yamaha’s new NMAX E, but the high-wheel layout gives it a distinctly more European commuter flavor.

Yamaha hasn’t officially announced pricing for each market, though early reports from Milan point to €7,799 in Germany. Deliveries are expected to begin in spring 2027.

It’s certainly not cheap, but the XENTER E looks like Yamaha is finally getting serious about building electric scooters that can replace its bread-and-butter 125cc urban machines, rather than simply supplement them.

#4880. Theaster Gates is Changing the Future of American Fine China

It all started when Theaster Gates wanted to throw a dinner party. The Chicago-based artist, whose practice blends ceramics, painting, performance, and urban renewal, imagined inviting 200 guests on each of five nights. Central to the project, he envisioned creating all the tableware himself.

“My friends were like, ‘Oh, you should work with Pickard,’ ” Gates recalls, referring to the last remaining producer of porcelain tableware in the US, founded in 1893 and headquartered since 1930 in Antioch, Illinois. When he told Pickard he would need some 10,000 items, the firm admitted such an order would be daunting but invited him to visit.

“I came to the warehouse and realized that the company had had better days,” Gates says. “I’m wanting to say this with a lot of dignity, but as we moved between tarps and equipment, it became clear that the company was just doing what it could to stay alive.”

China dishware fills two rolling racks at the Pickard factory in Illinois, which was recently purchased by Theaster Gates.

Gates at the Pickard facility with molds for dishware

Pickard’s august history of commissions includes White House services for first ladies Laura Bush and Michelle Obama, as well as the official State Department dinnerware used in US embassies around the world. Once an essential element of bridal registries, fine china has waned in popularity, however, along with the tradition of formal entertaining at home. After CEO Andy Pickard Morgan, a member of the fourth generation of the family to run the business, acknowledged that he was ready to call it a day, one thing led to another, and Gates says, “I am now the owner of Pickard China.”

His acquisition might seem out of character. Gates has forged a formidable reputation for himself and his Rebuild Foundation by buying rundown buildings in Chicago’s historically Black South Side, then converting them into thriving arts and education hubs. An abandoned residence is now the Black Cinema House; a century-old savings and loan is now the Stony Island Arts Bank, which mounts exhibitions and safeguards historical collections. Pickard, meanwhile, is located in a quiet, predominantly white village on the Wisconsin border, some 67 miles from his studio on Chicago’s South Side.

Gates, funnel in hand, surrounded by artifacts of the Pickard process

But Gates does not see a contradiction. For starters, he says, his interest in craft transcends racial or ethnic origins; one formative experience was studying with master potters in Japan. Gates notes that while many of his collections relate to Black culture, he also took on the late scholar Robert Bird’s library of films by the Russian director Andrei Tarkovsky and the classical music archive of the LaSalle Quartet, whose core members were Jewish. “I have those because they are rich, and someone passed, and those things needed a new life,” he explains. “The problem of the archive is not one of Black or white; it’s one of stewardship and imagination.” He has even shown an affinity for luxury goods, having made ceramic vessels for Prada Home in Milan this past April.

Pickard also presents the kind of big, multifaceted problem Gates, who holds a master’s degree in urban planning, says “makes me feel alive as an artist,” in this case encompassing craftsmanship and legacy, as well as the value of labor and community. His plans are still developing but include upgrading the facilities, rethinking the product line, and beefing up the staff, which now stands at 14 full-time employees, down from dozens at the company’s height. Preserving their know-how for posterity is a priority. “I want this really capable team to train a new generation of descalers and liners and glazers,” he says. Also definite: The company will continue operating in the commercial sphere, and he will use its massive kilns to fire his own large sculptures, something he’s wanted to do for a decade. “I’m thinking about manufacturing ceramics as an extension of my practice,” he notes.

Gates has worked with a variety of materials over the years, including tar paper—a nod to his roofer father—and salvaged fire hoses, which reference Civil Rights Era marchers who were blasted with water by police. Ceramics, though, were the focus of his formal studies and remain at the heart of his practice. “When I was training, I touched porcelain, but I was always a rough stoneware guy,” he says. “The more grog and sand and feldspar, the better.”

Today Gates considers porcelain “an amazing craft” and expects the workshop to become “a playground for artistic promise,” where he, together with staff and visiting artists alike, can “dream about a raw material that could be anything.”

Setting the table with bone china may no longer be the signifier of position and civility it once was, but Gates believes there’s still room for appreciation, as both an instrument of ritual and a family heirloom. “Legacy objects play a huge role in how we remember our grandparents, our parents, and our past,” he says.

And Gates’s own party is very much still on the table, so to speak. “Porcelain things are beautiful objects, but I’m drawn as much to the use of the thing as I am to the thing,” he says. “The idea that I could make large works of art with porcelain is exciting, but I’m also excited to be able to host a thousand-person dinner and make all the wares for it.”

#4878. Harbinger scores another $240M in latest investment round

Electric truck maker Harbinger has been racking up wins in recent months, and after scoring a $300 million order from international shipping giant FedEx and a defense contract with the US military, the company has drummed up another $240 million in Series D funding.

Companies like FedEx and Amazon have been making serious moves to fully electrify their fleet operations, both in an effort to decarbonize and to avoid the every-increasing price of diesel. At $300 million for 2,000 medium-duty electric delivery trucks, Harbinger’s latest deal with FedEx is believed to be one of the largest binding orders for electric medium or heavy-duty trucks in history – and it’s a big enough deal to inspire more than 240 million votes of confidence in the company’s future, one dollar at a time.

“Harbinger exists to challenge what industry has learned to accept, and reinvent the critical systems the world depends on,” said John Harris, the Co-Founder and CEO of Harbinger. “By rethinking critical systems from first principles, we aim to unlock better performance, greater reliability, safer operations, and more room to grow. Electrification is at the core of everything we do, and increasingly, electrification is at the core of industry, defense, mobility, and AI. Harbinger’s role is not simply to give customers another option. It is to reset expectations for what critical systems should deliver.”

Harbinger’s Series D funding round was co-led by Tiger Global, the Capricorn Technology Impact Fund, and Ridgeline. The company says the new funding will support expansion into energy storage systems, power generation, autonomy software, and defense applications, as well as the continued ramp up of its medium-duty platform manufacturing capacity. With this latest round of financing, Harbinger has raised over $650 million since 2021.

As for me, I’m hoping they use the money to come up with more fun, high-octane chase videos that show off their trucks’ performance capabilities. Time will tell.

#4875. Tesla deemed liable for fraud after Solar Roof ‘experiment’ on a home

A California judge has deemed Tesla liable for fraud in a Solar Roof lawsuit, after the company repeatedly failed to hand over evidence. Tesla had already admitted it breached the contract, broke its warranty, and was negligent on the job.

The homeowner says fixing the damage will cost “well over $2.5 million.” Tesla once offered him $5,000.

The case is Conroy v. Tesla, filed in 2022 in San Diego Superior Court’s North County division (case No. 37-2022-00043670). Electrek reviewed the court orders, the complaint, and the parties’ joint trial readiness report. Here’s what’s in them.

Ambrose Conroy, who lives in Rancho Santa Fe, signed up for a Tesla Solar Roof in March 2020. The original contract was $82,210 for a 13.1 kW system. By February 2021, after two amendments, it had grown to roughly $110,000 for an 18.2 kW roof and six Powerwalls.

The roof was never finished. It never passed a county inspection, and it never produced a single kWh for the house.

Tesla isn’t contesting most of that anymore. In the joint report filed by both sides last month, Tesla stipulated to liability on three of the five claims.

The other two claims, fraud and negligent misrepresentation, are where things went badly for Tesla.

Judge Cynthia Freeland sanctioned Tesla twice over discovery before going further. She ordered it to pay $15,467.50 in February and another $13,500 in June, both times for failing to produce a proper privilege log and responsive documents, and both times warning that issue sanctions could follow.

Tesla missed the next deadline anyway. So on September 11, the court deemed Tesla liable for fraud and negligent misrepresentation, limiting the trial on those claims to compensatory and punitive damages.

The judge found “ample evidence to support that Defendants have acted willfully.” Among the missing records were Microsoft Teams messages (Tesla’s own corporate witness testified they’re kept “for infinity”), OSHA logs, emails with San Diego County inspectors, and more than 20 quality, install, and safety reports from Tesla’s internal Bolt system.

Tesla asked her to reconsider, arguing it had since produced everything. She said no in a ruling dated October 27, writing that the late documents could support Conroy’s theory that Tesla “knowingly used Plaintiff’s home as an experiment while knowing they lacked the experience to perform as promised.”

And the “experiment” word shows up in Tesla’s own messages, according to the trial exhibit list. Exhibit 224 is described as text messages from January 13, 2021, between Tesla’s director of model engineering Bobby Yang and other Tesla employees.

To be clear, that’s how Conroy’s side describes the messages on the exhibit list. We haven’t seen the texts themselves. But Elon Musk is on Conroy’s witness list, and internal emails quoted in the complaint point the same way.

On February 9, 2021, Yang wrote internally that the house was in the “99.5 percentile in terms of complexity” and that “if we can do this job well, we should be able to do any job well.” RJ Johnson, then head of Tesla’s energy business, replied that the project would “skew numbers” but that Tesla needed “to make it right.”

Johnson was fired in April 2021. The roof had at least 59 mounting planes, and one employee called it the largest job Tesla had attempted at the time, according to the complaint.

Conroy’s account of the installation reads like a list of everything that can go wrong on a roof. These are his allegations, but Tesla has now conceded breach and negligence.

The tear-off was supposed to take two days and took two weeks. Tesla left in November 2020 with bare roof decking exposed, then had crews work overtime on December 27 to get underlayment down before a storm. Tiles arrived scratched and damaged and went on the roof anyway. Glass tiles shattered in the driveway and lawn, and the family says they still can’t walk there barefoot.

By March 2021, Johnson had agreed in writing that paying “when you’re happy with the outcome is completely reasonable.” Tesla’s billing department kept calling anyway.

In August 2021, an internal email weighed just leaving.

The project went through at least seven project managers. In November 2021, Tesla told Conroy the people behind its repair plan were gone and it had nobody in the area to do the work.

In April 2022, Tesla offered $5,000 to settle everything.

It didn’t end there. In February 2025, more than two years into the lawsuit, Tesla failed another San Diego County inspection after showing up without the plans for the venting system. And this summer, Conroy’s electrical expert opened up part of the roof and concluded that the way Tesla ran wiring and conduit under the tiles created a short-circuit and fire risk in heavy rain. The roof is now coming off. Tesla’s lawyers point out that even Conroy’s counsel called that fire risk “counterintuitive.”

Tesla is fighting the sanction hard. In an October 29 filing, it asked the court to freeze the case while it petitions the California Court of Appeal, arguing the order is “unduly punitive,” violates due process, and amounts to a directed verdict on fraud before trial.

Tesla also says the discovery fight had no connection to the fraud claims and that it has now produced everything relevant. The judge already rejected that argument once.

The November 20 trial date is off. A trial readiness conference is set for December 4, and Conroy’s lawyers want to go to trial in December.

This isn’t the first Solar Roof horror story in this case file. Conroy’s complaint attaches our 2020 report on a Solar Roof buyer left with tarps over his house after Tesla tried an untested underlayment on his home. It also cites a 2025 California Contractors State License Board action seeking to revoke Tesla’s contractor licenses over abandoned Solar Roof jobs.

What a nightmare. This is the most damaging Solar Roof case I’ve seen, and I’ve been covering this product since Elon Musk unveiled it on a Universal Studios backlot in 2016.

A botched install isn’t news on its own. Tesla only installed about 3,000 of these roofs before killing the product this year, and plenty of them went sideways. What’s different here is the paper trail: internal emails calling a customer’s home a test of whether Tesla could do “any job,” texts describing the job as an “experiment” with daily reports going to Musk, and then years of Tesla failing to hand over its own records until a judge ran out of patience.

And Tesla’s response to all of it was $5,000.

Look, it’s normal for things not to go as planned with a new product, and early adopters need to be flexible. However, we are talking about someone’s literal roof over their head here and a trillion-dollar company. Tesla could have and should have made it right a long time ago.

Any time there are internal messages in the record that say essentially "let's skip town rather than try to make this right", it's a tough lift to make a jury think that everything was aboveboard.

The question that strikes me is whether this may also rise to the criminal level, since the CEO was apparently involved in the decision.

The issue sanction might not survive the Court of Appeal. Courts don’t love deciding fraud through discovery orders, and Tesla’s due process argument isn’t frivolous. But even if it gets reversed, Tesla has still admitted it breached the contract and was negligent, and the jury will see how this customer was treated.

This also says a lot about how Tesla ran the Solar Roof business. It sold a product it knew was hard to install, on homes it knew were complex, while the people running the program kept leaving. Conroy’s house was one of the worst cases, but installers are still dealing with the fallout of Tesla walking away from the product earlier this year.

The real question is what’s in those Teams messages and texts Tesla fought so hard to keep out of this case. If this goes to trial in December, we might find out.

#4873. 5 New Retail Design Rules, According to Experts

When nearly anything can be bought with a few taps on a screen, brick-and-mortar stores have to work harder—and smarter—to earn a visit. What e-commerce can't replicate, even with the cleverest algorithms, is the thrill of stepping into a beautifully conceived space—and discovering something you didn't know you wanted. From side-by-side jewel-box fashion boutiques in Paris to a palatial bridal studio set in a 19th-century building in downtown New York, AD PRO highlights the big ideas shaping today’s most engaging retail environments.

The first goal for any new retail space is obvious: Make people want to be there.

Fashion designer Danielle Frankel Hirsch just opened a 12,000-square-foot studio in Tribeca, collaborating with Studio Partners Architecture and AD PRO Directory designer Augusta Hoffman to create a gasp-worthy dress boutique worthy of any bride's fantasies. Located in a 19th century edifice originally built to house the New York Mercantile Exchange, the shop, says Frankel Hirsch, is designed as a transportive experience. After guests enter the space through its unassuming Tribeca storefront entrance, they ascend a grand carpeted staircase to a large gallery boasting over 30-foot floor-to-ceiling windows. Hoffman worked with artist Miriam Ellner to create églomisé paneling surrounding the staircase, and outfitted the spaces to the height of elegance, with Fortuny fabrics, furniture sourced from Paris, and handpainted ceiling murals.

The 30-foot ceilings at New York City's Danielle Frankel guarantee a wedding dress shopping experience that's hard to equal

In Paris, Rubirosa's two petite boutiques, designed by Louis Charles Aka, deliver a similar sense of occasion on a much smaller scale. The original 500-square-foot shop, which opened in June 2025, was joined this past August by a second location just next door. Rather than simply duplicate the first boutique's nostalgic formality, Aka has given its new neighbor a distinct personality. The lacquered shelving remains, displaying cashmere sweaters, crisp poplin shirts, and soft leather slippers in a rainbow of colors. But where the original featured tall bookcases, the new shop has floor-to-ceiling arches whose cubbies become bands of color when filled with garments of cashmere, cotton, and silk.

In the original boutique, the defining gesture was a dense, velvety red wall-to-wall carpet from La Maison Bineau. “I spent everything on the red carpet,” Aka declares. “People criticized, ‘You will have to change it in three months.’ I said, ‘We don’t care. We want people to feel special.’” In the second shop, the color drama comes courtesy of vivid Yves Klein blue—a knowing wink to the fashion set. Voluminous white balloon drapery heightens the theatricality, suggesting a salon for elegant grand dames even as the boutique attracts a younger crowd of influencers and trendsetters.

Lacquered walls hung with portraits of Pope John Paul II and Mobutu Sese Seko at Rubirosa’s

The exterior of Rubirosa’s on Rue de Grenelle in Paris

AD100 designer Brigette Romanek took a more subtle approach to creating desire at Catherine Bloom for Nordstrom, an appointment-only shop in Los Angeles where Bloom, the department store’s Director of Luxury Styling, works with private clients. “I want to make people feel something,” says Romanek. “A store should be a destination you look forward to.” Her use of paneled walls in camel, khaki, and rose suede, plus her signature mix of elements both edgy (the angular leather sofa) and delicate (the plasterwork ceiling) creates what she calls “a protected feeling” that makes the space feel worth seeking out.

Suede-paneled walls and ornate plasterwork at Catherine Bloom for Nordstrom

A punchy red-orange “wallop” moment at Big Night’s West Village outpost

Where Romanek’s space whispers intimacy, Katherine Lewin’s Big Night shouts joy. The New York City shop Big Night, which sells brightly hued kitchen and entertaining goods, trades old-world refinement for modern exuberance. “People tell me they get a shot of serotonin walking into the store,” she says. Her brand’s signature color of punchy red-orange provides what she calls a “wallop”—an immediate sensory experience that registers before customers even process what they’re looking at. Handmade tiles from Smink Studio, screen-printed with bold geometric patterns, are mixed in unexpected color combinations throughout the West Village store.

FoundRae’s Aspen boutique, complete with velvety chairs, books, and intriguing display cases lined with handwritten letters, old photos, and found objects

Once you’ve lured customers in, the next challenge is keeping them there. “If someone is waiting for their partner to try on 50 things, they need to be comfortable,” says Romanek, who installed upholstered chairs from her collection for Crate & Barrel inside individual dressing rooms. Meanwhile, curtains at the store stay closed at all times, enhancing the protected atmosphere that encourages clients to relax and take their time.

Beth Hutchens, founder of fine jewelry line FoundRae, has turned lingering into an art form. The average customer stay at her fine jewelry boutiques—which include locations in New York, Miami, Los Angeles, Dallas, Palm Beach, and Aspen—is an hour and a half. It makes sense when you consider the cozy reading nooks well stocked with books and a rotating menu of complimentary snacks and wine.

The maze-like layout of Big Night’s West Village

In the age of algorithmic shopping recommendations, physical retail can offer something genuinely different: the pleasure of stumbling upon the unexpected. When Lewin found Big Night’s West Village location, for instance, it was a warren of small spaces and hidden corners that defied traditional notions of retail design. Rather than optimizing for efficient shopping (and increasing her renovation costs), she embraced the pre-existing layout and discovered that it primed shoppers’ sense of exploration and surprise. “The store feels like a house party with different things happening in different corners,” Lewin explains.

At Fishs Eddy's Brooklyn location, deliberately cluttered displays add to the sense of discovery

At Fishs Eddy, the beloved New York City housewares shop founded in 1986, the thrill of the hunt has always been part of the appeal. Now, four decades after opening its original Manhattan location, the retailer has expanded with two new outposts, at Chelsea Market and in Brooklyn's Dumbo neighborhood. The latter, an ambitious 3,800-square-foot space, deliberately preserves the cheerfully cluttered, homespun aesthetic that has become the brand's calling card. Rather than give the larger store a slick makeover, founder Julie Gaines embraced the same informal, decidedly human approach to retail design: Vintage-style dishes, deadstock dinnerware, and offbeat glassware are piled high on reclaimed-wood displays and mismatched vintage furniture, evoking the warehouses where the original merchandise was discovered. Gaines describes her merchandising philosophy as “very direct: the way we found it in the warehouse.” But that seemingly improvised look takes effort. “We spend a lot of time looking like we don’t spend time,” she admits. An old-fashioned soda fountain serving egg creams and inexpensive drip coffee adds to the new store's convivial atmosphere.

FoundRae takes discovery to remarkable lengths. Hutchens installed fully realized miniature vignettes behind unmarked cupboard doors—doll-size rooms that include recreations of her own home and FoundRae boutiques, complete with furniture and art. Display cases feature what Hutchens calls a “menagerie of the ephemeral”—handwritten letters, old photos, and evocative found items loosely pinned behind the jewelry. Then there are the “Discovery Drawers”—wood-fronted, glass-topped compartments that hold one-of-a-kind pieces unique to each location (never photographed or featured online).

At Sézane’s new 2,800-square-foot boutique on New York’s Upper East Side, designer Pierre Lacroix has created a similar element of surprise through a dramatic shift in mood. Dubbed L’Appartement on Madison Avenue, the space evokes a Parisian home crossed with an art gallery, with books, artwork by William Klein, and vintage furniture. Most of the boutique feels light and airy, with creamy walls, cerused oak parquet floors, and parchment light fixtures. But tucked off to one side is a small shoe salon, drenched in dark chocolate tones and moodily lit. The intimate, after hours lounge-like space offers an unexpected counterpoint to the rest of the shop.

Sézane’s newest shop, aka L’Appartement on Madison Avenue, designed by Pierre Lacroix

The shoe salon at Sézane’s L’Appartement offers an unexpected change of atmosphere

Even in Rubirosa’s tiny space, Aka has created moments of discovery. He’s filled the walls with oil portraits of Luciano Pavarotti, Pope John Paul II, and various international dictators—a quirky mix of imagery that gives the space a distinctive personality. Visual layers invite clients to continuously notice new details, rewarding repeat visits.

Wes Anderson calling! A particularly Instagrammable corner of Rubirosa’s

Aka admits that Rubirosa’s Instagram-friendly storefront serves as what he calls “a lure”—beautiful enough that people stop to photograph it even if they don’t come inside. “Some people come just to take pictures in front,” he says. The combination of the crimson carpet, dark lacquered millwork, and those quirky framed portraits creates an irresistibly photogenic and TikTok-friendly environment. Romanek’s thoughtful color palette and material mix at the Nordstrom boutique creates a similarly camera-ready backdrop. The social media effect, says Romanek, is simply a natural byproduct of creating a distinctive space. “When a space feels different, conversations about it happen,” she explains. “And that’s what we like. We like people talking about the experience.”

At the Danielle Frankel bridal studio, the sheer spectacle reaches another level entirely. From the soaring ceilings to the grand staircase with its shimmering églomisé panels, the interiors offer no shortage of photo opportunities. But for a boutique devoted to dressing brides for what is, ideally, a once-in-a-lifetime occasion, perhaps a little over-the-top grandeur is exactly the point.

Hoffman worked with the artist Miriam Ellner to create églomisé paneling surrounding the staircase. “The eglomise is one of my favorite things,” says Danielle Frankel designer Frankel Hirsch. The result is staircase that not creates a heightened emotional atmosphere for in-store shoppers but also provides a dramatic backdrop for photography.

Hutchens believes retail design isn’t inextricably tied to scalability. “Ask yourself, if every store was your only store, what would you do and how would you invest in it and change it?” she says. “You evolve faster when you think that way.”

While all seven FoundRae stores share some design DNA—Hutchens embeds her signature mythological symbols in floors, ceilings, and furniture throughout—each reflects its location. The Aspen boutique is swathed in green, alluding to the town’s namesake aspen trees, with a large marquetry sunburst as its centerpiece. The Palm Beach shop features gilded verre églomisé, while in Miami, a vintage Steinway grand piano with hand-applied gold symbols anchors the room. As a result, the stores feel both part of a cohesive brand while rooted in their specific communities.

Big Night’s location in Brooklyn’s Greenpoint

Even Lewin’s three Big Night locations—Brooklyn, West Village, and the Upper East Side, which she calls “sisters, not twins”—each have distinct personalities while maintaining the brand’s joyful, color-saturated identity. Lewin likens the Upper East Side shop to a Classic Six apartment and notes that the new millwork was designed to reflect an upscale, elevated home. Her cash desk placement in the West Village location, situated in the center of the largest room rather than tucked away, came from understanding that specific space and how her team could best interact with customers there.

The thread connecting these diverse approaches is a fundamental understanding that physical retail can no longer compete with e-commerce on convenience or selection. But the best retail spaces offer atmosphere, discovery, community, and the experience of being somewhere that feels genuinely special. As Aka puts it, “Like a good movie, when it ends...if you don’t have the feeling you want to see it again, it was not that good.”

The back room of Rubirosa’s is painted in a pale baby blue, “like a boy’s little bedroom,” says Aka. “So when you take out your credit card, it doesn’t bother you. It’s like you’re on a pillow.”

A leather, steel, and palmwood sofa by Pacific Green at Catherine Bloom for Nordstrom, designed by AD100 talent Brigette Romanek

Visit the AD PRO Video Library for more business insights and behind-the-design access. Explore Videos

#4870. Everyone wants an electric pickup truck. I want more electric 125cc motorcycles

If you follow the EV industry in North America, you’d be forgiven for thinking that the future of transportation revolves around electric SUVs and pickups. Every week seems to bring another teaser from a startup promising to reinvent the family car or truck. The latest electric SUVs, “coming soon” tiny trucks, lifestyle trucks, modular trucks, etc. Companies like Slate, TELO, and countless others have generated plenty of excitement, even if many of them still have everything left to prove.

Don’t get me wrong – I hope they succeed. We need more affordable electric vehicles. But after spending nearly two decades covering a specific part of the electric-vehicle space and a good chunk of that time traveling around the world to see how people actually get around, I can’t help but wonder if we’re chasing the wrong prize here.

Because while the West keeps trying to electrify pickup trucks and SUVs, much of the world has already settled on a far more sensible transportation appliance.

Western readers often think of a sedan as the world’s “normal” or “sensible” vehicle.

But go traveling almost anywhere in Asia, and that illusion disappears in about five minutes.

The roads are filled with Honda Clicks and CG125s, Hero Splendors, Yamaha NMAXs and YBR125s, and countless other small-displacement motorcycles. They carry workers to jobs, students to school, families to dinner, and every imaginable cargo in between.

They’re the delivery vans. They’re the commuter cars. They’re grocery getters, family transportation, and work trucks. And perhaps more importantly, they transport hundreds of millions of people every single day.

You don’t have to spend long in places like the Philippines before realizing something else: these aren’t really “motorcycle cultures.” They’re just transportation cultures. People ride these machines because they make sense.

And I’ve done it myself to see what all the fuss was about. Sure, I’m an e-motorcycle guy through and through. But I’ve been there to ride a mile in their motorcycle sandals, and so I get it.

Even so, having had one cheek on each saddle, I still have to think there’s a better way than the tried-and-true 125cc combustion engine motorcycles that have become the workhorse of much of the world.

The extreme proliferation of small motorcycles is what makes the current electric motorcycle market feel so strange. If there were ever a class of gasoline vehicle that batteries are already capable of replacing, it’s the 125cc combustion engine motorcycle.

These bikes aren’t demanding. They generally produce a modest 10-15 hp (8–11 kW) of power. They cruise comfortably around 45-60 mph (70-100 km/h). Most riders travel relatively short daily distances measured in dozens of miles, not hundreds.

A modern electric motorcycle with a 4-6 kWh battery can comfortably accomplish those same tasks while offering smoother acceleration, quieter operation, lower maintenance, and dramatically lower operating costs.

Unlike replacing a heavy-duty pickup truck that tows livestock trailers across Montana, replacing a 125cc commuter bike is already well within today’s battery technology. In many ways, it’s one of the easiest transportation sectors to electrify.

Instead, North America’s electric motorcycle market developed in an odd direction.

We have electric bicycles, and those are great for urban commuters. But then we jump almost straight to premium electric motorcycles costing $15,000 to $25,000. The practical middle has remained surprisingly sparse.

The ONYX RCR became something of a cult favorite by proving there was genuine demand for lightweight, motorcycle-like electric transportation without superbike performance.

Ryvid’s Anthem pushed further, adding highway capability while still remaining approachable and relatively affordable.

The new LiveWire S4 Honcho also feels like an encouraging step toward a lighter, more practical electric motorcycle instead of another oversized premium machine.

Even Infinite Machine’s Olto, while occupying a slightly different category, demonstrates growing interest in practical urban electric two-wheelers that prioritize transportation over headline horsepower.

These companies are proving something important: People don’t necessarily need 100 horsepower. Many simply need transportation.

Don’t misunderstand me. Electric pickup trucks absolutely have their place. Electric SUVs do too.

But we’ve spent the last decade pouring enormous engineering effort into electrifying some of the least energy-efficient passenger vehicles on the road.

Meanwhile, we’ve largely ignored one of the most efficient gasoline vehicle classes humanity has ever created.

A typical 125cc combustion engine motorcycle can already achieve fuel economy approaching or exceeding 100 mpg (2.35 liters/100 km). An electric equivalent becomes almost absurdly efficient.

We’re talking roughly 4-6 kWh per 100 kilometers (62 miles) – just a fraction of the energy consumed even by many compact electric cars. I once did the math, and a light electric motorcycle can travel 10,000 km (roughly 6,000 miles) for around $160 in electricity. Basically, your electrical bill doesn’t even notice when you own an electric motorcycle. Charging it simply doesn’t move the needle.

Instead of asking how we can fit ever-larger battery packs into increasingly larger SUVs and trucks, perhaps we should also be asking how many trips don’t need a large vehicle in the first place.

Transportation statistics across much of the developed world tell a familiar story.

Most trips involve one person. Maybe there’s also a backpack in the mix. Maybe there are some groceries getting picked up on the way home. Occasionally, there’s another passenger.

Those things are exactly what small motorcycles have been doing around the world for decades.

I recently spent some time in the Philippines, where I watched motorcycles carrying impossible-looking loads every day. Entire families would ride together. Construction materials. Furniture. Livestock. I saw two pigs on a 125cc bike, I kid you not. I’ve seen even crazier things in Thailand.

Again, I’m certainly not advocating that we adopt some of those practices in the West – there are obvious safety considerations – but it demonstrates just how incredibly capable these machines can be.

Even within normal Western expectations, a 125cc-class motorcycle is capable of handling an astonishing percentage of daily transportation needs.

This is where discussions about motorcycles often become unnecessarily polarized.

No, motorcycles aren’t ideal in every situation. Yes, heavy rain exists. Snow exists. Families with multiple children exist. Long interstate road trips exist.

Nobody is suggesting a 125cc electric motorcycle should replace every car.

If a household can replace even half of its daily driving with a lightweight electric motorcycle, that’s already a massive reduction in energy consumption, congestion, parking demand, and transportation costs.

Transportation doesn’t have to be all-or-nothing. So enough with this “but what if it rains?” stuff. Either put on a jacket that day or drive your car. But for the majority of the trips each year you take where it’s not raining, you could have done it in a much more efficient (and fun) way.

One of the biggest conversations surrounding EV adoption is charging. But smaller electric motorcycles barely participate in that problem. A 4-5 kWh battery charges overnight from an ordinary household outlet. The outlet you plug your laptop charger into is basically the equivalent of a Level 2 public charger for an e-motorcycle – that’s how fast it is.

DC fast charging isn’t really necessary on a light electric motorcycle. There’s no need for an expensive home charger. There’s no massive battery pack. Just plug it in before bed and wake up with a battery that’s been fully charged and waiting on you for hours already.

And if you want to go wild, there are swappable battery networks like Gogoro in Taiwan or Vammo in South America that can make charging unnecessary.

Another advantage rarely discussed is intimidation. Many people don’t want a 600 lb (270 kg) motorcycle producing superbike acceleration. They want something approachable, something lightweight, maneuverable, easy to park, and easy to live with.

That’s exactly why 125cc motorcycles became so successful globally.

Electric versions preserve all of those advantages while making them even easier to ride thanks to instant torque and the absence of clutch work. Many of them even come with reverse mode, making them easier to park and wiggle around even on hilly terrain.

One reason Europe has seen more progress here is that the regulatory framework already exists.

Manufacturers regularly market bikes as “125cc equivalents,” and consumers immediately understand what that means.

Licensing systems and insurance companies recognize the category. It’s a mature transportation class.

North America, by comparison, often treats motorcycles as either recreational toys or large-displacement touring machines. We’ve largely forgotten that much of the world simply sees them as transportation.

Ironically, younger riders may already be showing us the way forward.

The explosion in popularity of Sur Ron-style electric motorcycles and lightweight e-motos demonstrates that younger generations are perfectly willing to embrace two-wheeled electric transportation.

Not everyone buying those bikes is looking for motocross. Many simply enjoy the freedom, accessibility, affordability, and fun that come with traveling on two wheels.

Rather than dismissing that trend, perhaps we should recognize it as an opportunity. Today’s recreational rider often becomes tomorrow’s commuter.

If someone discovers they enjoy getting around on an electric two-wheeler, moving from a lightweight e-moto to a practical 125cc-equivalent commuter becomes a much smaller leap than convincing someone to abandon their car entirely.

One observation has stuck with me after so many years of covering the electric vehicle industry and traveling through countries where motorcycles dominate daily life. The West has spent years trying to electrify the largest, heaviest, and least efficient vehicles on our roads. Meanwhile, we’ve paid surprisingly little attention to electrifying the vehicle that already moves much of humanity every single day.

In Europe at least, there's a cliff between a pedal ebike, which anyone can ride without any pre planning, and a motorcycle which needs passing a specific test, taxing, taking out insurance and wearing safety gear. Even people with car licences need to take driving lessons and the test. It means you can't just step on a motorcycle when it makes sense you need potentially months of planning. It's much easier just to take a car you're already licensed for.

I’m not arguing that everyone should ride a motorcycle. And I’m certainly not arguing that cars no longer have an important place.

But perhaps we’ve underestimated just how many people could benefit from something in between.

Not just an electric bicycle, and not just a $25,000 electric motorcycle. Just an honest, practical, affordable electric equivalent of the vehicle that already keeps much of the world moving.

Because if we’re serious about accelerating electric transportation, maybe the future isn’t another electric pickup truck. Maybe it’s finally giving the world’s favorite motorcycle an electric motor.

FTC: We use income earning auto affiliate links. More.

The e-bikes that make up Micah’s current daily drivers are the $999 Lectric XP 2.0, the $1,095 Ride1Up Roadster V2, the $1,199 Rad Power Bikes RadMission, and the $3,299 Priority Current. But it’s a pretty evolving list these days.
