#4869. The Customer Loyalty Secret? It’s Not Just About Shopping

Imagine you’re at home one morning—settled in, slippers on, cozy. Your kids are playing in the next room. A couple of miles away, your local Pottery Barn is hosting a bed-styling workshop: You’ll learn how to layer coverlets and quilts, mix patterns, and plan for seasonality. And maybe at the end of it, you’ll get a small discount for an in-store purchase.

Do you go? Or, do you hop onto YouTube or TikTok and search for “bed-styling ideas”—slippers still on? Maybe you find what you’re looking for on Pottery Barn’s own YouTube channel. Maybe you search for some quilts like those you saw in the video, and—what’s this? A similar-looking option for less, from another brand that isn’t Pottery Barn?

Getting people out of their slippers and into the store—again and again—is one challenge that pros like Evan Hutton, McGee & Co.’s head of retail store operations and a veteran of the industry, are taking seriously. “It used to be you could just do a bed-styling class or [a class on] how to decorate a mantel, and people would come for that,” she says. No longer: Thanks to YouTube tutorials, the lure has to be much more compelling. The promise of social and experiential elements like gelato carts or floral-arranging workshops give customers a social and sensory reason to show up, she says. This strategy for cultivating customer loyalty is already making its way into the first McGee & Co. flagship in Salt Lake City, which opened in September.

Retails are seeing a growing interest for hands-on events, like this keychain-making activity at McGee & Co.

Shea McGee's finished keychain, complete with a monogram.

“I think of in-store programming as retail theater,” says Geri Moran, vice president of marketing at ABC Carpet & Home, a longtime New York City home shopping mecca. The beauty of programming, she notes, is that it brings together “commerce, culture, and discovery.” Importantly, it doesn’t even need to produce a sale right away, either. “It can build awareness, strengthen relationships with the design community, introduce new customers to the store, and give existing customers another reason to return,” she says.

During the holiday season, ABC Carpet & Home hosts carolers at its NYC flagship as a way to “give [customers] a reason to visit, connect, and come back,” says Moran.

She points to a caroling event held at ABC during the holiday season. Those fa-la-las aren’t frivolous: They’re how ABC makes an emotional imprint on its community, even if it may have very little to do with a specific product. “The merchandise gives people a reason to shop,” she says, “but the experience gives them a reason to visit, connect, and come back.”

Another way to get people coming back? Introduce elements that can only be discovered in person, whether that’s through store design, specialty goods, or locally made wares. Across the industry, brands are tackling this in a few different ways. McGee & Co., for one, is planning to include regional variations on their signature design language across its outposts as they expand across the US. Syd McGee, the company’s CEO, imagines a McGee fan encountering the store in different cities, each location informed by local culture. How does McGee do Texas versus California? There’s only one way to find out, he says.

The business is also embracing the potential of merchandise that can only be purchased in-store. At the opening of their Salt Lake City flagship, McGee & Co. offered gardening gear, Cire Trudon candles, Match pewter, and vintage pieces only shoppable in person. Though customers may have initially come to the flagship to check out a new piece of furniture, they’ll discover that their local store can also be a resource for gifts and one-off finds from the McGee universe.

The Lawson-Fenning showroom creates a welcoming environment for clients.

Lawson-Fenning, a homeware brand that has built a reputation for California-inflected design, takes a similar approach to layering, blending their own collection with vintage they’ve found and work from makers they admire. “There’s always something they didn’t come in for,” says cofounder Grant Fenning, adding that “plenty of our longtime clients first walked in without a plan.” While much of their consumer base encounters the brand for the first time through a designer, shoppers will often return on their own a year or two later.

A retailer’s job isn’t just to showcase hero products—it’s to build a world. Which might be why Il Buco Vita, a lifestyle brand offshoot of Il Buco’s hospitality business, does around 60 percent of its business in person, despite having a website full of products. Founder Donna Lennard notes that art, antiques, and decorative pieces frequently sell more in store, while online sales skew toward more everyday kitchen and dining products. “It all comes down to keeping the discovery process interesting,” says Lennard. “With each person that walks through the door, there is an opportunity to introduce them to something they might not have known they were looking for,” she says.

The associates working the showroom floor help complete the picture, educating customers about the artisans, product pairings, and long-term care of pieces. But this year, Il Buco Vita is taking their world-building efforts one step further by opening a new retail flagship on Bond Street, just a few doors down from their original restaurant. This proximity, Lennard says, “creates a stronger connection between hospitality and home,” and provides a setting to present larger pieces, antiques, and collections in a more experiential atmosphere. “Customers can see how they live together and imagine them in their own homes.”

Every issue, every era. Explore 100+ years of the Architectural Digest Archive, an unparalleled digital resource for research and inspiration. Start Searching

Across the board, the brands interviewed for this story identified trust as the key ingredient in creating repeat customers. Cultivating it begins from the moment someone begins engaging with your team—and it doesn’t end when the credit card swipes. “The sale isn’t truly complete when the customer pays,” Moran says. “It’s complete when the product has arrived, the customer is happy with it, and the experience makes them want to return.”

Associates’ product knowledge is the first half of the equation. Customers depend on sales staff to understand not just a product, but the whole-room experience that brands can deliver. This expertise also supports conversations on pricing. In the showroom, customers can test-drive a sofa, run their fingers across different types of upholstery, and scrutinize construction alongside an associate, who can help them unpack the differences between two similar models. “The conversation then becomes less about price and more about value, craftsmanship, longevity, and whether the product is right for the customer,” says Moran. “Our team can also help identify alternatives when something isn’t the right fit for a customer’s budget.”

Hutton notes that for McGee & Co., there was a deliberate decision made to refer to in-store employees as “design associates.” “When a customer comes in, we don’t want it to be, ‘Oh, let me take you over to the design concierge,’” says Hutton. “We want all our team members to be able to start those conversations—and oftentimes finish them too.”

Shea McGee, who briefed many of those first-time flagship associates herself, notes that education on the product forms a significant part of their training. The goal is for trainees to understand showroom furniture from the perspective of a designer, and to be able to translate that to the customer. By doing so, she says, associates will help buyers “make that connection between ‘designer led’ and our brand.”

Studio McGee's senior-level designers host regular check-ins with McGee & Co.’s design associates to help bridge the brand's designer-led vision.

For trade-focused businesses, trust-building exists outside of a single transaction. Tyla Caccese, the founder of the 15-year-old trade-only rug company Tailor-Made Textiles, emphasizes a practical tack. For her new 2,000-square-foot showroom in Manhattan, “We wanted it to feel more like a workshop,” she says. The design followed: There’s a big marble kitchen island where designers can spread out drawings, plug in their laptops, or work through a project over lunch.

This thinking was informed by her experiences in a shared space her brand occupied previously, where she noticed remote-working designers using the showroom as a kind of third place—not quite a home, not quite an office. “It’s been wonderful to see designers using the showroom almost as an extension of their own studios,” she says. These current and prospective customers can get to know and trust the brand through proximity and natural conversation, turning to Caccese to work through ideas and feel at home, whether or not they’re there to source a rug. “I don’t want designers to feel like they need a project or a purchase order to come through the door,” she says. Showing that you can help them solve their problems is what will earn you their business. “Ultimately, what brings designers back isn’t necessarily a particular rug. It’s knowing they can walk in with an idea, a problem, or a completely unrealistic deadline and have a real conversation about how to make something work.”

Once a customer makes a purchase, overcommunicate, overcommunicate, overcommunicate, says Lawson-Fenning co-founder Glenn Lawson. He says that wait times are the biggest challenge facing their business, which makes all their furniture to order. A piece can take 10 or 12 weeks to produce—and for a designer working toward an install date, that matters. “We’d rather give a realistic date and hit it than promise something faster and miss,” says Lawson. “When people know where their piece is, the wait feels shorter.” And in a pinch, those only-in-store items can help too: Never underestimate the power of a floor sample.

At the new ABC Carpet & Home store in Boca Raton, Florida, customers can explore the brand's floor coverings in a range of settings, from residential vignettes to more gallery-like arrangements.

Ultimately, retailers need to recognize that in 2026 and beyond, you’re no longer just a merchant—you’re a builder of relationships. That kinship builds through events that cultivate brand affinity, solving customers’ problems together, and delivering compelling, can’t-find-it-elsewhere in-store discovery. But nurturing that connection is just as important as making it in the first place.

“It’s not uncommon for our top buyers to receive a personal email from our sales team, letting them know about what we have coming up next and personally inviting them to check out new collections,” says Lennard. Fenning seconds the motion: “Designers hear about new work first,” he says. “We invite them in for a new collection or a maker’s show. And when a project wraps, we want to see the photos.” The transaction might end at the register—but the relationship doesn’t have to.

#4867. New ID. AURA is the mainstream EV VW needs – but its software is the real story

Volkswagen built its global empire on the promise of affordable, accessible transportation, embodied by the iconic Type 1 Beetle. Now facing an existential crisis after a decades-long push upmarket, the automaker faces a reckoning – and a retreat back into affordable, mass-market electric sedans like the new FAW-VW ID. AURA L5 (and the software that powers it) might be exactly what VW needs to reach a new generation of buyers.

Like the Ford Model T, the original VW Type 1 Beetle was an engineering-led answer to a basic problem: making family transportation affordable and accessible to the masses. And regardless of how you feel about Elon Musk, the Tesla Model 3 has become the spiritual successor to those cars, bringing electric transportation to millions of mainstream drivers. As the Model 3 approaches its 11th year in public view, the recently revealed all-electric ID. AURA L5 and VW JETTA looks like it could be a compelling rival to the mainstream M3.

Co-developed by Volkswagen’s Chinese and German design teams and set to be built by the FAW-VW JV, the ID. AURA L5 is an Accord/Camry-sized electric family car a practical fastback-style rear hatch, frameless doors, and a claimed “84.4% usable floor-area ratio.” The power-operated hatchback opens its maw more than 70 degrees, revealing a 39″ wide opening that can handle groceries, luggage, golf clubs, pets, bodies – whatever you got.

Don’t take my word for it, though. This is the original text from the company’s press release (translated from Chinese by Google):

The ID. AURA L5 is a pure electric family sedan, jointly designed by Chinese and German design teams, and highly replicates the design essence of the ID. AURA concept car shown at the 2025 Shanghai Auto Show. Its short overhang and long wheelbase proportions create a sleek and flowing coupe-like stance, achieving a “usable floor area ratio” of 84.4%. The sides feature true frameless doors, semi-concealed door handles, and an electric hatchback tailgate with an opening angle exceeding 70°, and an opening width exceeding 1000 mm, greatly improving the convenience of loading and unloading large items, deeply integrating fastback aesthetics with practicality. Some models will be equipped with LiDAR and feature the Hyper Sense 8 end-to-end large model, making daily travel easier and more convenient for customers.

The production version of the ID. AURA L5 is expected to pack the same 155 kW (~208 hp) electric motors announced when the concept AURA L5 was first shown last year (battery specs were not released), and was shown as part of a broader product announcement of what VW Group China would be showcasing at this year’s China International Import Expo (CIIE). These include the first two models fully equipped with Volkswagen Group’s locally developed VEGA “integrated technology matrix” (read: software), and the Volkswagen Anhui intelligent pure electric “coupe” crossover.

The announcement coincides with reports from outlets like Autocar and Reuters that Volkswagen is evaluating whether to deploy its China-developed electrical architecture across the majority of its European markets.

“According to internal discussions confirmed by Group board member and Volkswagen brand CEO Thomas Schäfer,” reports Autocar’s Greg Kable, “up to 75% of the firm’s core models in Europe could eventually use the China Electronic Architecture.”

The Volkswagen Group has been struggling to deliver a software experience that resonates with customers. To their credit, they’re looking for outside help, spending billions of dollars on a software collaboration with EV startup Rivian for its upcoming electric truck brand Scout rather than going it alone. With billions of dollars and potential future of the company at stake, then, this seems like the most interesting bit of the announcement:

During the event, the immersive interactive experience exhibit “Digital Magic Cube,” which made its debut at last year’s China International Import Expo, will undergo an iterative upgrade to showcase the core strength of CEA (China Electronic Architecture), the software pillar of Volkswagen Group’s integrated self-developed technology matrix VEGA, to the attendees.

To date, CEA has enabled five new models from three joint ventures within the Volkswagen Group on a large scale, and software iterations have already been completed this year. CEA brings three core advantages to the Group’s new model lineup in China: intelligent accessibility, continuous upgrades, and safety redundancy. Through immersive interactive presentations, it provides consumers with the most intuitive experience. Empowered by the new AI R&D toolchain “C-Brain,” CEA introduces AI agents into software development, design, and engineering verification, enabling more flexible and efficient integration and innovation of vehicle intelligence capabilities, giving vehicles powerful thinking and perception abilities. Intelligent vehicles powered by CEA can better understand consumer needs, more efficiently create in-vehicle intelligent application scenarios, and better enhance digital intelligent safety redundancy, bringing Chinese consumers a new generation of intelligent mobility experience defined by Volkswagen standards

Sensible looking average car which should appeal to VW buyers provided the price, charging speed and range are right. Especially like that it has a hatch without being SUV shaped.

The show runs through November 10th, and we’ll be keeping an eye out for more details on the ID. AURA lineup and the Anhui intelligent pure electric coupe, including its powertrain, battery, and software specifications, as they become available.

#4863. French carmaker Renault wants to get into the military robot business

Forget what your Fox News-addicted uncle told you about cheese-eating surrender monkeys. The French military has centuries of storied military victories and traditions behind it, and Renault wants in on that action with a new, autonomous electric vehicle platform designed to tackle the Armée de Terre’s most demanding missions.

Co-developed by the EV experts at Renault Group and the defense experts at Belgian electronics firm John Cockerill, the new Forefront concept previews a new, modular ground vehicle that can be rapidly industrialized at a controlled cost, with unique mobility and off-road performance, and capable of autonomously navigating complex terrain. The result is a machine that could front-line logistical capabilities for both defense and civilian applications, enabling missions to be carried out remotely in very high-risk and hard‑to‑reach environments.

“Forefront Concept … demonstrates the contribution that Renault Group’s industrial and technological expertise can make to a strategic sector such as unmanned ground vehicles with multiple applications expected across both civilian and military sectors in the years to come,” explains François Provost, CEO, Renault Group. “This innovative project brings together two complementary areas of expertise: Renault Group’s in the rapid, large-scale and cost-effective development and production of high-performance, reliable technological products and John Cockerill’s in the defense industry.”

Renault is quick to point out that its new concept isn’t just suitable for military work. Although it’s worth pointing out that the Renault-provided image, above, isn’t just AI slop: it would be impractical if it was real, with no real obvious way to get those RCP pipes on or off the thing. That said, presenting their military killbot as a construction mule is at least more credible than the “ambulance” and “troop transport” use cases we’ve seen touted by similar concepts on the American side.

Call me cynical but military contracts are the best and last refuge for a company that cannot compete in the open market. With no / limited international competition, a famously slow procurement process that favors "proven" (read 3+ generation old) technology, and equipment that is rarely used in a competitive environment and whose failures are cloaked behind national security classifications, it is the perfect place to hide. (C.f. Oshkosh and AM General)

Forefront seems to be little more than a rendering at this stage, with the first of the Forefront concept prototypes set to take part in a number of exercises and trials in the months ahead to further refine the concept.

#4861. 10 Best Leggings on Amazon, Tested By Editors and a Fabric Expert

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

Oh, leggings, how we love thee: They’re perfect for Pilates, brunch, and everywhere in between. Here at Women’s Health, our editors practically live in leggings—whether we’re testing new athleisure brands or revisiting our tried-and-true favorites, we always stay on top of what's new.

While we love premium activewear brands like lululemon, Athleta, Alo Yoga, and Girlfriend Collective, our editors all agree that one of our favorite destinations for cute, affordable leggings is Amazon. To help you find the perfect pair, we did the dirty work and tested a bunch of the best-selling leggings on the everything retailer—from leggings with pockets to compression styles.

Our editors test tons of leggings on Amazon, but this pair from Crz Yoga consistently earns the highest marks across the board. And when it comes to affordable activewear that actually competes with high-end labels, Crz Yoga is one of the first brands I recommend to friends and family. The Butterluxe Legging has a smooth, stretchy feel and light compression that performs similarly to more premium brands like Lululemon, Alo, and Vuori.

The Butterluxe fabric—an 81 percent nylon and 19 percent elastane blend—has that smooth, second-skin softness. It’s ultra-stretchy without feeling flimsy, offers light-to-moderate compression, and features a high-rise waistband that stays put through squats, lunges, and long walks. Bonus: a hidden waistband pocket makes it easy to stash small essentials on the go. As a runner myself, I’ve found them perfect for carrying keys during jogs—they always stay in place.

Our only caveat? This legging comes in a single length, which may bunch slightly at the ankles on petite frames, and while the pockets are handy, they’re not the most secure option for larger phones during high-impact workouts.

Travel days are uncomfortable enough without leggings digging into your skin. That’s why contributor Hayley Helms swears by this pair from Baleaf. “If you get bloated on airplanes like I do, these are a godsend,” she says. The wide waistband panel offers light tummy support without feeling restrictive, making them ideal for long flights, train rides, or road trips.

The nylon-spandex fabric is soft, lightweight, and breathable. It feels like you're wearing nothing at all, yet still stays in place on even the busiest travel days. While they’re budget-friendly, these aren’t the most durable leggings we’ve tested. If you’re looking for a pair for intense workouts, we’d steer you elsewhere. But for staying cool, comfy, and sweat-free on travel days, Helms can’t recommend these enough.

There’s a lot to love about these leggings, but I’m a huge fan of the high, compressive waistband. It hits just above the belly button and applies just enough compression to keep my core feeling locked in without feeling too tight or falling down mid-workout. Plus, the compression holds up through everything from weightlifting to yoga, and the high-waisted fit is both flattering and comfortable. You get full range of motion without that annoying stomach pinch.

I also love the smooth, flat seams, which help prevent chafing, and the contoured stitching on the back that helps flatter the bum. Helms wears these weekly and swears by the cooling fabric. The quick-drying material keeps sweat from lingering, so if you’re heading to the store post-gym, you won’t feel gross in the checkout line.

While we wish this legging came in more colors, the true-to-size, flattering, and comfortable fit still makes this pair one of our faves.

Nothing ruins a good booty workout like worrying your underwear is on full display (IYKYK). Thankfully, this legging passed our squat tests with flying colors. The material is totally opaque, offers full coverage, and has tons of stretch that makes getting low feel effortless.

We also love that the brand offers a range of designs, from pocketed options to classic, streamlined pairs. It’s available in three lengths, so there’s a pair for every height. But no matter the length, you’re getting the same flattering fit: high-waisted, super slim, and contouring in all the right places—supportive without ever feeling too tight.

If you’re on a budget, it doesn’t get better than this three-pack of leggings for under $30 (yep, really). The best part? The low price doesn’t mean you’re sacrificing quality. These are actually some of Amazon’s top-rated leggings, and for good reason.

Editors who tested this pocketed trio say they’re soft yet supportive, holding up well through deep squats and mobility work. They also offer standout tummy control, making them a solid pick if you’re after a slimming fit.

Another bonus: there are no front or inner thigh seams, so you won’t get any extra bulk or uncomfortable rubbing. The only downside? The fabric is a bit on the thinner side, so you’ll want to be careful while washing to avoid pilling.

Unlike other plus-size leggings our editors tested, this legging doesn’t dig in at the waist or roll down mid-workout. Plus, it’s made with flat-lock seams to help prevent chafing and irritation.

While it’s not the most technical pair (cotton isn’t moisture-wicking, and there are no pockets), it earns high marks for comfort, everyday wearability, and price.

Looking for a cropped legging for summer or warmer workouts? I recommend this chafe-proof, squat-proof, and sweat-wicking pair. It’s perfect for hot, humid weather, and the polyester and spandex material is super stretchy with enough compression to keep you feeling locked in without that uncomfortable pinching feeling.

While the seams and waistband lie flat, we did notice that the side pockets stick out slightly and add a bit of width to the legs. That said, the hidden interior pocket at the waistband is a major win for securely stashing small items at the gym. Plus, the waistband features built-in tummy control that holds you in and adds a smooth, toned look while you work out.

Women’s Health executive health and fitness director Jacqueline Andriakos swears by this high-waisted, budget-friendly pair. “I love a high-waisted legging with pockets for my phone and credit card, so I can really go hands-free when I walk and run,” she says.

She wears them for everything: from running and weight-lifting to travel days and lounging at home. The fabric is smooth and substantial, yet still super breathable. “They feel more expensive than they are,” she adds, noting that she owns “way too many” pairs.

During testing, we did notice that the waistband can roll down slightly, and we wish there were more inseam options beyond the standard 25 inches. But for the price, comfort, and versatility, this pair is tough to beat.

Plus, the high-rise fit offers just enough tummy control, so we can confidently wear it with just a high-impact sports bra or a crop top. The V-shaped waist is also super flattering, giving the appearance of a slightly longer torso. That being said, there is a bit of extra fabric around the crotch, which is supposed to give that area more breathing room, but might be too baggy for some wearers.

Women's Health food producer Tina Martinez touts these Hey Nuts leggings for their smooth, light feel and ability to stay in place. She loves that they offer ample support, and that they feel like they're holding you in the right places. Martinez also loves their great compression, the fact that they don't roll down, and how opaque they are (no panty lines here!). They're also super stretchy and comfortable, so you can wear them at home or to the gym.

These leggings have the most inseam options on this list, ranging from 25 inches to 31 inches if you're on the taller side. In terms of sizing, our testers find that these leggings run totally true-to-size, which, if you're an avid Amazon shopper, you know is hard to come by.

We had very few cons about these leggings, but we did agree that the butt seam felt a bit too low, so they're not the most flattering option on this list.

Professor Preeti Arya, PhD, specializes in textile development at the Fashion Institute of Technology. She says that the right fabric for leggings depends on your needs. But if you're worried about maintenance, "you can go for something that is petroleum-based because care is easy," she says. Petroleum-based fabrics include spandex, polyester, and nylon. These options are also typically much cheaper, according to Arya, but you can also opt for wool or silk, which sometimes feel better on your skin since they're natural fibers. Heads up: these fabrics are a bit harder to care for, though, and require special attention on laundry day.

Rise is really up to your personal preference, but higher-rise leggings usually have tummy control, which is a plus when you want to feel snatched at the gym. However, tummy control typically comes with a bit more compression, which may not be great for lower-impact activities like yoga or casual walks.

Again, length is up to your personal preference, but the most flattering cut is typically just above your ankle. For most people, that means a 7/8 style of leggings. Full-length leggings, or ones that extend all the way to your feet, might be better for barefoot workouts like yoga. Other lengths, like capri leggings, are really up to your personal style.

To find the best leggings on Amazon for all body types and activity preferences, the WH team asked a panel of anonymous testers and our in-house editors for feedback on the brands they wear and love. To determine which leggings on Amazon are the best, we looked at factors including material options, the fit of each pair, price points that suit every budget, and what type of activity each legging is best suited to. This final list includes something for everyone—from the best high-waisted leggings, to the best leggings for workouts.
